## Introduction: A Small Building and a Large Historiography

Few medieval interiors carry as much interpretive weight as the single-naved, barrel-vaulted brick hall that rises among the gardens of the old Roman amphitheatre in Padua. It is a modest building by any architectural measure. The nave is usually given as 20.88 metres long, 8.41 metres wide and 12.65 metres high, and it ends in a shallow chancel made up of a square bay about 4.49 metres deep and a five-sided polygonal apse about 2.57 metres deep. Into this space [Giotto di Bondone](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Giotto-di-Bondone.html) and his workshop painted one of the most coherent decorative programmes to survive from medieval Europe. The painted surface is commonly estimated at roughly seven hundred square metres, nearly a quarter of it the blue vault. The chapel has been called the founding monument of modern European painting, a theological summa in images, and a usurer's plea for salvation. Each of these descriptions holds part of the truth and each has distorted the rest.

The account that follows sets the building and its paintings against the documentary record, the earliest literary testimony and the material evidence brought to light by modern conservation. It distinguishes throughout between what is documented, what is probable and what is conjectural. It also corrects several errors that recur in general and even specialist literature: that the chapel was built purely to expiate the usury of Enrico Scrovegni<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-scrovegni-enrico" class="footnote"><sup>1</sup></a>'s father; that Dante<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-dante-alighieri" class="footnote"><sup>2</sup></a> names that father in the *Inferno*; that the chapel was consecrated in 1303 or 1304 rather than in 1305; and that Enrico's image in the Last Judgment is the first donor portrait of its kind.

Three modern scholars frame the chapel's historiography. [Roberto Longhi](/scholars.html#longhi-roberto)'s essay "Giotto spazioso" of 1952 redirected attention to Giotto's construction of real space. [Chiara Frugoni](/scholars.html#frugoni-chiara)'s *L'affare migliore di Enrico* of 2008 overturned the expiatory reading of the commission. [Laura Jacobus](/scholars.html#jacobus-laura)'s work, especially her 1999 study of the chapel's Annunciation and her 2008 monograph *Giotto and the Arena Chapel: Art, Architecture and Experience*, has reconstructed the building as a functioning space and argued for Giotto's role as its designer.

## Padua around 1300: Commune, Arena and the Scrovegni Fortune

At the turn of the fourteenth century Padua was a self-governing commune of considerable wealth and ambition. It had dominated Vicenza through the later thirteenth century, and its university already drew jurists, physicians and notaries from across Italy and beyond. The city's intellectual life in these decades is usually described as "pre-humanist", meaning a culture of Latin learning, classicizing historiography and lawyerly rhetoric. That culture produced Albertino Mussato and Lovato Lovati. The commune's public life was also violent. Within two decades the city passed from the faction struggles of the Guelph<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-guelphs" class="footnote"><sup>3</sup></a> oligarchy to the lordship of the Carraresi and the intervention of Cangrande della Scala. Enrico Scrovegni's career followed this curve, from civic eminence to exile.

The site that gave the chapel its popular name was the elliptical Roman amphitheatre, the *Arena*, in the northern part of the city near the convent of the Augustinian Hermits<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-augustinian-order" class="footnote"><sup>4</sup></a>, the Eremitani<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-eremitani" class="footnote"><sup>5</sup></a>. By the late Middle Ages its ruins had become a walled enclosure containing houses, gardens and other buildings. The deed of sale, preserved among the papers of the later owners, describes among the properties a large walled house with an upper floor and a tiled roof, with a stove-heated room or bath close by, standing in the middle of the Arena, with a loggia behind it:

> una domus intus magna murata et solarata coperta cupis cum stupa prope posita in medio ipsius Arene cum loza post ipsam domum.

Other accounts drawn from the same document mention stables and two towers or *dongioni* over the entrances, one facing the Eremitani and the other the river. The phrase matters, because it shows that Enrico did not buy an empty field. He bought an existing seigneurial complex inside a monumental ruin. Recent archaeological and archival research has pressed this point. It has asked how far the chapel was built from scratch and how far it reused or replaced earlier structures, since the substructure, a semi-basement of the same plan as the nave, sits on remains associated with the amphitheatre. Those questions remain formally open, in part because fifteenth-century fires destroyed much of the Paduan archive.

The vendor was Manfredo Dalesmanini<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-manfredo-dalesmanini" class="footnote"><sup>6</sup></a>, head of an old Paduan magnate lineage whose fortunes had been declining for some time. The sale on 6 February 1300, for four thousand lire<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-gold-florin" class="footnote"><sup>7</sup></a> according to the standard biographical dictionary, marks one stage in the transfer of status from an older feudal aristocracy to a family whose wealth came from credit. That family was the Scrovegni. Their history is more complicated than the image of parvenu usurers spread in the early fourteenth century by their enemies, above all the Paduan chronicler Giovanni da Nono in his hostile *De generatione aliquorum civium urbis Padue*.

Archival research has traced the lineage to the twelfth-century *de Saurellis*, landholders east of Padua. Their surname and the heraldic sow (*scrofa*) seem to derive from a property called *peta de Scrufa*. Members of the family lent money to Paduan bishops in the 1220s and 1230s and acted as guarantors of a peace between Padua and Venice in 1235. Enrico's father, Rinaldo (Reginaldo), first appears in 1249 as a witness in Venice, and as a creditor in 1261. He lent both to private persons and to public bodies, among them Gherardo da Camino, lord of Treviso, in 1284, and the commune of Vicenza. His contracts show careful legal drafting. The surplus over the principal was framed not as interest but as a *poena* for failing to repay within an impossibly short term, which in effect produced annual rates of ten to twenty per cent. Giovanni da Nono put his fortune at half a million lire. Rinaldo died between November 1287 and October 1289 and was buried in the cathedral, apparently without objection. This small fact sits awkwardly with the later picture of a damned usurer whose son had to buy back his soul.

Enrico was born in or before 1266 and was the youngest of Rinaldo's three legitimate sons. By the late 1290s, after the deaths of his elder brothers, he was in effect head of the family. He married first a daughter of Bonifacio da Carrara and then, probably by 1310, Iacobina d'Este, daughter of the marquis Francesco. The two marriages trace the arc of his social ambition. He received honorary Venetian citizenship in 1301. In papal correspondence of 1304 he appears as *dilectus filius* and *familiaris noster* of Benedict XI<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-benedict-xi" class="footnote"><sup>8</sup></a>, the former Niccolò Boccasini of Treviso, a relationship probably formed while the future pope was in the Veneto. In 1307 he served on a Paduan embassy to Azzo VIII d'Este, and in January 1311 he was among the Paduan representatives at the coronation of Henry VII in Milan. A biographer of the family has called him "uomo politico, proprietario terriero, prestatore di denaro, benefattore, mecenate". Every one of those roles is documented, and none of them on its own explains the chapel.

## Enrico Scrovegni, Usury and the Commission: The Documentary Record

The documents bearing directly on the chapel's foundation are few, and they need to be read in order and with care. After the purchase of February 1300, the next fixed point is indirect. The Eremitani's complaint of January 1305 refers to an earlier licence from Bishop Ottobono dei Razzi permitting Enrico to build a small church, *ecclesiola*, essentially for the use of his household. The licence does not survive and is undated. It can only be placed between the purchase of 6 February 1300 and 29 April 1302, when Ottobono was translated to the patriarchate of Aquileia. The permission is sometimes dated "6 February 1300 / 1302", but it is documented only by later reference and falls within those two limits. The date of 6 February belongs to the deed of sale, not to the episcopal act.

The first dedication of the building is usually placed on 25 March 1303, the feast of the Annunciation. The evidence is a lost inscription, transmitted in a sixteenth-century document. The date is therefore probable rather than securely documented, but it fits everything else.

On 1 March 1304 Benedict XI granted an indulgence to the faithful who visited the

> ecclesiam beatae Mariae Virginis de Caritate de Arena, civitatis Paduae

in a spirit of humility.

The papal letter shows that the church was usable for worship by that date. It also fixes its title as Santa Maria della Carità, a relatively rare dedication that was probably chosen by the patron. The indulgence, of one year and forty days for penitents visiting on Marian feasts, changed the building in practice from a household oratory into a destination for the Paduan public.

This was precisely the change the Eremitani found intolerable.

Their complaint is dated 9 January 1305 and was presented by the convent's syndic to the new bishop, Pagano della Torre. It states that the friars had complained before. They objected that Enrico, described with heavy irony as a noble and powerful knight and magnificent citizen of Padua, had gone beyond the terms of the original licence. Instead of a small household chapel he had built a large church with several altars, together with a

> novum campanile in Arena … ad ponendas campanas magnas,

to the

> grave scandalum, damnum, praeiuditium et iniuriam

of the friars.

In a phrase quoted again and again since, the complaint also refers to

> alia multa quae ibi facta sunt potius ad pompam et ad vanam gloriam

than to the honour of God.

The document thus offers the earliest hostile description of the building's splendour, and perhaps an oblique reference to its painted decoration. It is a record of a dispute over pastoral territory, bells and offerings. It is not, as is sometimes implied, a record of a court order to cut the building down.

What followed is uncertain, and the uncertainty matters for the history of the building. The outcome of the complaint is not documented. One influential reading, set out in Frugoni's biographical account of the family, holds that the friars obtained only the demolition of a large campanile and a rearrangement of the triumphal arch, after which the building could be consecrated again. Other scholars have doubted that Enrico ever gave up the bell tower. Many more have connected the complaint with the obvious difference between the chapel as built and the model Enrico offers in the Last Judgment, which shows a transept and a more elaborate east end. That connection is plausible, but it remains an inference rather than a fact.

The documents become firm again in the second half of March 1305. On 16 March the Great Council of Venice approved a loan to Enrico *de pannis sancti Marci* for the consecration of his chapel at Padua. The request is sometimes placed in January 1305, but the deliberation usually cited is dated 16 March. What the *panni* were is debated. One suggestion is that they were the precious cloths covering the high altar of San Marco. The phrase does not support the popular claim that they were relics of the Evangelist's garments.

The consecration itself took place nine days later, on 25 March 1305, the feast of the Annunciation. Since a proposal first made in 1974, the occasion has been linked with the motet

> Ave Regina celorum / Mater innocencie / Ite missa est,

which, as the *Dizionario Biografico degli Italiani* (vol. 69, 2007) notes, "reca il nome dell'autore sotto forma di acrostico (Marcum Paduanum)", that is, Marchetto da Padova. A 2007 study in the *Rivista di Analisi e Teoria Musicale* objected that the motet's *Ars nova* writing is "non compatibile con la datazione alta (1305)", so the association is plausible but not certain. The date 25 March 1305 should be kept distinct both from the probable first dedication of 25 March 1303 and from the papal indulgence of March 1304, all of which are sometimes confused in secondary accounts.

The later documents reveal how the patron himself presented the foundation. Enrico installed a provost in 1307 to look after the spiritual needs of his household, his retinue and the inhabitants of the Arena enclosure. In a deed of endowment of 1317 he described the church as built at his own expense "to the honour and benefit of the city and Commune of Padua and for the suffrage of his soul and those of his predecessors".

The formula combines civic munificence with ordinary concern for the dead. It does not read as a confession. From 1306, according to the same line of research, the feast of the Annunciation, with its procession and sacred play, became an annual festival under communal patronage that ended before Santa Maria della Carità. Other scholars stress that a civic procession and Annunciation play at the Arena were already established before the chapel was built. In either case Enrico had placed his building at the end point of the most important civic and Marian ritual in the Paduan calendar. The procession ran under the auspices of the commune rather than the bishop, and the chapel gave it a permanent and highly personal setting.

Enrico's later life ended in exile. During the struggles between the Carraresi and Cangrande his town palace was sacked in 1318. He secured a Venetian licence to bear arms within days of Giacomo da Carrara's election as lord in July 1318, and from about 1320 he lived mainly in Venice. He made a brief and ill-fated return in 1328 and fled again by night. Marsilio da Carrara then confiscated his Paduan estate, except the Arena. In Venice he continued to lend on a large scale, now largely in the form of the *colleganza*, which was regarded as licit. His clients included two future doges, Marino Falier and Marco Corner<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-doge-marco-corner" class="footnote"><sup>9</sup></a>, the latter also his son-in-law.

He dictated his last will at Venice (Murano) on 12 March 1336 and died on 20 August 1336. The will was long unpublished and was edited and translated in an appendix to Frugoni's 2008 book. It contains generous provisions for the church at the Arena and a section on restitution of ill-gotten gains. That section, in Frugoni's reading, is marginal and "more velleitarian than real", since it rested on the recovery of property Enrico no longer controlled. His body was eventually laid in the chapel's choir, where a tomb with a recumbent effigy survives. The effigy must date from after his death.

The documentary record therefore cannot support the tidy story that Enrico built the chapel solely to atone for his father's usury. That story became fixed in the nineteenth century, when one Paduan writer said Enrico had "shielded himself from a tercet of Alighieri with a church by Giotto". It depends heavily on Dante, and the chronology works against it. The relevant canto of the *Inferno* was written well after the consecrations of 1303 and 1305, a point Frugoni stresses. In it Dante does not name Rinaldo at all. In the circle of the violent against art the pilgrim meets a usurer who bears on his white purse "una scrofa azzurra e grossa", an azure and pregnant sow, and who announces that he alone is Paduan among these Florentines, prophesying the arrival of his "vicin Vitaliano".

The identification with Rinaldo Scrovegni comes from the heraldry and from the early commentators. It is secure, but it is not explicit. Frugoni has argued the alternative case most fully. On her reading Enrico used Giotto's paintings to proclaim the good use of riches when spent on works of charity, and to present himself as a pious benefactor and generous patron rather than a penitent sinner. Jacobus also questions the dominance of the atonement narrative. She treats the chapel as a multi-functional social space serving household, civic and devotional purposes, and its frescoes as expressing the interests and insecurities of a new entrepreneurial class. Neither scholar denies that concern for salvation was present. The 1317 deed speaks of suffrage for his soul and his predecessors, and the Last Judgment is anything but neutral about the fate of usurers. What the evidence does not support is a single motive, and least of all a motive pinned to a canto written later.

## The Building: Architecture, Lost Elements and the Question of Giotto as Architect

The chapel as it stands is a simple brick rectangle covered by a barrel vault, lit by six tall lancets in the south wall and a triple-light window in the façade. The north wall has no windows, because it was originally attached to or faced by the Scrovegni palace and its connecting structures. Beneath the nave lies a semi-basement of the same dimensions, entered from outside, decorated in a modest way and traditionally called the "cenobio".

At the east end a pointed triumphal arch opens into a square chancel and a polygonal apse covered by a ribbed vault. A small sacristy opens off the north side. Above it lies a room that was once a loggia linking chapel and palace, later walled up. The exterior is plain today, but it was not plain originally. At least the façade carried mural decoration, removed in 1885 in an intervention later judged harmful, because it exposed the masonry to moisture. A nineteenth-century print suggests that the façade once followed the curve of the amphitheatre beneath it. A portico in front of the entrance collapsed in 1817.

Comparison with the model in Enrico's hands in the Last Judgment has driven much of the discussion of the building's original form. The painted model shows a nave with a transept and a more developed east end. The surviving building has no transept. Whether the model records a design that was built and then demolished, a design that was planned and then abandoned, or an idealized emblem of the foundation has long been debated.

The restoration campaign concluded that the present apse and the small sacristy appear to belong to a phase later than the nave Giotto painted. Several scholars have linked the alteration to the funerary function of the choir, which was to receive Enrico's tomb, and to the possible effects of the Eremitani's protest. Jacobus's reconstruction is the most systematic attempt to model the chapel "at the time of its consecration in 1305". She argues that changes in the design brief between 1300 and 1305 prevented the full realization of the original scheme. In her more recent work on the roof she suggests that the east end was raised when the patron's plans changed, forcing improvised solutions on the builders. These proposals are well argued and widely discussed, but they are reconstructions and should be described as such.

The most disputed question is whether Giotto designed the building. No document says so. The case rests on internal evidence: the exact fit between the architecture and the painted system, the lack of windows on the north wall, which leaves an unbroken narrative field, the placing of the south windows so as to fall within the painted framework, and the way the illusionistic architecture of the frescoes seems to have been conceived together with the real structure. Jacobus has made the strongest form of the argument. Her publisher's summary states it plainly: "Giotto was the architect of the Arena Chapel, architecture and decoration were completely integrated in his design."

In her later formulation the building was designed "from the inside out" to display the frescoes. Others have been more cautious. They point out that the plain hall with a barrel vault was a common type, that the integration of painting and wall could have come from close collaboration between painter and builder, and that Giotto's documented architectural work, the Florentine campanile of 1334, belongs to the end of his career. The fairest position is that Giotto's responsibility for the design is possible and in some versions probable, but undocumented. The building was certainly planned with a painted programme in mind, and that programme was certainly Giotto's.

The building cannot be understood apart from the residence it served. The Scrovegni palace was raised along the curve of the amphitheatre, and the old arena floor became its enclosed garden. A door at the east end of the north wall linked palace and chapel, while the principal entrance in the west façade opened toward the public. The arrangement made the chapel both a household oratory and a public church. Jacobus has interpreted it as a space divided along lines of gender and social status, with different constituencies entering and standing in different places. After the Scrovegni conspiracy of 1439, palace and chapel were confiscated by the Venetian Council of Ten and sold.

They passed through the Capodilista and Cardinal Ludovico Trevisan to the Foscari, and in the early nineteenth century by marriage to the Gradenigo. The palace was demolished in the 1820s. The date most often given is 1827, though 1824 also appears in the literature. Its materials were sold, and two blocks of dwellings were raised in its place. The loss of the palace, which had buttressed and sheltered the north side of the chapel, contributed to structural cracking and water damage. In 1880 the commune of Padua resolved to acquire the chapel, by a council deliberation of 10 May 1880 followed by a notarial act in 1881. It demolished the newer dwellings and began restorations that later generations judged unevenly.

## The Programme: Joachim and Anna, the Life of the Virgin, the Life and Passion of Christ

The narrative cycle unfolds in three registers along the side walls and across the chancel arch. The usual count is thirty-seven or thirty-eight scenes, depending on whether the two halves of the Annunciation on the arch and the panel of the Pact of Judas are counted separately. The story begins in the uppermost register of the south wall at the chancel end and runs westward. It continues in the upper register of the north wall from the entrance eastward, and then moves down register by register, always in the same clockwise direction, until it reaches Pentecost at the east end of the lowest register of the north wall. The Last Judgment fills the entrance wall. A viewer who follows the narrative in sequence moves round the room three times, and turns at the end toward the great image of the end of time above the door through which they will leave.

## The Scrovegni Chapel: The Story of Joachim and Anna

The narrative of the Arena Chapel does not begin with the Gospels. When Giotto set out, around 1303, to cover the walls of Enrico Scrovegni’s chapel with the longest coherent story of his career, he opened it one generation before the evangelists do, with the parents of the Virgin: Joachim, a rich and righteous man of Jerusalem, and his wife Anna, childless after twenty years of marriage. Their story occupies the entire upper register of the south wall — six panels, each roughly 200 by 185 centimetres, read from the chancel towards the entrance — and it has no canonical source. It descends from the apocryphal tradition of the Protevangelium of James and the Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew, transmitted to the medieval West above all through the Legenda aurea of Jacopo da Varazze<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-jacopo-da-varagine" class="footnote"><sup>10</sup></a>, and it tells of a public humiliation, a penitential exile, a double annunciation, and a reunion that theology would come to read as the immaculate conception of the Virgin herself. That a cycle of such ambition should devote a full sixth of its narrative surface to apocryphal matter, and to the sufferings of an elderly couple, was an unusual choice, and it was almost certainly a deliberate one.

The choice has been read, persuasively, through the figure of the patron. Joachim is wealth vindicated: a man of means whose generous offerings are publicly refused by the priesthood, whose disgrace is reversed by God himself, and whose riches are finally shown to have been pleasing to heaven all along. For Enrico Scrovegni, son of a lender whom contemporaries ranked among the most notorious in the Veneto, and himself condemned within months of the chapel’s completion by the neighbouring Eremitani friars for its pomp and vainglory, the story of the rich man rejected by the institution and accepted by God made an eloquent opening statement. Chiara Frugoni, whose reading of the chapel’s iconography has reshaped the study of its patronage, has drawn the parallel explicitly: the Joachim cycle is, among other things, a mirror held up to the donor, and the cycle’s silence about money in any negative function belongs to the same careful design. The viewer need not choose between this civic-apologetic reading and the devotional one; the strength of the programme is that it sustains both at once.

Within these six panels Giotto also laid down the grammar of everything that follows. Here the continuous azurite sky of the chapel first shows its range of meanings — the void into which Joachim is expelled, the heaven that opens over his sacrifice, the corridor of descending angels, the calm blue above the Golden Gate; here the convention of the persistent set is established, Anna’s chamber answering the sheepfold across the register; and here the painter’s deepest innovations of narrative first appear in sequence — the figure folded shut by shame, the dog’s uncalculating welcome, the angel caught half through a window, the soul of an offering ascending as a small human form, the vision granted to one shepherd and withheld from the man beside him, and finally the first great kiss of Western painting, two aged profiles merging within their overlapping haloes. The register runs from threshold to threshold: it opens with a man turned away from the sanctuary of the Temple and closes with the same man received at the gilded gate of the city, and between those two architectures of exclusion and welcome it carries the entire preparation of the Virgin’s coming.

The six scenes — the Rejection of Joachim’s Sacrifice, Joachim among the Shepherds, the Annunciation to St Anne, the Sacrifice of Joachim, the Dream of Joachim, and the Meeting at the Golden Gate — are discussed in the order in which the wall presents them, which is also the order in which Giotto’s workshop, proceeding from the top of the walls downward in the campaign of about 1303 to 1305, brought them into being. They are best read in sequence, as the first visitors read them: as a single story told in six breaths, in which the painter of the Arena Chapel<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-arena-chapel" class="footnote"><sup>11</sup></a> announced, before a single Gospel scene had begun, that sacred history would henceforth take place among observable human beings, under a continuous sky.

### The Rejection of Joachim’s Sacrifice (Scenes from the Life of Joachim, 1)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-01.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Against a deep blue ground, a white marble sanctuary enclosure occupies the left four fifths of the scene, with a canopied ciborium on twisted columns over the altar and a raised pulpit with stairs behind. Inside the enclosure a priest in a red robe leans towards a kneeling youth whose head alone is visible above the parapet. At the right, a second priest in green, seen from behind, pushes away the elderly, haloed Joachim, who clutches a small lamb in his draped arms and steps towards the edge of the platform and the empty blue beyond">
  <figcaption>The Rejection of Joachim’s Sacrifice, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), upper register of the south wall. The opening scene of the narrative cycle: the childless Joachim, bearing a lamb for sacrifice, is turned away from the Temple of Jerusalem by the priest, while the offering of another worshipper is accepted within the sanctuary enclosure.</figcaption>
</figure>

The story told on the walls of the Arena Chapel begins not with grace but with humiliation. In the first panel of the upper register of the south wall, at the chancel end, Giotto opens the cycle with an episode drawn not from the canonical Gospels but from the apocryphal tradition. When Joachim brings his offering to the Temple, the priest refuses it, for barrenness was read as a mark of divine disfavour, and a man without issue had no standing among the offerings of Israel. From this rejection the entire economy of the cycle unfolds: Joachim’s retreat among the shepherds, the angelic annunciations to husband and wife, the meeting at the Golden Gate, and the birth of the Virgin who will undo the shame of her parents. That Giotto, or the learned adviser behind the programme, chose to begin here — with an elderly just man publicly expelled from a holy place — gives the opening of the cycle a severity that the viewer entering the chapel cannot mistake.

The setting is a marvel of economical stagecraft. Giotto does not attempt the Temple as a building seen whole; he gives instead its liturgical heart, a raised sanctuary enclosed by a low parapet of white marble whose panels are veined with swirling pink and grey, rendered with the same delight in fictive stone that governs the dado of the chapel itself. Within the precinct, at the left, rises a ciborium over the altar: a gabled canopy carried on four slender twisted columns with Corinthianesque capitals, their Solomonic spirals an evident allusion to the columns believed to have come from the Temple of Jerusalem itself. To the right, behind the enclosure, a staircase with an open balustrade ascends to a raised pulpit or ambo on colonnettes, its parapet ornamented with incised rinceaux and rosettes and crowned at the corners by lathe-turned finials. The whole structure is set obliquely, its angled parapet projecting towards the picture plane at the lower right, so that the sacred precinct reads as a solid, habitable volume thrust against the blue — one of the earliest and clearest statements of the spatial intelligence that governs the entire cycle. The architecture is scaled to the figures’ actions rather than to archaeological plausibility: it is a stage, and everything upon it is legible.

Within this stage Giotto sets two actions in deliberate counterpoint. At the left, inside the enclosure, the high priest — a grave, grey-bearded figure in a red mantle over a gold-bordered vestment, his head covered by the pointed cap that marks his office — bends towards a young man kneeling within the sanctuary, of whom only the head and a raised profile are visible above the parapet. His open hand hovers in a gesture of acceptance or blessing: this worshipper’s offering is received. At the right, outside the gate of the precinct, the same sacerdotal authority shows its other face. A second priest, wrapped in a green mantle shot with a broad gold-embroidered border, is seen almost entirely from behind — one of those turned figures that Giotto uses throughout the chapel to fold the viewer into the depicted space. With his left hand he grips Joachim’s shoulder; his right arm, extended across the old man’s back, presses him firmly away. The gesture is neither violent nor merely ceremonial. It is the measured, implacable push of an institution, and its restraint makes it crueller than any blow.

Joachim himself absorbs the rejection with a pathos that has few precedents in Italian painting. He is an old man of patriarchal dignity, grey-bearded, wrapped in a rose-pink mantle whose heavy folds Giotto models with the full weight of his new plastic style. In the crook of his left arm, half-swaddled in the drapery, he carries the small white lamb destined for the sacrifice — now a useless gift, pressed against his body like the child he does not have. The animal’s quiet presence is the emotional key of the scene: it transforms the rejected offering into an image of thwarted paternity. Joachim’s bearded face, turned back towards the priest even as his body is propelled forward, wears an expression of bewildered grief; and around his head, alone among the figures, shines a gold halo, worked in relief and applied *a secco*. The man whom the Temple expels is the only sanctified presence in the scene — a silent irony that the first viewers, moving from this panel into the story of divine vindication that follows, were surely meant to register.

The most audacious element of the composition, however, is its emptiness. The marble precinct and its figures are crowded into the left four fifths of the field; the right fifth is nothing but the deep azurite blue, interrupted only by the narrow platform on which Joachim is forced to step, its edge falling away before the open blue. Joachim is being pushed not merely out of the Temple but towards a void. Modern criticism has repeatedly dwelt on this vacancy as one of Giotto’s most radical narrative inventions: the unpainted space becomes an actor, the visible form of exclusion itself, anticipating the wilderness into which the old man will withdraw in the following scene. The composition also works across the panels: the sanctuary platform at the left end of the register finds its answering structure in the bedchamber of Anna two scenes on, and the blue into which Joachim stumbles is the same continuous heaven that arches over the entire cycle, so that his disgrace is staged, literally, under the eyes of the God who will reverse it.

Technically the panel displays the mixed procedure that the restoration campaign of 2001–2002 documented throughout the nave: the figures and architecture executed in buon fresco over the giornate of fresh plaster, the background laid in azurite bound *a secco* over a prepared ground, the halo and the gold borders of the vestments applied with mordant gilding. The fictive marbles of the parapet belong to the same family of polished, illusionistic surfaces as the dado below, and remind us that from the very first scene Giotto conceived the cycle as a continuous fabric of real and feigned materials.

### Joachim among the Shepherds (Scenes from the Life of Joachim, 2)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-02.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Before a rocky ochre ridge crowned with round-headed trees against a deep blue sky, the elderly haloed Joachim enters from the left, wrapped in a rose mantle, his head bowed and hands drawn into his drapery. A small white dog leaps up towards him. Facing him stand two young shepherds, one holding a tall staff, who look at each other rather than at the old man. At the right, a wooden hut with a pyramidal shingled roof shelters a dense flock of sheep pressing out of its dark doorway, led by a ram with curled horns">
  <figcaption>Joachim among the Shepherds, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), upper register of the south wall. Expelled from the Temple, Joachim withdraws in shame to his flocks in the wilderness: two shepherds exchange an uncertain glance, while only the small dog springs forward to welcome him.</figcaption>
</figure>

The second scene of the cycle carries the narrative out of the Temple and into the wilderness, and with it Giotto paints what is in effect the first landscape of the chapel. The apocryphal sources — the Protevangelium of James and the Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew, known to the Trecento above all through the Legenda aurea — relate that Joachim, ashamed to return home to Anna after the public refusal of his offering, went instead to his shepherds in the hills, resolving to remain among his flocks and to make of his exile a penance. Giotto condenses that decision into a single arrested moment: the instant of arrival, before a word has been spoken, when the disgraced man stands at the threshold of the pastoral world that must now receive him. Nothing happens in the scene, and that is precisely its subject. After the institutional drama of the rejection, the cycle pauses on silence, and the silence is made visible.

Joachim enters from the left, continuing the movement with which the priest expelled him from the sanctuary in the preceding panel, so that the two scenes read as one continuous action across the painted frame. He is the same patriarchal figure, grey-bearded and haloed, wrapped in the same rose mantle; but everything in his bearing has changed. The upright elder who faced the priest now walks with his head sunk forward, his eyes lowered to the ground, his hands withdrawn into the folds of the drapery so that the mantle closes around him like a shell. The gesture is one of Giotto’s profoundest inventions of body language: a man who no longer offers anything, who has literally nothing in his hands, folded into himself by shame. The heavy, columnar fall of the rose drapery, hemmed with gold and lined with patterned silk at the hem, gives the figure the gravity of a mourning statue, and the halo — burnished gold worked *a secco*, catching the real light of the south windows — sits above the bowed head with a kind of quiet irony: sanctity unrecognized, even by its bearer.

The reception he meets is a masterpiece of social observation. The two young shepherds stand before their fold in rough tunics of grey-violet and dusty rose, one grasping a tall staff, a knife at his companion’s belt. Neither moves towards the newcomer. Instead they look at each other — a sidelong, questioning exchange in which surprise, embarrassment and deliberation are all legible. What is to be done with a master who arrives unannounced, humiliated, and does not lift his eyes? Giotto stages the awkwardness of the encounter with an economy that no written source dictated: the hesitation of subordinates before a superior’s disgrace, the suspended instant in which no one yet knows the etiquette of pity. The only creature unburdened by such calculations is the small white dog that springs up at Joachim’s knee, forepaws lifted, muzzle raised in greeting. The animal’s uncomplicated loyalty — it alone welcomes the man whom the Temple has rejected and the shepherds cannot yet acknowledge — is among the most quietly affecting details in the chapel, and criticism has long cherished it as the emotional hinge of the scene: the dog completes an arc that descends from the averted human faces to the one being who is glad.

The setting is built with the abstractive logic that governs landscape throughout the cycle. A ridge of pale ochre rock rises behind the figures, its stepped, faceted masses cut as if with a chisel, scaled not to nature but to the needs of the drama: it closes the scene like the back wall of a stage, isolating the figures against it, and its barrenness gives body to Joachim’s desolation. Along its crest a row of round-headed trees — each leaf cluster patiently described — stands against the azurite sky, a schematic grove that reads as sign rather than description: this is “the wilderness”, stated with the brevity of a word. At the right, the sheepfold: a timber hut with a pyramidal shingled roof, drawn obliquely so that its volume projects convincingly into the space, its dark doorway releasing a dense, jostling stream of sheep. The flock is painted with evident delight — ewes pressing shoulder to shoulder, a ram with heavy curled horns leading the way, muzzles lowered and lifted — and its warm, living disorder plays against the frozen decorum of the human group. The animals, like the dog, do not know that anything is wrong.

Beneath the quiet surface the composition is exactly calculated. Joachim’s bowed silhouette at the left and the hut at the right frame the two shepherds at the centre; the diagonal of the ridge descends from the high rocky outcrop above the fold towards the old man’s halo, binding the terms of his new life — rock, flock, shelter — into a single arc above his head. The blue, which in the previous scene was the void into which he was expelled, has here become a sky over an inhabitable world, and the following panels will fill it with the angel of the annunciation to Joachim and the sacrifice whose smoke ascends to a blessing hand. The viewer who reads the register in sequence thus watches the same blue change meaning from scene to scene — exclusion, refuge, revelation — one of the subtlest demonstrations of how Giotto makes a continuous heaven serve a discontinuous story.

Technically the panel shows the procedure documented by the 2001–2002 restoration throughout the nave: figures, rocks and architecture in buon fresco over the day’s fresh plaster, the sky in azurite laid *a secco* over a prepared ground — with the losses characteristic of that fragile technique visible in places along the upper field — and the halo and mantle borders in mordant gilding. Within the history of European art its claims are large. Here, for the first time in the cycle, Giotto sets solid human figures in an open landscape under a continuous sky and entrusts the entire meaning of the image to posture, glance and interval. The sacred story is told in the grammar of observed humanity, down to the leap of a dog.

### The Annunciation to St Anne (Scenes from the Life of Joachim, 3)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-03.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. A house shown in cutaway against a deep blue ground: a gabled pediment with a fictive relief of a blessing figure between two angels crowns the main chamber, whose front wall is removed to reveal Anna kneeling in profile in a red gown, hands joined in prayer, a gold halo behind her head. Beside her stands a curtained bed; domestic objects hang on the rear wall. From a window at the upper right an angel with red wings and a halo leans into the room, head and shoulders only, hand raised towards Anna. At the left, under an external staircase with a balustraded landing, a maidservant in pale yellow sits spinning with spindle and distaff.">
  <figcaption>The Annunciation to St Anne, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), upper register of the south wall. In the chamber of her house, the praying Anna receives the angel who announces that she will conceive; the heavenly messenger thrusts head and shoulders through the window, while a maidservant sits spinning in the porch below the stairs, unaware of the miracle within.</figcaption>
</figure>

With the third scene the narrative divides, as the apocryphal sources require, between husband and wife: while Joachim does penance among his flocks, grace seeks out Anna at home. The apocryphal sources tell that Anna, lamenting at once her barrenness and her husband’s unexplained absence, was visited by an angel who promised her a child destined for glory; a parallel annunciation, painted two scenes later, reaches Joachim in the wilderness. Giotto thus opens a diptych of revelations that will converge at the Golden Gate, and he gives the domestic half of that diptych a setting of extraordinary novelty: a house rendered as a habitable box, its front wall removed, within which the sacred event unfolds in the midst of furniture, hangings and the humblest utensils of daily life. No earlier surviving Italian painting had brought an angel so matter-of-factly into a bourgeois interior, and the scene stands at the head of a long lineage of annunciations in furnished chambers that runs through the entire later history of European painting.

The architecture is a small manifesto of Giotto’s method. The house is seen obliquely from the left, so that its gabled porch, external staircase and balustraded upper landing project convincingly into the viewer’s space, while the main chamber opens frontally in cutaway, framed like a proscenium by pilasters banded with ornament. The pediment above carries a fictive relief — a half-length blessing figure within a radiance, borne by two flying angels, painted in the pale monochrome of sculpture — so that the heaven which is about to break into the room is already announced, in stone as it were, on the facade. The illusionism is layered with deliberate wit: a painted house wearing a painted relief, inside a chapel whose own dado imitates marble statuary. Within the chamber every furnishing is observed with inventory-like candour: the bed with its striped, tasselled coverlet and the white curtain drawn along its rod; the small shelf on the rear wall bearing what appears to be a lamp or candle; the towel hanging from a ring and the dark flask suspended beside it; the red chest at the foot of the bed, its lid studded with nails. These objects have invited symbolic readings — the chest as the ark, the hanging cloth as purity — but their first function is more radical: they establish that the miracle occurs in a real room, at a particular hour, among things that could be touched.

Into this sealed domestic volume the angel enters by the only opening left — the window. It is one of the boldest inventions in the chapel. The messenger does not stand before Anna in the conventional manner; he is caught in the act of arrival, head, shoulders and a single blessing hand thrust through the window frame, his red wings and the swirl of his rose mantle still outside, pressed against the wall as if the velocity of his descent had not yet spent itself. The halo overlaps the window’s edge; the body remains divided between two worlds. Giotto has made the architecture itself dramatize the theology: heaven does not dissolve the walls of the human house but penetrates them at their one point of permeability, and the partial figure — a device of almost cinematic daring — renders the suddenness of grace as no complete figure could. The angel’s profile is bent towards Anna, lips parted in speech; the words of the promise travel diagonally across the room, over the bed, to the kneeling woman.

Anna herself is all reception. She kneels in strict profile beside the bed, dressed in a red gown with gold-embroidered cuffs and collar, a veil of white falling behind the gold leaf of her halo. Her hands are joined before her breast, her chin lifted, her eyes fixed on the apparition. Giotto has made her neither young nor idealized: the long profile with its strong nose and the furrow beside the mouth belongs to a woman of years, and precisely this gravity gives the promise of a child its full weight of improbability and wonder. The red mantle she has laid aside spills from the bed’s step beneath her knees, a quiet notation that she was interrupted — that prayer, here, is something a woman was in the middle of. Between her raised hands and the angel’s blessing hand the composition stretches its essential thread; everything else in the chamber holds still around that axis.

The most humanly disarming figure, however, sits outside the sacred perimeter. Beneath the external staircase, in the shadowed porch, a maidservant in a pale yellow gown sits spinning, the loaded distaff raised in her left hand, the spindle dropped on its thread from her right. She hears nothing, sees nothing; the wall between porch and chamber divides the scene into two simultaneous and incommunicable worlds, revelation within and routine without. The sources give Anna’s servant a name — Judith in the Protevangelium, where her taunt deepens Anna’s grief — and some commentators have read the spinner as that same sharp-tongued maid, now excluded from the mystery; but Giotto’s figure carries no polemic. She is simply the ordinary world continuing, and her patient thread, the oldest emblem of domestic time, measures the very hours into which eternity has just entered unnoticed. The later tradition — down to the sleeping attendants of countless sacre conversazioni — lives in its debt.

Technically the panel follows the mixed procedure established by the 2001–2002 restoration campaign for the whole nave: the architecture and figures in buon fresco, the deep azurite field laid *a secco* around the house’s silhouette, gold applied with mordant to the haloes and to the embroidered borders of Anna’s gown. The green-grey of the chamber walls sets off the two reds — Anna’s gown, the angel’s wings — so that the eye binds annunciant and annunciate before it has read a single gesture. Within the register the scene also works as architecture among architectures: Anna’s house answers the Temple precinct at the head of the wall, interior to exterior, and its chamber will return, identical, in the Birth of the Virgin on the opposite wall — Giotto’s quiet convention of the persistent set, by which the viewer learns that sacred history happens in places that endure, and can be revisited.

### The Sacrifice of Joachim (Scenes from the Life of Joachim, 4)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-04.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. In a rocky ochre landscape against a deep blue sky, the elderly haloed Joachim kneels in profile in a rose mantle, leaning towards a pink masonry altar filled with flames, on whose top lie the skeletal remains of the sacrificed animal. Above the altar a small dark human figure, the soul of the offering, ascends towards a blessing hand emerging from the sky at the upper centre. At the right stands a haloed angel in pale rose, one hand raised towards the altar, the other holding a trefoil-tipped rod against the shoulder. At the left a young shepherd stands praying, hands joined; below, sheep and goats rest and graze on the barren slope">
  <figcaption>The Sacrifice of Joachim, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), upper register of the south wall. In the wilderness Joachim offers a burnt sacrifice to know whether his prayers are heard: the victim is consumed on the flaming altar, an angel attends the rite, and from the height of the blue the blessing hand of God appears to accept the offering.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fourth scene answers the first. The cycle opened with an offering refused at the heart of the institution; here, in the open wilderness, far from priest and precinct, a second offering is accepted — and accepted not by human mediation but by heaven directly. The apocryphal narrative, transmitted through the Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew and the Legenda aurea, tells that Joachim, during his penitential exile among the flocks, sacrificed to the Lord in order to know whether his disgrace was final, and received with the burnt offering the assurance that his prayer had risen. Giotto builds the panel as the theological inversion of the Rejection: there a sanctuary of white marble and an outstretched arm that expelled; here a rough altar on bare rock and a hand that blesses. The viewer who reads the register in sequence is meant to feel the symmetry — the Temple said no, and God says yes — and to understand that the story of the Virgin’s conception begins with a judgement overturned.

The altar stands at the centre of the composition on the crest of the rocky rise, a squat construction of pink masonry with a projecting cornice, its two arched openings glowing from within: Giotto paints the fire not as decorative flicker but as a contained furnace, tongues of flame filling the arches and licking up the inner walls, the brightest and hottest passage of colour in the whole upper register. On the altar slab lies what the fire has left of the victim — a small skeletal carcass, ribs and spine starkly described, the head thrown back. Along this axis, midway between the burnt victim and the divine hand, a small dark form rises against the blue: a diminutive figure in human shape, ascending.

It is the soul of the offering made visible — the medieval convention that renders the invisible substance of prayer and sacrifice as a little human form, the same animula that the age painted issuing from the mouths of the dying and that Giotto himself would employ for the soul of the Virgin in the Dormition. The vertical thus completes itself before our eyes: on the altar the consumed body, in mid-air the ascending soul, at the summit the hand that receives. No clearer diagram of the accepted sacrifice could be drawn, and its placement at the exact centre of the field makes it the theological hinge of the panel — the visible proof, granted to Joachim and to the viewer alike, that the prayer has risen. Directly above, at the summit of the field, the answer appears: a hand emerging from the blue, foreshortened, fingers extended in blessing.

Giotto gives the theophany no surrounding cloud or mandorla visible today — the hand alone breaks the continuous azure, as if the sky of the chapel had opened at a single point. No further trace of cloud or mandorla survives in the blue along the same vertical, and the fragile *a secco* surface counsels caution; the composition’s meaning does not depend on it.

Joachim himself is a study in abasement transfigured. He kneels in profile on the slope before the altar, his rose mantle gathered in heavy folds across his back so that the whole figure becomes a single bowed mass, like a boulder among the boulders. He leans forward onto his hands — the left, with its embroidered cuff, pressed flat against the rock — and lifts only his bearded face towards the fire, lips parted, eyes wide. The posture is that of the proskynesis, total prostration before the divine, yet Giotto fills it with suspense rather than repose: Joachim is caught at the instant of looking up, the moment between petition and answer, and the golden halo above the straining profile burns like a second flame. The man whom we saw folded shut in shame two scenes earlier here opens again towards heaven, and the drapery tells the story as eloquently as the face — no longer a shell to hide in, but a weight the body rises against.

The two witnesses frame the mystery without intruding on it. At the right stands the angel, a tall figure in pale rose and white with gold-hemmed mantle, wings folded, the halo tooled in gold; the right hand is raised towards the altar in a gesture of presentation or assent, as if officiating at the rite the Temple refused to perform — heaven supplying its own priesthood. In the left hand, held against the shoulder, the angel carries a slender rod tipped with a trefoil — the staff or sceptre that Byzantine tradition assigns to the heavenly messenger, its three-lobed head carrying an unmistakable Trinitarian resonance.

The attribute confirms the figure’s office: this is heaven’s herald and officiant, rather than the angel of the promise, who belongs to the following scene; the painter keeps the two offices distinct. At the left, standing on the lower ground, a young shepherd in the rough grey tunic and leggings of the previous panel joins his hands in prayer and gazes upward past Joachim towards the hand of God — the ordinary man as witness, admitted to the revelation that the institution missed. His quiet verticality balances the angel’s on the opposite margin, so that the kneeling patriarch and the burning altar are held between two standing figures, one human and one celestial, in a composition of almost heraldic clarity.

Below, on the barren foreground slope, the flock — sheep, goats, a ram with curled horns, a dark buck clambering over its neighbour — rests and grazes with the same untroubled animal life that warmed the scene of Joachim’s arrival. Giotto scatters among the rocks a few dry shrubs and, at the right, small flowering plants picked out leaf by leaf against the ochre: the wilderness is not quite dead, and the delicate vegetation under the angel’s feet reads almost as a first hint of the fruitfulness about to be restored to Joachim’s house. The rocky ridge itself, rising steeply at the left and falling towards the right, repeats in reverse the ridge of the second scene, as though the narrative were moving across the back of the same mountain; the continuity of terrain between the pastoral panels is one of Giotto’s quiet devices for binding separate episodes into a single remembered country.

Technically the panel displays the now familiar division of labour documented by the 2001–2002 restoration: figures, rocks and altar in buon fresco; the azure laid *a secco* in azurite, with the irregularities and losses to which that technique is prone visible across the upper field; the haloes and the gold borders of mantle and cuff applied with mordant. The reds are orchestrated with particular care — the fire in the altar, Joachim’s rose mantle, the angel’s drapery — a chain of warmth ascending from the earthbound to the celestial across the cool blue. Within the economy of the register the scene completes the preparation of Joachim: rejected, withdrawn, and now heard, he has only to receive the explicit promise, which comes to him in sleep in the panel that follows; and the hand that here breaks the sky for the first time in the cycle announces the greater interventions to come, in a chapel whose entire painted heaven exists, as the chancel arch will declare, to be opened.

### The Dream of Joachim (Scenes from the Life of Joachim, 5)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-05.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. In a barren rocky landscape against a deep blue sky, the elderly haloed Joachim sits asleep at the right, completely wrapped in a rose mantle, his head sunk on his knees, before the dark doorway of a wooden hut with a pyramidal roof. From the upper left an angel with red wings and gold halo flies steeply down towards him, right arm extended, a trefoil-tipped rod in the left hand and a trail of cloud behind. At the left stand two shepherds: the hooded one in dark brown looks up and sees the angel, while his companion in a wide-brimmed hat, holding a staff, gazes ahead unaware. Sheep and goats rest on the pale slope between them and the sleeper">
  <figcaption>The Dream of Joachim, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), upper register of the south wall. Asleep before the sheepfold, Joachim receives in a dream the angel’s promise: his prayer has been heard, Anna has conceived, and he is to return to her without fear. Two shepherds and the flock attend the apparition from the left, but only the hooded one sees it.</figcaption>
</figure>

The fifth scene of the cycle delivers the promise for which everything before it has prepared, and delivers it to a man who is asleep. The apocryphal sources — the Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew above all, mediated by the Legenda aurea — relate that after Joachim’s sacrifice an angel appeared to him, declared that his prayer and his alms had ascended before God, announced that the deeds of mercy he and Anna had done were known before the Most High, that Anna had conceived a daughter destined for a blessedness beyond all women, and commanded him to return to her without fear. In Pseudo-Matthew the command to go to the gate called Golden is given to Anna alone, after the thirty days of Joachim’s homeward journey; the later tradition transmitted by the De nativitate Mariae and the Legenda aurea extends the sign of the gate to Joachim as well, and it is this doubled promise that the cycle’s next panel will fulfil.

Giotto sets the annunciation in sleep, as the visual tradition of Joachim’s vision generally did, and in doing so creates one of the most quietly radical images of the entire chapel: a revelation whose recipient does not see it, witnessed instead by the open eyes of shepherds, animals and the viewer. The panel completes the diptych begun with the Annunciation to Anna two scenes earlier — the wife reached through the window of her chamber, the husband through the gate of sleep — and sets in motion the return journey that the Meeting at the Golden Gate will conclude.

Joachim’s sleeping figure is the formal and emotional centre of the composition, and it has been admired for seven centuries as a summit of Giotto’s plastic imagination. The old man sits on the bare ground before the door of the sheepfold, knees drawn up, and the rose mantle is wrapped around the whole of him — body, arms, legs — so that nothing emerges but the bearded head, fallen forward onto the knees, and the edge of one foot. The drapery, modelled in long unbroken slopes of light and shadow with a gold-fringed hem pooling on the rock, turns the figure into a single closed volume, massive and mineral, a human boulder set among the boulders of the wilderness. The invention concentrates into one silhouette everything the preceding scenes have told us of Joachim: the man who folded himself shut in shame now folds himself into sleep, and the same gesture of closure becomes, unknown to him, the vessel of grace. The gold halo resting above the sunken head keeps vigil over a sanctity that, for once, is entirely passive. Later criticism has repeatedly singled out this bundled sleeper as a founding image of the represented dream in Western painting — consciousness shown from outside, as pure bodily fact.

The angel is all velocity. He enters from the upper left in steep descending flight, body nearly horizontal, the great wings — red above, shading to grey beneath — beating against the blue, and behind him trails a pale wisp of cloud, the visible wake of his passage out of heaven. The right arm is thrust forward and down towards the sleeper, the hand open in the act of address; the profile is bent, lips parted, the words of the promise already travelling the empty diagonal between his fingers and Joachim’s shrouded form.

In the left hand, held back against the body, the messenger carries the same trefoil-tipped rod borne by the officiating angel of the Sacrifice in the preceding scene — the angelic sceptre of Byzantine descent. The repeated attribute binds the two apparitions into a single embassy: the herald who presided over the accepted offering now returns at speed to deliver its answer. Nothing in the chapel moves faster than this figure, and nothing is stiller than the man he addresses: the whole meaning of the scene is strung between those two states, the urgency of heaven and the unconsciousness of earth, across a gulf of blue that Giotto leaves almost entirely empty. That emptiness, once again, is structural. The diagonal of the rocky ridge descends from right to left precisely to clear the upper field for the flight, and the void between angel and sleeper is the space of the dream itself.

The witnesses complicate the scene in a way no written source demanded. At the left stand the two shepherds of the earlier panels — one hooded in dark brown, the other in a broad-brimmed traveller’s hat, leaning on his staff with a knife slung at his neck — and Giotto divides the vision between them with the finest of calibrations. Only the hooded shepherd, the darker and more withdrawn of the two, has lifted his eyes to the angel: his face, half-shadowed by the cowl, turns upward and follows the flight. His companion sees nothing; his gaze runs level, ahead across the slope, and the apparition passes above him unperceived.

The distinction is deliberate and profound. Revelation, in this panel, is granted in degrees — fully to the sleeping patriarch in his dream, partially to one humble watcher, not at all to the man standing beside him — and the three conditions of seeing are ranged across the field like a quiet demonstration that grace chooses its witnesses without regard to station or wakefulness. Their alert verticality, answering the closed mass of the sleeper across the width of the field, anchors the composition’s left margin as the hut anchors its right.

Between the two groups the flock rests and stirs on the pale slope: a lamb couched on a ledge, a dark goat clambering below it, a horned ram picking its way down among the tufted shrubs that Giotto scatters, leaf by leaf, across the barren ground. The animals’ indifference, by now a running motif of the Joachim panels, here acquires a shade of irony: the creatures are awake while the man sleeps, and none of them knows that the wilderness is ending.

The setting binds the pastoral group of scenes into a single remembered place. The hut is the same timber sheepfold, with the same pyramidal shingled roof and dark doorway, before which Joachim first arrived in the second panel; the ridge behind it, crowned at the right by a tall splintered crag with a wind-bent tree, continues the same rocky country. Giotto’s convention of the persistent set — Anna’s chamber on one side of the register, the sheepfold on the other — gives the two interleaved narratives their geography, and the viewer who has followed the wall from the Temple now knows this ground as Joachim knows it. That familiarity is about to be dissolved: the next scene will carry the old man back to the city, and the sheepfold will not appear again. The Dream is thus the last and fullest statement of the wilderness theme, and the fact that the promise of return is spoken here, at the heart of the exile, over the head of a man too weary to hear it waking, is among the most humane strokes of the whole cycle.

Technically the panel follows the mixed procedure documented by the 2001–2002 restoration throughout the nave: figures, rocks and hut in buon fresco over the day’s plaster; the sky in azurite laid a secco, with the characteristic unevenness of that fragile layer visible across the broad blue field; haloes and hems in mordant gilding; and the angel’s cloud-trail brushed thinly over the completed azure. The pale, almost lunar tonality of the rocks — colder here than in the neighbouring scenes — isolates the two warm accents of the composition, the rose of the mantle and the red of the wings, so that sleeper and messenger call to each other chromatically across the void before the eye has measured the distance between them. It is the simplest of means, and it produces one of the profoundest images in the chapel: the annunciation that arrives, as grace so often does in Giotto’s Padua, at the one moment when no one is looking.

### The Meeting at the Golden Gate (Scenes from the Life of Joachim, 6)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-06.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Before a pale city gate with two crenellated towers and a round arch gilded on its inner face, the elderly Joachim and Anna embrace on a stone bridge, their haloed heads pressed together in a kiss, her hand cradling his face. Behind Anna stand five women: one wrapped in a dark mantle that half hides her face, the others in festive dresses of cream, red and green, one carrying folded cloths. At the far left a young herdsman with a basket, a pole over his shoulder, enters the scene cut in half by the painted frame. The sky above is deep blue.">
  <figcaption>The Meeting at the Golden Gate, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), upper register of the south wall. Obeying the angel’s command, Joachim returns from the wilderness and Anna comes out of Jerusalem to meet him: the spouses embrace and kiss on the bridge before the gilded gate, the moment that medieval tradition read as the immaculate conception of the Virgin. A group of women attends the reunion, one wrapped in a dark mantle; at the left a herdsman of Joachim’s company enters the field, half cut by the frame.</figcaption>
</figure>

The sixth panel resolves everything. The two narrative threads that the register has kept apart since the Rejection — the husband in the wilderness, the wife in the city — here converge on a bridge before the walls of Jerusalem, and the cycle’s first movement closes with an embrace. The apocryphal sources, transmitted to the Trecento through the Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew and the Legenda aurea, tell of the angel’s command — given to Anna alone in Pseudo-Matthew, to both spouses in the later tradition that the Legenda aurea transmits — converging on a single threshold: Anna, warned that her husband would come to her that very day, goes out with her maidens to the gate called Golden and waits.

Theology had long read the meeting as more than a reunion. In the tradition that would eventually crystallize as the doctrine of the Immaculate Conception, the kiss of Joachim and Anna at the Porta Aurea was understood as the very moment of Mary’s conception without concupiscence — generation by promise and by embrace. Giotto paints that doctrine as an event between two elderly people who have missed each other, and the panel’s enduring power lies precisely in the fact that the dogma and the human reunion are the same image, neither reducible to the other.

The kiss itself is one of the most audacious passages in early Italian painting. Joachim, entering from the left in his familiar rose mantle, and Anna, come out from the gate in her orange-ochre gown, lean into each other until their profiles merge: forehead against forehead, nose against cheek, the two faces fusing into a single contour within the overlap of their golden haloes, which Giotto lets intersect like two coins pressed together. Anna’s right hand rises to cradle her husband’s bearded head — a gesture of possession and tenderness without precedent in the schematic embraces of the Byzantine tradition — while her left arm circles his shoulder, and his hands gather her towards him under the mantle. The painter has studied what an actual embrace does to two bodies: the braced feet, the bell-like merging of the draperies into one mass of rose and red, the slight bow of the old man’s back. Nothing is idealized, and for that reason everything is transfigured.

Within the chapel itself the kiss finds its dark twin, two registers below, in the other fatal meeting of mouths — Judas closing on Christ — so that the wall itself teaches the viewer that an embrace can carry either life or death.

The architecture concentrates the meaning. The Golden Gate is a massive pale structure of two crenellated towers flanking a round-arched portal, its rusticated lower courses picked out block by block, its upper walls smooth and slotted with arrowloops, the open timber galleries of the towers standing against the blue. Giotto takes the gate’s name literally: the inner face of the arch is gilded, a broad band of gold leaf — now partly abraded, with the dark underlayer showing through — that turns the portal into a halo for the event taking place before it. Through the opening a glimpse of a coffered passage and the city beyond establishes depth with the same economy as the coretti beside the chancel arch. In front, a stone bridge with a low parapet spans a stream or moat, its arch answering the arch of the gate; the spouses meet upon it, suspended between city and wilderness, exactly on the threshold that the whole pastoral sequence has been preparing. The bridge is Giotto’s addition to the iconography, and it does quiet theological work: the meeting happens on a crossing, neither inside nor outside, as befits a conception that bridges nature and grace.

The witnesses divide, once again, into degrees. Behind Anna a group of women has come out of the gate — her maidens, whom Pseudo-Matthew sends with her to the Golden Gate (*cum puellis suis*) — their festive dresses of cream, vermilion with gold-embroidered bands, and green, their braided hair bound with ribbons, giving the reunion the air of a civic celebration; the foremost of them, in cream, carries folded cloths over her arm, perhaps the garments or linens that custom brought to such a homecoming. But at the head of the group, closest to the embracing pair, stands a figure who has troubled viewers for a century: a woman entirely wrapped in a black mantle drawn up over her head, half-veiling her face, her eyes alone turned towards the kiss.

Her darkness, struck into the middle of the bright procession, has received many readings, but the source itself offers the key: in Pseudo-Matthew, Anna greets her returning husband with the words “Vidua eram et ecce iam non sum” — I was a widow, and behold I am so no longer; I was barren, and behold I have conceived. The dark-mantled woman standing at the edge of the embrace is best understood as the visible form of that dismissed condition, the widowed and barren past that the kiss abolishes. Her shadow makes the joy measurable, and Giotto, who never wastes a bystander, surely intended the dissonance. At the opposite margin, a last witness enters from the wilderness side: a young herdsman with a pole over his shoulder and a wicker basket, sliced in half by the painted frame.

He is Joachim’s man, following his master home with the gear of the pastoral life now ended — Pseudo-Matthew has Joachim return with his flocks and herdsmen — and his truncation is one of the most discussed formal devices in the chapel: the frame cuts him as a doorway cuts a figure passing through it, declaring that the painted field is a window onto a world that continues beyond its edges.

Compositionally the panel gathers the register’s long horizontal momentum and turns it vertical. Everything that has moved — Joachim expelled rightward, walking on rightward into the wilderness, the angel plunging, the shepherd following — comes to rest in the paired figures at the left of centre, locked into a single pyramidal mass, while the gate rises behind them like a benediction in masonry. The blue above is here simply sky over a city: no hand, no messenger, nothing but the completed promise. The women’s diagonal procession, descending from the arch to the embrace, delivers the eye to the kiss along the same path by which Anna herself arrived. It is a composition of profound repose, and it needs to be: the Virgin’s story will open, on the opposite wall, with the Birth of the Virgin — the fruit of this embrace — and the viewer who follows the story around the chapel experiences the Golden Gate as both an ending and a hinge.

Technically the panel shows the campaign’s established division of labour: architecture and figures in buon fresco, the azurite sky laid a secco with its characteristic losses along the upper field, gold applied with mordant to the haloes, to the embroidered borders of the women’s gowns, and — exceptionally — to the great band of the arch, where the gilding’s partial abrasion now exposes the preparation beneath. The chromatic design is among the subtlest in the register: the rose and red of the embracing pair fuse into a single warm core, framed by the pale masonry and set off by the one black accent of the veiled woman, so that the eye reads the scene’s whole emotional structure — union, celebration, shadow — before a single gesture has been deciphered. With this image the story of Joachim and Anna is complete.

## The Scrovegni Chapel: The Story of the Virgin

The second movement of the Arena Chapel’s upper register tells how the child promised to Joachim and Anna became the wife of Joseph — the story that runs from Mary’s birth to her bridal procession, painted by Giotto around 1303–1305 along the north wall, facing the six scenes of her parents across the void of the nave. Like the Joachim cycle it answers, it rests not on the canonical Gospels, which open with Mary already betrothed, but on the apocryphal tradition: the Protevangelium of James and, above all, the Gospel of Pseudo-Matthew, the Latin retelling through which the medieval West received the Virgin’s early life. From that tradition come the birth in Anna’s house, the three-year-old climbing the Temple steps alone, the ordeal of the rods by which a husband was found for a girl vowed to virginity, and the five named companions who walked her to Joseph’s house. The chapel itself was dedicated to Santa Maria della Carità, and stood beside the old Roman arena where Padua staged its annual re-enactment of the Annunciation: a cycle of the Virgin’s own story, the fullest painted in Italy to that day, was not an ornament of the program but its foundation.

Read in sequence, the six scenes trace a single transfer: Mary passes from her mother’s hands to the Temple’s, and from the Temple’s to Joseph’s, before God claims her for a purpose none of her guardians suspects. The register is thus the hinge of the whole chapel. Above, it receives the story of her parents — the same architecture returns, and the Temple that drove Joachim from its precinct opens its arms to his daughter; below and beyond, it hands the narrative to the chancel arch, where the Annunciation waits, and to the life of Christ on the walls beneath. Giotto paces the transfer like a dramatist. Two moments share one frame in the Birth; a single suspended beat fills the Bringing of the Rods; the Prayer of the Suitors stops time altogether, a whole panel of kneeling men beneath the barely visible hand of God descending in the apse; and the resolution, when it comes, is withheld from view — the flowering happens between the panels, and we meet it as a fact in an old man’s fist. No painter had yet treated a wall as a sequence of tensions held and released; this register is where that art was invented.

Its quietest theme is the dignity of the margin. Twice Joseph stands last in line, half-cut by the frame, his halo the only sign against the social logic that discounts him; in the Marriage he is at the centre, and the rejected suitors — sober, young, merely human — inherit his old place at the edge. Mary herself is painted throughout in the same key of composed interiority: the small girl who climbs ten steps without looking back, the bride whose hand advances a final centimetre toward Joseph’s, the young woman gathering her train as the trumpets sound. Around these two reticent figures Giotto builds the loud, observed world of his own Padua — midwives and basins, a gift passed through a doorway, an altar dressed in imported silk, a wedding guest’s hand raised for the ritual blow, musicians ahead of the bride and a green bough hung from the house that receives her — so that the sacred story advances entirely inside the textures of contemporary life, witnessed by buildings that recur, rotate, and seem at last to greet the woman who will sanctify them.

The six scenes follow the north wall from the chancel toward the entrance: the Birth of the Virgin, in the same house where the angel appeared to Anna; the Presentation in the Temple, the child ascending to the priest’s open arms; the Bringing of the Rods, the suitors surrendering their wands at the altar; the Prayer of the Suitors, the community on its knees beneath the blessing hand; the Marriage of the Virgin, the flowered rod, the dove, and the joining of hands; and the Wedding Procession, Mary conducted with music toward Joseph’s house and toward the purple thread that the Annunciation will find in her hands. Together the six panels carry the chapel’s story to the threshold of the chancel arch, where God summons Gabriel and the history of salvation begins.
