Giotto accepts the iconography and humanises it past its own intention: his Joseph is simply an old man at the end of a hard journey, hunched into his ochre mantle, chin sunk, hands folded, sleeping the sleep of the exhausted just. He is placed directly below the manger, so that the vertical of the composition runs from the adoring angels through the Child to the sleeping guardian: heaven awake, the mother awake, the animals awake, and the one ordinary man in the picture granted the mercy of rest.

The right half of the panel belongs to the world’s first notice. Two shepherds stand with their backs to us — Giotto’s instrument, as always, for opening the space and planting the viewer inside it — their rough cloaks falling in heavy verticals, their faces hidden; we see exactly what they see, which is an angel plunging headlong out of the sky toward them, caught at the moment the good tidings begin. Their flock rests at their feet, sheep pressed together, a goat rising dark among them with the same observed matter-of-factness as the herds of Joachim’s exile — the shepherd world of the cycle’s opening returning, two registers down, to receive the news that ends all exile. Above the shed, four more angels hover in a broken line, hands joined or spread, each a distinct note of jubilation; with the diving messenger they make five, and the group reads less like a choir posed for adoration than like adoration itself breaking out, mid-air, in real time.

Time has taken from this panel with both hands, and what it took is instructive. Mary’s mantle, once the most precious surface of the scene — azurite laid a secco over a red preparation — has lost most of its blue, and the red ground now flares through the losses, so that the Virgin lies wrapped in a strange marbling of fire and sky that Giotto never painted; the deep blue above has suffered the same erosion. The damage, honestly read, is a lesson in the chapel’s technique and economy: the costliest pigment reserved for the costliest figure, and the most fragile. Beneath the losses the design stands intact — the long diagonal of the reclining mother, the vertical of the gaze, the counter-diagonal of the diving angel — and the panel keeps, in ruin, the thing Giotto built it around: a woman on a rock, turning to look at her son for the first time.

### The Adoration of the Magi (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 2)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-18.jpg" alt="Fresco of the Adoration of the Magi. At the right, beneath an open timber shed on rocky ground, Mary, haloed, in a red dress and a mantle with extensive blue losses, sits enthroned holding the Christ Child upright on her knee; behind her stand the haloed Joseph and a second angel, and beside her an angel in white holds a tall golden vessel. Before her, the eldest Magus, haloed, in a lilac mantle, kneels and raises the Child’s feet toward his face; his jewelled crown lies on the rock below. At the left stand the two younger Magi, haloed and crowned, in white and orange, one holding a horn-shaped gift, with a young groom behind them gazing up; behind them two camels with red bridles crane forward. In the blue sky above, a comet with a blazing head at the lower right and a long tail trailing up and to the left streaks toward the shed. Heavy azurite losses throughout the blue.">
  <figcaption>The Adoration of the Magi, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the south wall. The eldest of the Magi has laid his crown on the ground and kneels to lift the Christ Child’s feet to his lips; Mary, enthroned beneath the shed with Joseph behind her, holds the Child upright; an angel bears one of the gifts, the two younger kings wait their turn, and a groom steadies the camels. Overhead, in place of the conventional star, burns a comet — long connected, by a modern hypothesis, with the apparition of Halley’s Comet in 1301.</figcaption>
</figure>

The poor man’s shed of the Nativity has become, one panel on, a throne room — without changing a single timber. The same lean-to on the same rock now shelters Mary seated, turned toward the kneeling king, the Child upright on her knee, Joseph behind her beside a second angel, while the first stands at her side holding one of the gifts like an acolyte at an altar. It is Giotto’s quiet way with the theology of the Epiphany. Nothing has been added to the poverty — rock, wood, swaddling cloth — and yet the composition declares that the kings who have crossed the world kneel before a king. The viewer who walks the register reads the transformation at a glance: the reclining mother of the first panel sits in majesty in the second, and what was a birth becomes an audience.

The eldest Magus performs the panel’s act. He has taken off his crown — it lies on the rock at the lower right, a closed circle of gold abandoned on stone, perhaps the most eloquent still-life detail in the chapel — and kneels to lift the Child’s feet toward his lips. The gesture is the full feudal act of homage, the kiss owed by the vassal to the lord, transposed onto an infant who grabs at the old king’s homage with a baby’s unceremonious interest. Giotto builds the exchange with his usual double exposure of the sacred and the human: the king worships; the Child, held steady by his mother’s two hands, behaves like a child. Behind the kneeling elder wait the two younger kings, crowned, grave, their gifts in their hands — one cradling a horn-like vessel — and behind them a beardless groom gazes upward, open-mouthed, at the thing in the sky.

For what burns above this scene is not the polite gold star of tradition but a comet: a blazing head trailing fire across the blue, driving down toward the shed as if still in flight. Painters before Giotto had made the star of Bethlehem a radiant disc or a many-pointed ornament; he painted a celestial event, observed and specific. The identification proposed by [Roberta Olson](/scholars.html#olson-roberta-j-m) in 1979 has become famous: in the autumn of 1301, while Giotto’s Paduan years were beginning, Halley’s Comet crossed the skies of Europe, visible from mid-September into November, and was recorded by the chroniclers — one of whom described its great trailing rays of smoke; the form Giotto gives his star, a condensed fiery head with a streaming tail, matches the comet’s photographed apparitions closely enough that the European Space Agency named its 1986 probe to Halley Giotto, in homage to this fresco. The identification has its dissenters — the painted comet is red where the chronicles of 1301 describe a white one, and a comet seen at Christmas 1304, nearer the feast the panel depicts, has been proposed as a likelier model — and it cannot, in the nature of things, be proven. But the larger truth stands on the wall whichever comet lit it: the first painter to make the sacred story obey the laws of bodies and weights also made its star obey a sky he had actually watched.

The camels complete the panel’s report on the real. They press in from the left edge behind the kings, red-bridled, long-necked, their heads craning forward with a ruminant’s swaying curiosity while the groom steadies them — among the earliest camels in Italian painting rendered as animals rather than heraldry, their anatomy imperfect (Giotto can hardly have studied one at leisure) but their behaviour exact. They carry the East into the picture: the journey, the caravan, the distance crossed, everything Matthew compresses into “from the east to Jerusalem” stands at the edge of the scene chewing and staring. And the angel beside the throne, holding the tall golden vessel of one of the gifts, binds the exotic offering back into liturgy: gold, frankincense and myrrh arriving at the shed.

The blue has suffered here as everywhere — the sky abraded around the comet, Mary’s mantle fallen from azurite to its dark preparation so that the Virgin of the audience sits in ghost-colour — but the panel’s structure survives intact: the diagonal of kneeling homage rising to the enthroned Child, the waiting file of the kings, the fire overhead. Within the register’s sequence the scene does double duty. It closes the infancy’s first chapter in glory — the world’s powers on their knees — and it plants, in the crown laid on the rock, the theme the next panels will darken: what kings do when they meet this Child.

### The Presentation of Christ in the Temple (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 3)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-19.jpg" alt="Fresco of the Presentation in the Temple. At the centre, a white marble ciborium with spiral-fluted columns and a pyramidal roof rises over an altar draped in a red cloth with white geometric lattice; a fleur-de-lis crowns its peak. To its right, the aged Simeon, haloed, white-bearded, wrapped in a white mantle over orange, holds the haloed Christ Child, dressed in an orange-red tunic, who twists in his arms and stretches both hands back toward his mother. Mary, haloed, in an ochre mantle over red, extends both arms to receive him; behind her stand Joseph, haloed, carrying two doves in his draped hands, and a young woman in rose at the far left. At the far right, the elderly prophetess Anna, in an ochre mantle and wimple, raises one hand and holds an inscribed scroll in the other. Above her, an angel dives from the sky toward Simeon. Deep blue ground with heavy losses; dark pavement below.">
  <figcaption>The Presentation of Christ in the Temple, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the south wall. At the altar ciborium of the Temple, the aged Simeon holds the Christ Child, who twists back toward his mother with outstretched arms; Mary reaches to take him home, Joseph carries the offering of doves, the prophetess Anna raises her scroll, and an angel descends toward Simeon. The Temple is marked by the same spiral-columned ciborium beneath which Mary herself was presented, one register above.</figcaption>
</figure>

Forty days after the birth, the Law summons the Child to the Temple — and Giotto summons the viewer’s memory to meet him there. The ciborium at the centre of the scene, white marble on spiral-fluted columns, a draped altar beneath it, is unmistakably the furniture of the Virgin’s own presentation, painted one register above on the opposite wall: the canopy under which the three-year-old Mary was received now receives her son. The recurrence works exactly as the shared house of Anna worked, and as the rotating Temple of the rods worked — architecture as memory, binding the two halves of the program into one history. The mother who once climbed these precincts as an offering returns carrying one; the viewer who has walked both walls knows the building, and knowing it, understands the symmetry the panels never state aloud: every presentation in this chapel is a handing over of a child to God.

Luke’s account gives the scene its cast, and Giotto distributes it with notarial clarity across the width of the panel. Joseph bears the offering of the poor — the pair of turtledoves the Law allowed those who could not afford a lamb — carried carefully in his draped hands like the modest treasure it is. A young woman accompanies the family at the far left, closing the group. At the altar stands Simeon, the just and devout man to whom it had been revealed that he should not see death before he had seen the Lord’s Christ: Giotto gives him a magnificent old age, long white hair and beard streaming over the white mantle, and places in his arms the fulfilment of his whole waiting life. And at the far right stands the prophetess Anna, the widow of fourscore and four years who departed not from the Temple — aged, wimpled, caught in mid-speech with one hand lifted and a scroll unfurling from the other. The scroll can still be read:

> ISTE ERIT REDEMPTIO SECULI - this one shall be the redemption of the world.

The wording is bolder than its source, for Luke’s Anna speaks of the Child to all who looked for the redemption of Jerusalem; the painted prophetess enlarges the promise from the holy city to the *saeculum*, the whole age of the world — the Gentile chapel in Padua writing itself into the prophecy. One material oddity has been noticed: the final word, *seculi*, is darker than the rest and slightly out of alignment, and may be a later emendation of the scroll — a small philological question the inscription still carries.

But the panel’s genius is the Child himself. Simeon holds him — and the baby, held by a stranger, does what babies do: he twists in the old man’s grasp and stretches both arms back toward his mother, his whole small body a single vector of return. Nothing in the iconographic tradition required this; everything in observed life did. The gesture turns the scene’s theology into drama: between Simeon’s arms (the old covenant, satisfied, ready to depart in peace) and Mary’s answering reach (the mother, and with her the human future of the story) the Child hangs suspended, wanted by both, turning from the one to the other — and the prophecy Simeon is about to pronounce over this very exchange, the sword that shall pierce the mother’s own soul, is already legible in the space between the baby’s outstretched hands and hers. Giotto has painted the Nunc dimittis and its cost in a single infant’s squirm.

Above, an angel dives toward Simeon out of the blue — an addition to Luke, who sends no angel to the Temple — and it comes bearing the insignia of its office: a slender rod tipped with a single pointed leaf. The staff belongs to a family of emblems the cycle’s viewers have met before, for the angel of Joachim’s sacrifice and of his dream, two registers above, carried a wand of the same kind, there finished with a trefoil; Giotto equips his heavenly messengers like officers of one court, the slender rod their common badge of embassy, its finial varying from mission to mission. An angelic legate attended the grandfather’s offering at an altar, and an angelic legate attends the grandson’s. The descent, head-first on the diagonal like the announcing angel of the Nativity two panels back, has been read variously: as the visual seal of the revelation Simeon had received (the promise that he would see the Christ, here honoured and discharged), or as the summons the old man’s own canticle invites — now lettest thou thy servant depart — heaven already reaching for the one person in the chapel’s story who asks leave to die. Both readings make the same point in different keys: the angel marks Simeon’s moment as finished, the long watch ended. The fleur-de-lis that crowns the ciborium’s pyramidal roof rises directly on the panel’s central axis, a small vertical flourish of purity over the altar where the exchange takes place.

The altar frontal beneath the ciborium is a red silk figured with the white geometric lattice of the Temple’s other furnishings — the imported magnificence that dressed the altar of the rods, here in its crimson variant — and the whole sacred apparatus stands against a blue that has suffered the register’s usual losses, the azurite fallen in continents around the figures. The panel closes the infancy’s Temple chapter: the Law has been satisfied, the prophets have spoken, the old have seen and may depart. What follows on the wall is flight: the family that here stands composed around an altar will next be seen in motion.

### The Flight into Egypt (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 4)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-20.jpg" alt="Fresco of the Flight into Egypt, moving to the right. At the centre, Mary, haloed, in a red dress under a pale travelling mantle with blue losses, rides a grey ass in strict profile; the haloed Christ Child sits upright against her, secured by a striped sling knotted over her shoulder, his arm around her neck. A youth with a leafy wreath in his hair leads the ass by the bridle, looking back; ahead walks Joseph, haloed, grey-bearded, in an orange mantle, a staff over his shoulder with a small flask and basket hanging from it. Behind the ass follow three young companions in green, red and grey, in conversation; the youth leading the ass carries a water gourd on a stick. Above right, a haloed angel, hands clasped at the chest, flies toward the travellers. Behind, a steep grey mountain rises to a peak against the blue, with scattered dark trees along its ridges.">
  <figcaption>The Flight into Egypt, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the south wall. Warned in a dream, Joseph leads his family out of Herod’s reach: Mary rides the ass with the Child bound to her in a travelling sling, a wreathed youth leads the animal, three companions follow, and the angel of the warning flies ahead, urging the party on. Behind them rises the great grey pyramid of rock that makes the journey monumental.</figcaption>
</figure>

Between this panel and the last, an angel has spoken in the night: rise, take the young child and his mother, and flee into Egypt. Giotto omits the dream — his register has no room for every station of Matthew’s infancy — and paints instead its consequence at full stride: a family in motion, caught mid-journey against a mountain, with the whole measure of the scene given by the slow, continuous, unstoppable walking pace of a loaded ass. After the verticals of the Presentation — ciborium, prophecy, altar — the composition turns horizontal, and the register itself seems to move: every figure, every profile, every gesture points right, in the direction of reading, in the direction of Egypt, away from the crowned man whose answer to the manger fills the next panel. The Flight is the hinge between Epiphany and massacre, and Giotto paints it as pure direction.

Mary is its centre and its monument. She rides in strict profile, upright as a column, and the pyramidal grey mountain behind her repeats and enlarges her silhouette, so that the woman on the ass and the rock of the desert become one form at two scales — the composition’s quiet way of saying that the refugee carries something immovable. The Child does not lie in her arms: he sits against her, upright, and a striped cloth sling, knotted over his mother’s shoulder, binds him to her body — the actual equipment of medieval travel with an infant, painted from life, down to the way the small passenger has worked an arm free to circle his mother’s neck. The practical detail does the theological work: mother and Child, bound into a single bundle, are the cargo of the journey and the reason for it; everything else in the panel exists to move them.

Around this still centre Giotto deploys the caravan. Joseph walks ahead — for once not asleep, not marginal, but in command of the road, his staff over his shoulder with the travelling flask and a small basket slung from it, his bearded face turned back toward the family he leads: the guardian finally guarding. At the ass’s head walks a youth crowned with a leafy wreath, leading the animal by the bridle and looking back at Mary with an expression between encouragement and awe — the green garland on his hair an unexpected festive note in a flight, and an echo the attentive viewer will catch: the viella player who led Mary’s wedding procession, one register above, wore the same wreath of leaves. The procession that carried the bride to Joseph’s house and the caravan that carries her out of Herod’s reach are led by the same sign. And behind follow three young companions in green, red and grey, talking among themselves as walkers do, while the youth at the ass’s head carries the water gourd. The escort comes from the apocrypha: Pseudo-Matthew sends the family into Egypt accompanied — three boys travelling with Joseph and a girl with Mary — and Giotto’s retinue gives the Gospel’s desperate flight the realistic texture of an actual departure: a household on the road, provisions, servants, conversation, the ordinary logistics of extraordinary fear.

The angel above completes the machinery of the journey. It flies ahead of the party, not behind — leaning out of the sky with clasped hands, the guide rather than the guard — and its presence converts the landscape’s emptiness into route: the travellers are not wandering, they are led. Giotto’s angels in this register form a sequence of their own — announcing to the shepherds, attending Simeon, warning and now escorting — heaven’s continuous service to the infancy, each apparition on the same diving diagonal. Beneath this one, the mountain does the panel’s other work: a bare, faceted mass of grey rock, climbing to a peak directly above the Virgin’s head, with dark trees posted along its ridges like sentinels. It is the same mineral desert in which Joachim kept his exile at the beginning of the chapel — the wilderness returning for the grandson, one register down — and its barrenness measures what the family has left behind.

The surface records the usual tax of time: Mary’s mantle, azurite over the red dress, has shed its blue in flakes across her lap, and the sky holds its losses. But the panel’s construction — the profile ride, the echoing mountain, the chain of walkers, the angel’s vector — survives with a clarity that made this image one of the most loved of the chapel: the Flight as the Middle Ages knew it from the roads of Italy, a woman, a child, an ass, a man with a staff, moving through a hard country on the word of a dream.

### The Massacre of the Innocents (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 5)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-21.jpg" alt="Fresco of the Massacre of the Innocents. At the upper left, the crowned Herod leans from a white two-storey balcony, his right arm extended in command. Below him stand soldiers and courtiers, half-turned away. At the centre, two executioners act: one, hooded in pale yellow, holds a naked infant upside down by the leg; the other, in violet with a dagger at his belt, drives a long, broad-bladed knife while seizing a child a mother in gold-figured blue still clutches to her body. At their feet lies a heap of naked, dead infants. At the right, a dense crowd of mothers presses forward, faces distorted in grief, hands raised, torn or clutching; a man in ochre at the far right turns away with fists to his face. Behind them rises a white octagonal building with two tiers of windows and orange roofs. Deep blue ground with abrasions.">
  <figcaption>The Massacre of the Innocents, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the south wall. From his balcony Herod extends the arm of command; below, two executioners tear the last child from its mother above a heap of small bodies, while the mothers press together in a wall of grief and the king’s men look away. At the right rises an octagonal building — a baptistery-like structure over the children baptised in their own blood.</figcaption>
</figure>

The register’s long preparation closes on its catastrophe. The crown laid on the rock before the Child three panels back receives here its answer from the crown that stayed on: Herod, enthroned in neither hall nor judgment seat but leaning from a balcony — power at its window, commanding at a distance the thing it will not touch — extends his right arm over the square, and the gesture falls through the composition like a blade. Everything below happens inside that arm’s jurisdiction. Giotto has built the panel as a machine of transmission: the order descends from the balcony at the upper left, is executed at the centre, and breaks against the wall of mothers at the right; and the diagonal of command, from the king’s hand to the executioner’s, is the picture’s true weapon. Matthew needs one verse for the deed — Herod sent forth, and slew all the children — and Giotto spends his whole frame on the verse’s grammar: who sends, who slays, who suffers.

The centre is as terrible as anything in the chapel, and as controlled. Two executioners work side by side. One, hooded, his face a professional blank, holds a naked infant upside down by one leg, the small body swinging like carrion; the other, bearded, a dagger at his belt, drives home not a soldier’s sword but a long, broad-bladed knife — a butcher’s implement, and the choice is Giotto’s verdict on the deed: this is not combat but processing — while his left hand drags at a child whose mother has not let go — and will not: her arms are locked around the half-naked body even as it is pulled from her, and the contested infant, stretched between the killer’s grip and the mother’s, is the panel’s unbearable heart. Beneath the struggle lies what the struggle will add to: a heap of small corpses, naked, interlocked, rendered with a bluntness without precedent — not emblems of martyrdom but dead children, weight upon weight, their pallor the brightest passage of the lower picture. Giotto does not avert the viewer’s eye; he organises it.

The mothers are the panel’s second crowd, and its conscience. They press in from the right in the close-packed file Giotto built for choirs and processions — the same compositional instrument that sang at the Presentation of the Virgin and walked in the wedding cortège, here recomposed into a wall of grief: mouths open, brows knotted, hands flung up, clawing, clutching, one woman still holding her living child against the picture’s future. Their faces, crowded profile on profile, run the whole register of lament, from shriek to stupor; and at their far edge a man in ochre has turned his back on the scene entirely, fists pressed to his face — Giotto’s oldest device, the figure seen from behind, converted from an instrument of space into an instrument of the unwatchable. On the opposite side, Herod’s own men manage the sight no better: the courtiers and soldiers beneath the balcony stand half-turned, eyes sliding away, one gathering his mantle as if cold. Between the two crowds, only the executioners look at what is being done.

At the right rises the panel’s enigma: a tall octagonal building, white, two tiers of windows, orange roofs — too grand for a backdrop and too particular for a convention. Its eight-sided form is the canonical shape of the baptistery, and the association can hardly be innocent above this subject: the Church venerated the Innocents as the first martyrs, baptised not in water but in their own blood, and the baptistery-like mass presiding over the slaughter turns the square into the font of that terrible sacrament. Some have sought a specific model in the buildings of Giotto’s world; the type, in any case, would have spoken instantly to a Paduan or Florentine eye. Opposite it, Herod’s balcony — white marble, inlaid panels, elegant — belongs to the same civic vocabulary: the massacre happens not in a legendary Judaea but in a recognisable Italian piazza, between a palace and a baptistery, and the familiarity is the sermon.

The panel completes the arch’s dark arithmetic one wall early. Money changed hands on the chancel arch to buy the Child’s death; here power spends lives wholesale in the same cause and fails — the one child the order was written for is already on the road, a panel back along the register. Herod’s arm commands everything in the frame and misses everything that matters. What tyranny achieves is the heap at the bottom of the picture; what it cannot achieve rides an ass toward Egypt bound to its mother with a striped cloth. The mothers’ grief, meanwhile, is left standing — unconsoled inside the panel, answered only by the whole program around it, which knows where this story, and the slain children’s, ends.

### Christ among the Doctors (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 6)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-22.jpg" alt="Fresco of Christ among the doctors, heavily worn. Inside a deep red hall with an arcaded apse, a half-dome at the centre and garlands hung along the cornice, the young Jesus, haloed, in a red tunic, sits at the midpoint of a long bench, one hand raised with fingers in a counting gesture. To either side sit ten doctors — bearded men in mantles of white, ochre, red and violet, five per side, some gesturing in debate. At the upper left, Mary, haloed, in a blue mantle, stretches both arms toward her son, with the haloed Joseph behind her. The architecture recedes in oblique perspective with arched openings left and right; the painted surface shows extensive abrasion and loss throughout.">
  <figcaption>Christ among the Doctors (The Dispute in the Temple), c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the north wall. In a deep apsed hall, the twelve-year-old Jesus sits at the centre of the assembled doctors, counting his arguments on his fingers, while Mary and Joseph, who have sought him three days, enter at the left with outstretched hands. The panel is the most damaged of the register, its surface worn to the preparatory layers across large areas.</figcaption>
</figure>

A dozen years pass between this panel and the last, and the register does not mark them: the infant of the Flight sits here a boy of twelve, and the viewer learns the interval from Luke, not from the wall. The episode is the only canonical window into the hidden years — the Passover journey, the boy missing, the three days’ search, the finding in the Temple among the doctors, hearing them and asking them questions — and Giotto stages it as the Gospel does, at the moment of discovery: Jesus mid-argument at the centre of the learned assembly, and his parents just arrived at the edge, their relief and reproach still in their outstretched hands. The panel thus carries two scenes in one frame — a disputation and a reunion — and the drama lives in their collision: the boy who belongs, for the first time visibly, to his Father’s business, and the mother and father of his childhood reaching for him.

The architecture is the grandest interior of the cycle so far. Giotto abandons the open-sided pavilions of the infancy for a true hall: a deep room in reds and russets, closed by an arcaded apse with a half-domed recess at its centre, the side walls pierced by arched openings, the cornice hung with green garlands as for a festival, the whole space drawn in the empirical oblique perspective of the register but deeper, more enclosing, more inhabited than anything before it. It is the Temple again — but no longer the Temple of ciborium and parapet where infants are handed over; this is the Temple as institution, the house of the Law’s interpreters, and the boy has penetrated to its centre.

For Jesus sits exactly at the centre, and he is the smallest figure in the room. Giotto refuses every easy glorification — no raised throne, no radiance, no doctors theatrically confounded; the boy holds the midpoint of the bench as one speaker among many, distinguished only by the halo, the red of his tunic, and the gesture: one hand lifted, fingers tallying the points of an argument — the computing gesture of the schools, which every medieval viewer knew from the disputations of their own cities. The authority of the image is entirely in its geometry: the arc of the learned bends around him; the apse crowns his position; the room itself has organised everything toward the child. The doctors respond in the range Luke implies — some lean in, some gesture back, some confer — an academy genuinely engaged, astonished, as the Gospel says, at his understanding and answers, and painted without caricature: Giotto’s Temple scholars are neither fools nor villains, but serious men meeting something beyond their series.

Mary’s entry gives the panel its second current. She comes in at the upper left with both arms thrown forward — the gesture of the Golden Gate, of the Nativity, of every reaching mother in this chapel, here charged with three days of fear — and Joseph follows behind her. Luke gives her the words the posture speaks: son, why hast thou thus dealt with us? behold, thy father and I have sought thee sorrowing. And Luke gives the answer that the composition has already painted: wist ye not that I must be about my Father’s business? The whole theology of the scene is in the fact that her outstretched arms do not reach him — between the mother and the boy sit the doctors, the Law, the institution, the new allegiance — and in the fact that, as Luke concludes and the next panels assume, he went down with them nevertheless, and was subject unto them. It is the cycle’s first, gentle rehearsal of a separation that the Passion registers will complete.

No panel of the chapel has suffered more. The surface is worn across its whole field — the azurite of Mary’s mantle reduced to scattered grains, the doctors’ draperies abraded to the preparatory layers, faces thinned to drawings of themselves — and the causes belong to the wall as much as to time: the panel’s position and the vicissitudes of the building have cost it dearly, and the restoration campaign of 2001–02 could consolidate but not restore what was gone. The damage asks the viewer for a double seeing — the composition, which survives entire, and the surface, which is a ruin — and rewards it: even in this state, the panel remains one of Giotto’s most lucid constructions.

### The Baptism of Christ (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 7)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-23.jpg" alt="Fresco of the Baptism of Christ. At the centre, Christ, haloed with a cross-inscribed nimbus, stands unclothed in the pale green water of the Jordan, which rises in a mound around his body; his hands are drawn before him. From a rock ledge at the right, John the Baptist, haloed, in a pink mantle over a hair tunic, strides forward and extends his right arm to touch Christ’s head. Behind John stand two men: an elderly bearded one in green and red, haloed, and a young one in blue, without a halo. On the left bank wait four haloed angels in white, rose and pale violet, the foremost holding a red garment over their covered hands. Above, a wide burst of white light breaks through the deep blue sky, and within it appears the half-figure of God the Father, in rose, bending downward with a book in one hand and the other extended toward Christ. Rocky cliffs frame both sides; the blue shows heavy abrasions around the light.">
  <figcaption>The Baptism of Christ, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the north wall. In the cleft of the Jordan, between two walls of rock, Christ stands in the water as the Baptist bends from the bank to lay a hand on his head; four angels wait at the left holding his garments, two witnesses — a haloed elder and a young man — watch from the right, and above, in a great burst of light that parts the blue, God the Father himself leans out of heaven over his Son.</figcaption>
</figure>

Eighteen silent years fall between this panel and the last: the boy of the Temple stands here a man in the river, and the register resumes at the moment the Gospels themselves resume — the public manifestation. Giotto compresses the Jordan into a cleft: two walls of pale rock, the descendants of Joachim’s wilderness and the Flight’s grey mountain, part just enough to admit the river, and the river rises — against all hydraulics — in a green mound around the body of Christ, the old Byzantine convention of the heaped Jordan retained not as naivety but as reverence, water gathering itself like a garment around the one it covers. In that cleft the composition locks its axis: Christ at the centre, vertical, frontal, nude; the Baptist’s arm descending from the right; the light descending from directly above. Everything else in the panel stands on the banks of that vertical.

The Baptist performs the scene’s office with the energy of his whole legend. He comes down the rock in full stride, the camel-hair tunic rough at his wrists and hem beneath the pink mantle, and his extended right hand reaches the crown of Christ’s head — the instant of the sacrament itself, the touch that the Gospels surround with protest (I have need to be baptized of thee, and comest thou to me?) and with command (suffer it to be now). Giotto gives the exchange its tension by posture alone: John leans far out over the water, committed and almost overbalanced, while Christ stands perfectly still, hands drawn before his body, receiving — the greater submitting to the less, *ut impleatur omnis iustitia*, and the composition registering the paradox as a diagonal breaking against a vertical. Behind John, on the right bank, wait two witnesses — and Giotto has graded them: the elder, bearded, in green and red, bears a halo; the youth in blue beside him does not. The pair have usually been taken for disciples of the Baptist, the men who will shortly hear him say behold the Lamb of God and cross the river to follow it; the unequal nimbi suggest the painter distinguished their standing — a sanctified elder witness beside a young follower whose vocation has not yet found him. Whoever they are by name, their office in the picture is plain: they are the bank of those who watch and will believe, answering, across the water, the bank of those who serve and already know.

On the left bank, the angels. Four of them, haloed, graded in white and rose and pale violet, press together at the water’s edge in the close file of all this chapel’s choirs — and the foremost hold out, over veiled hands, the red garment of Christ. The detail is liturgy made narrative: the deacons of heaven attend the font holding the clothing of the baptized, exactly as sponsors held the white garment at every font in Christendom; the viewer of 1305 saw at the Jordan the ceremony of his own baptistery, served by a higher clergy. Their bent heads and covered hands give the left bank the hush of a sanctuary — and complete the panel’s quiet architecture: the river as nave, the rocks as walls, the sky about to become a dome.

For the sky opens. A vast burst of white light breaks the blue from above — rays driving downward toward Christ’s head in a cone that the azurite losses have ragged but not destroyed — and within the radiance, at the very top of the panel, appears what almost no painter had dared: God the Father in person, half-figure, bending out of heaven, a book in one hand and the other extended over his Son. This is the Father of the chancel arch — the enthroned sender of Gabriel, there painted on his wooden door at the summit of the chapel — now leaning from his own light at the summit of a single panel; and the voice the Gospels record (this is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased) is given, Giotto’s way, not as a scroll but as a posture: paternity bending down. Between the Father above and the Son in the water descends the light itself — and no dove is legible within it today. Whether the Spirit’s bird was once painted among the rays, where the losses are heaviest, or whether Giotto let the radiance alone carry the third presence, the surface no longer says; the theology does not wait for it, for the Father bends, the light falls, and the Son receives — the Trinity complete in persons, gesture and beam.

With the Baptism, the manifestation begins — and the medieval calendar bound this panel to its neighbours more tightly than the modern eye suspects. The feast of the Epiphany celebrated three miracles as one: the star that led the Magi, the baptism in the Jordan, and the water made wine at Cana — tribus miraculis ornatum diem sanctum colimus, sang the antiphon. On this wall the three manifestations stand in sequence across the registers’ turn: the Adoration with its comet closing the infancy, the Baptism and the Wedding at Cana opening the ministry side by side. The visitor who walked the chapel at Epiphany walked the feast itself. And the nude figure in the heaped green water carries one more weight, which the program will redeem at the far end of the building: the next time Christ stands bared and central and still, between two groups and beneath a sky that attends, will be on the entrance wall, in judgment.

### The Marriage at Cana (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 8)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-24.jpg" alt="Fresco of the wedding at Cana, set in a room with high ochre walls, a projecting carved gallery with pierced parapets and vase finials below a strip of blue sky, and striped red hangings behind the table. At the left end of the white-clothed table sits Christ, haloed with a cross-inscribed nimbus, in red and blue, his hand raised in blessing; beside him a young man in patterned dress and a grey-haired haloed man. A girl in pink and a servant in green stand before the table. At its centre, facing out, sits the bride in a red gold-trimmed gown and dark headband; to her left sits Mary, haloed, in a blue mantle with heavy losses, her hand raised in speech. At the right, a stout man in red with a white cap stands drinking from a dark cup, his belly swelling the robe. Behind him a young servant and a serving-girl with braided hair pour and attend. In the right foreground, six large ribbed white stone jars stand on and beside a wooden bench.">
  <figcaption>The Marriage at Cana, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the north wall. In a festively hung chamber, the wedding feast of Cana at the instant after the first miracle: the servants have filled the great stone jars ranged in the foreground, a serving-girl pours, and the master of the feast — Giotto’s unforgettable stout steward — tilts the cup to his lips and tastes the water become wine. Christ blesses from the head of the table; Mary, who set the miracle in motion, sits opposite among the household.</figcaption>
</figure>

The ministry’s first act is a kindness at a party, and Giotto paints it with the domestic relish the subject deserves. The room is the most festive interior of the chapel: high ochre walls banded with ornament, striped hangings of red and white drawn around the table like the lists of a tournament, and above, open to a strip of blue, a projecting wooden gallery with pierced quatrefoil parapets and vases flourishing at its corners — the upper loggia from which, in any prosperous household of Giotto’s Italy, a wedding below would be watched. The table runs the room’s width under its white cloth, set with the knives, loaves and dark cups of an actual meal; the guests sit along its far side in the old convention that leaves the near side open — not yet the convention of a Last Supper, simply the practice of service — and in the right foreground, on and beside a wooden bench, stand the true protagonists of the plot: the great stone jars.

John’s account is the register’s most precisely staged narrative, and Giotto has chosen his instant with a dramatist’s cunning: not the command, not the pouring, but the tasting. The six waterpots of stone have been filled to the brim; the drawn water has been borne to the governor of the feast; and the governor — Giotto’s magnificent steward, stout in his red robe, white-capped, his belly carrying the authority of his office — stands tilting the dark cup to his lips at the panel’s right edge, eyes inward, in the full concentration of a professional palate. He is the hinge of the miracle and its first witness, and he does not know it: the ruler of the feast tasted the water that was made wine, and knew not whence it was. The viewer knows; the servants pouring behind him know, the servants which drew the water knew; and the comedy of the Fourth Gospel — knowledge upside down, the expert ignorant and the waiters informed — stands on the wall intact. In a moment he will call the bridegroom and pronounce the verdict the Church never tired of repeating: thou hast kept the good wine until now.

The miracle’s cause sits at the table, and it is not the bridegroom. Mary, beside the bride, raises her hand in the gesture of speech — the mother who noticed the shortage (they have no wine), absorbed the strange rebuff (woman, what have I to do with thee? mine hour is not yet come), and gave the servants the instruction that is her last recorded sentence and the whole of her theology: whatsoever he saith unto you, do it. Giotto places her in the midst of the household, between the bride and the steward, exactly where the Gospel’s logistics put her; and Christ, at the table’s other end, blesses — the raised hand that, across the room’s whole width, answers his mother’s raised hand: the request and the consent holding the panel between them like the two ends of the cloth. Beside Christ sits a young man in rich patterned dress, by long tradition the bridegroom himself — a tradition that went further and named him John, the beloved disciple called from his own wedding; and next to him a grey-haired haloed guest extends the sacred company. The bride, meanwhile, sits at the exact centre, frontal, crowned with her dark headband, splendid and slightly stunned — the one person at her own feast with nothing to do but be married.

The jars deserve their foreground. Giotto gives them the number and the scale John gives them — six, containing two or three firkins apiece, vessels of real capacity, waist-high, ribbed, heavy enough that the serving-girl must tip one with both arms to pour — and ranges them across the picture’s front like a still-life of the sacrament to come: stone vessels of water become wine, set before an altar-shaped table, in a chapel where wine became something greater at every Mass. Baptism and Cana, side by side, show water sanctified in one panel and transformed in the next. The medieval reader went one step further down the same road — the wedding as figure of the union of Christ and Church, the good wine kept until the last as the new covenant after the old — and the panel lets every level stand, because Giotto has anchored them all in the one thing he never sacrifices: a particular room, on a particular afternoon, where the wine ran out and did not.

Time has spent itself unevenly here. Mary’s mantle has shed its azurite in the familiar flakes, and scattered losses fleck the table company; but the room itself, its hangings and gallery, the steward, the jars, survive with a freshness that keeps the panel among the most legible of the register. And its humour survives with it — for the stout taster of Cana is one of the first full-blooded comic figures of Italian painting, observed without contempt, a man doing his job well at the exact moment his job exceeds him. The register that opened on a woman giving birth on bare rock here pauses, before Lazarus and Jerusalem and everything that follows, for the Gospel’s one feast; Giotto, who will paint the Passion’s suppers soon enough, lets this table be happy.

### The Raising of Lazarus (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 9)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-25.jpg" alt="Fresco of the raising of Lazarus. At the left stands Christ, haloed with a cross-inscribed nimbus, in a red mantle over blue with heavy losses, his right arm extended across the scene toward Lazarus; behind him press four haloed apostles. At the lower centre, two haloed women — Mary and Martha of Bethany — kneel and fall prostrate at Christ’s feet, one stretching her hands to the ground. At the right, before a dark tomb opening cut in the rock, Lazarus stands upright, wholly wrapped in white grave-bandages, his face grey-green with half-open eyes; a man in ochre grips him from one side, and a haloed figure veiled in rose holds the veil across nose and mouth; another veiled figure stands behind. Between Christ and Lazarus a crowd of men in green, red and blue throw up their hands. In the foreground, a man in blue, bent double and seen from behind, and a boy at the far right carry away the great stone slab. A steep rock rises behind the tomb, with two trees on its summit. Deep blue ground with abrasions.">
  <figcaption>The Raising of Lazarus, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the north wall. Before the opened rock tomb, Christ’s raised hand crosses the panel toward the dead man: Lazarus stands bound in his grave-clothes, grey-faced, his eyes just open, steadied by those beside him, while a bystander draws a veil against the stench of the fourth day. Mary and Martha lie prostrate at Christ’s feet, the crowd breaks into amazement, and two labourers carry off the stone.</figcaption>
</figure>

The ministry’s last sign, and the Passion’s trigger: John places the raising of Lazarus at the turning of his Gospel — from that day forth they took counsel together for to put him to death — and the register places it accordingly, the panel from which the wall’s road runs straight to Jerusalem. Giotto builds it as a single current crossing a gap. At the left, Christ: upright, wrapped in red over blue, his arm extended at full length across the picture — and between his hand and the bound man at the right lies open air, charged like the void of the chancel arch, through which nothing passes but the words. Lazare, veni foras: the Gospel’s shortest command, and the panel’s whole engine.

And the dead man is dead. Giotto’s Lazarus is the panel’s most fearless passage: upright before the black mouth of the tomb, wound head to foot in the criss-crossed bandages of burial, his face a grey-green that no living figure in the chapel wears, his eyes barely unsealed, his body still a bound column that others must steady — a man in ochre grips him from the side, for the summoned cannot yet stand alone. He is at the exact seam between the two states, obedient to the voice but not yet returned to himself, and the fourth day declares itself around him: beside the tomb, a figure veiled in rose holds the cloth across nose and mouth — and the figure is haloed. Giotto has given the scene’s crudest gesture to one of its saints: Martha’s objection made flesh, Domine, iam foetet, quadriduanus est enim, performed not by a squeamish bystander but by a holy witness, so that the proof of death is certified by sanctity itself. A second muffled figure stands behind. Giotto spares nothing of the miracle’s physical truth: resurrection, in this panel, smells of the grave it defeats, and the stench is part of the proof.

At Christ’s feet, the sisters. Mary and Martha of Bethany have gone down together — one kneeling, one stretched almost flat, her hands laid on the bare ground, the white mantle pooling around her prostration — in the double collapse of grief and faith that John distributes between them: each had met him with the same reproach and creed, Lord, if thou hadst been here, my brother had not died. Their lowness is the composition’s foundation: the panel’s great diagonal runs from their prostrate bodies up through Christ’s extended arm to the standing dead man, so that the eye climbs from the sisters’ despair, along the command, to its answer — grief, word, and resurrection laid on a single rising line. Behind and between, the crowd does what crowds do at the unthinkable: hands fly up, heads crane, profiles stack in the dense file of all this chapel’s choruses, here struck into a chorus of alarm — and among them some shade their eyes or draw back, the amazement already shadowed, for John’s crowd divides at this miracle into believers and informers.

The labour of the scene is given its own dignity. In the foreground, a man in blue bends double over the great veined slab of the tomb, seen entirely from behind, his back a hill of effort; a boy at the far right takes the other end. Take ye away the stone is a command in the Gospel too, and Giotto honours it with the full weight of the thing commanded: the stone is heavy, the carrying is work, and the miracle rests — as this painter’s miracles always do — on a ground of documented physical fact. The bent porter belongs to the same family as the water-carriers and basket-bearers of the earlier registers, the anonymous labour that attends every wonder of the cycle; his faceless effort in the picture’s front is the last ordinary thing the eye crosses before the extraordinary.

The setting is the cycle’s bare mountain one more time — the rock of Joachim’s exile and the Flight’s desert, here split by the dark rectangle of the tomb, with two trees on the summit keeping their watch — and the recurrence has an arc: the wilderness that opened the chapel as the landscape of barrenness stands here as the landscape of death, and is defeated on its own ground. For Giotto’s first viewers the panel carried a further, domestic resonance: the raising of Lazarus was the Gospel’s great pledge of the resurrection of the body, the scene painted in cemeteries and funerary chapels across Christendom — and this chapel, built over its founder’s intended tomb, with the Last Judgment waiting on the entrance wall, makes the pledge structural. One register below, the same voice will cry from the same kind of rock-cut tomb — and no one will need to carry the stone.

### The Entry into Jerusalem (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 10)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-26.jpg" alt="Fresco of the entry into Jerusalem. At the left, Christ, haloed with a cross-inscribed nimbus, in a red tunic under a pale gold-figured mantle with blue losses, rides a grey ass in profile to the right, his hand raised in blessing. Behind him follow the apostles, a dense file of haloed bearded men in red, green and patterned white. Ahead, from the right, a crowd streams out of a white city gate flanked by two tall towers: men and women pressing forward, some raising hands, some holding branches. In the lower right foreground, a kneeling man in rose spreads a cloak before the ass; above him a man in green and white pulls his garment over his head. In two trees against the blue, two boys climb and cut leafy branches. Deep blue ground with heavy abrasions, especially at the centre.">
  <figcaption>The Entry into Jerusalem, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the north wall. Christ rides toward the gate of Jerusalem in blessing, the apostles in file behind him; the crowd pours from the city to meet him, one man spreading his cloak beneath the ass’s hooves and another caught mid-gesture pulling his garment over his head, while two boys climb the trees to cut branches. The white towers of the city wait at the right; between triumph and gate, the register’s penultimate panel.</figcaption>
</figure>

The ministry ends at a gate. The Entry is the register’s penultimate panel, and Giotto stages it as the whole wall’s destination: every scene since the Baptism has moved the story toward Jerusalem, and here the city itself finally stands at the right edge — white, towered, its gate open — while Christ advances toward it from the left at the pace of an ass. The composition is two crowds and a passage: the apostles behind, pressed into the familiar file of haloed profiles; the citizens before, pouring out of the gate in a counter-wave; and between them, on the open ground that Giotto always keeps for what matters, the rider — in strict profile like every decisive figure of this cycle, hand lifted in blessing, moving at the unforced, unstoppable walking pace that has carried the register since the Flight into Egypt. The two processions of the chapel meet here: the one that followed him out of Galilee and the one that will, within a week, follow him to Calvary.

The Gospel’s choreography is honoured item by item, and each item turned into observed life. A very great multitude spread their garments in the way: Giotto’s garment-spreader kneels in the right foreground, laying his cloak under the hooves with the care of a man handling his best possession — and above him occurs the panel’s most famous invention, a bystander caught at the exact midpoint of pulling his tunic over his head, face vanished in the cloth, arms crossed and struggling, a figure of pure transitory awkwardness that no painter had thought worth a wall before. The crowd’s homage, in Giotto, costs effort and looks like it: clothing resists, undressing is graceless, and the gracelessness is the devotion. Others cut down branches from the trees: two boys have climbed into the two trees that stand against the blue, small agile bodies among the foliage, cutting and passing down the green — and the trees themselves are no stage palms but the leafy, ragged trees of the cycle’s own hills, the branches of a real countryside pressed into a liturgy improvised on the road.

Christ receives the triumph as he received the Jordan: in stillness. He neither waves nor exults; the blessing hand is raised at the same calm angle as in Cana, the profile set toward the gate with the composed interiority that his mother wore through her whole register — and the ass beneath him, grey, patient, ears forward, carries its rider with the unhurried gait that is the panel’s true tempo. The humility of the mount was the point of the prophecy — behold, thy King cometh unto thee, meek, and sitting upon an ass — and Giotto sharpens it by context: the register’s other crowned arrivals rode camels and sat balconies; the King enters his city on the animal that carried his mother into Egypt. The beast of refuge has become the beast of kingship, the cycle’s humblest actor given the last entrance.

The crowd from the gate is a gallery of reception. Faces press over faces — bearded elders, women with bound hair, the young — some hands raised in acclaim, some holding the cut branches, the foremost figures bending already toward homage; and their wave breaks against the open ground around the rider without crossing it. Giotto paints the acclamation and, inside it, its fragility: these are the same massed profiles, the same civic crowd, that his viewers will meet again on the lower register with different cries, and the painter of the two moments knew they were the same people. Between the hosanna and the crucifige stands only the width of a wall. The white city above them — towers, battlements, the gate’s deep arch — is drawn in the civic vocabulary of the cycle’s Italian architecture: Jerusalem as a commune, entered the way a bishop or a victor entered Padua, through a real gate between real towers.

The panel has paid heavily across its centre: the blue has fallen in broad continents between Christ and the crowd, Christ’s gold-figured mantle has shed much of its surface, and the ass’s body carries the wear of the wall. But the design’s two waves and their meeting survive — and so does the panel’s position in the chapel’s larger liturgy. Padua kept Palm Sunday as every commune did, with its procession and its branches; the panel gave the rite its monumental mirror, and the chapel’s visitors, entering with their own palms in hand, met at this wall their own procession painted. With the gate at its edge, the register nears its close; what the city does with its guest begins one register down — directly below this panel’s wall runs the road to Calvary — and the viewer’s descent from the middle register to the lower repeats, in the architecture of the chapel itself, the week that runs from the branches to the cross.

### The Expulsion of the Money-changers from the Temple (Scenes from the Life of Christ, 11)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-27.jpg" alt="Fresco of the expulsion of the merchants from the Temple. At the centre, Christ, haloed with a cross-inscribed nimbus, in a red tunic and pale mantle, raises his right fist with a scourge of cords above his head; his left hand grasps his mantle. Before him a trader in violet-grey recoils with raised hand, and a second man in blue and red draws back; at the far right, two grey-bearded priests in gold-trimmed robes confer with others behind them. At the left stand the apostles, haloed, led by two prominent elders; a small child presses into the mantle of one, holding a dove. A red trestle table lies overturned at the centre; at the lower left two oxen move away, at the right a ram stands before an empty wooden cage on the ground. Behind rises the white Temple façade: three gabled porches with rose medallions, marble columns, and sculpted animals — lions and horses — standing on the gable peaks; at the right a carved pulpit-like balcony against the blue.">
  <figcaption>The Expulsion of the Money-changers from the Temple, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), middle register of the north wall, last panel of the register. Before the porticoed façade of the Temple, Christ raises the scourge of cords: a table lies overturned, the traders recoil, the freed animals scatter, and a child shelters inside an apostle’s mantle clutching a dove, while two priests confer at the right. In the chapel of a banker’s son, the panel on which money is driven out of the holy place.</figcaption>
</figure>

The register’s last word is an act of violence — the only one Christ performs in the whole chapel — and Giotto places it where the program’s arithmetic demanded: in the house of God, against the tables of money. The composition is built on the raised arm. Christ stands at the centre, and for once the hand that has blessed at Cana, summoned at Bethany and saluted at the gate closes into a fist: above his head swings the scourge of small cords that John’s account puts into it, and the whole scene radiates from the arc of the coming blow — the traders recoiling to the right, the overturned trestle at his feet, the animals breaking loose below, the apostles massed in alarm at the left. Giotto, the master of arrested gesture, does not let the blow land: the fist is raised, the recoil has begun, and the panel hangs — like the Prayer of the Suitors, its distant twin — in the charged second before the release.

The traders take the storm each in his own key. The foremost, in violet-grey, twists away with a hand flung up — the merchant’s forearm against the prophet’s fist; behind him a second in blue and red retreats sidelong; and at the far right, out of the blow’s reach, two magnificent grey-bearded priests in gold-trimmed robes confer, heads together, eyes on the disturber — the institution already in counsel, as it was in the Pact of Judas on the chancel arch, and as it will be, with a verdict, on the register below. Giotto distributes the scene’s politics with his usual precision: the small men suffer the scourge, the great men take notes. Between the groups the trestle table lies capsized at Christ’s feet, legs in the air — the furniture of exchange, the cousin of every counting-bench in Padua, the first table of the chapel to be overturned rather than laid.

The mercy of the panel is at its edges. At the left, a small child has pressed himself into the mantle of one of the apostles, half hidden in the drapery, and from the folds he holds out a dove — one of the birds being sold for sacrifice, snatched up and saved. The frightened child sheltering in a grown man’s cloak with a rescued bird in his hands is the kind of invention no text supplied and no painter before this one would have spent a figure on: inside the one scene of divine anger, a pocket of pure protection. Below, the liberated livestock make their exits — two oxen shouldering away at the left, a ram at the right beside the empty wooden cage from which the doves have gone — the sacrificial economy of the Temple dispersing on its own four feet, driven out alive from the house where it was priced to die.

The Temple itself is the most splendid architecture of the register: a white façade of three gabled porches on marble columns, rose medallions in the tympana — and on the gable peaks, sculpted beasts, lions and horses standing against the blue. The horses have long invited a particular thought: free-standing bronze-like steeds crowning a sacred façade were, for any north Italian of 1305, one building above all — San Marco in Venice, where the four horses of Constantinople had stood on the loggia for a century — and Giotto’s Temple, whatever its intended geography, borrows the authority of the lagoon’s great shrine. Jerusalem, throughout this cycle, is built of the viewer’s own world: a commune’s gate, a baptistery’s octagon, and now a façade that remembers Venice. At the right, a carved balcony on brackets — a pulpit or singing-gallery — closes the precinct against the blue.

For this chapel’s first audience, the panel was the program looking at its patron. A sanctuary purged of money, painted with a banker’s fortune, in a building whose own legitimacy turned on the question of what wealth may do in holy places: the Cleansing of the Temple was the one Gospel scene Enrico Scrovegni could not commission innocently, and its placement — last of the register, directly in the sequence that runs from the Pact of Judas on the arch to the bargains of the Passion below — braids it into the cycle’s long meditation on coin. The chapel does not flinch: the scourge hangs over the money-tables on its own wall, and the donor kneels on the west wall offering the building anyway — wealth confessing, wealth hoping. Between those two images the whole Scrovegni wager is strung; and the middle register, which opened with a child born on bare rock, closes with the grown man clearing his Father’s house for the week that will cost him everything.

## The Scrovegni Chapel: The Passion

The lowest register of the Scrovegni Chapel tells the end of the story at the height of the living. Eleven fields run from the Last Supper to Pentecost — five on the south wall, from the upper room to the crowning with thorns, six on the north, from the road to Calvary to the descent of the fire — and they occupy the band nearest the floor, where no spectator can look at them without standing among them. The registers above told of promises: a child granted to the barren, a virgin given to the Temple, a kingdom announced. This one tells what the promises cost, and it begins, with terrible aptness for this chapel, with a payment.

For the Passion register does not start in the upper room. Its true opening stands on the chancel arch itself, where Judas receives his thirty pieces of silver under the hands of the priests — money counted out at the very threshold of the sanctuary, in the chapel a banker built to answer for money. From that pact the register descends into the long night: a supper where the betrayer sits unmarked among the marked; a kiss that gathers the whole tumult of the arrest into two profiles that never quite touch. Then the painted darkness itself: the first night scene of the cycle, lit from a torch inside the picture, as the prisoner passes from judge to judge toward the hill.

On the north wall the register slows to the pace of grief. Christ carries his cross alone, as John alone tells it; the Crucifixion gathers heaven and earth around one vertical, from the skull of Adam to the titulus that conflates two Gospels into a single charge; and the Lamentation lets all the cycle’s weight come to rest at the foot of a bare ridge, where the arms that first received the newborn receive the body. Then the great reversal, scene by scene: an empty pink tomb and a banner that answers the dark standard of Calvary; a Christ whose hands leave the top of the picture as if the fresco itself could not hold him; and a white room where fire falls in straight red lines on twelve seated men. Even the numbers keep the doctrine — eleven kneel on Olivet, twelve sit at Pentecost, and the unpainted casting of the lots falls silently between two frames.

The register ends where the chapel ends. Pentecost, the birth of the Church, is the last field of the north wall, beside the chancel arch, and the visitor who turns from it faces, down the length of the nave, the Last Judgment over the door — the return that the two white messengers of the Ascension promised in so many words. The whole wall has been walking toward that turn. What began on the arch with silver counted into one man’s hand finishes at the door where every hand is counted; and between the two, in eleven fields at eye level, hangs the price of everything the chapel was built to buy.

### The Last Supper (Scenes from the Passion of Christ, 1)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-28.jpg" alt="Fresco of the Last Supper. Inside an open timber loggia with slender corner colonnettes, pierced Gothic trim, inlaid panels and a red-tiled roof bearing two pale birds and small finials, the twelve apostles sit around a long table with a white cloth, bread and dishes. At the far left corner sits Christ, haloed in gold with a cross; the young John leans on his breast with closed eyes. Along the far side sit six apostles with gold-rimmed dark haloes — among them the grey-haired Peter beside John — conversing in low gestures. On the near bench, seen from behind, sit five more apostles in yellow-white, blue-green, gold-patterned white, rose and grey-green mantles, their haloes rendered as dark discs in front of their hidden faces. The rear wall is pale, with two small windows and faint roundels; deep blue sky above the roof, with losses. A vertical repaired crack runs through the right side of the scene.">
  <figcaption>The Last Supper, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), lower register of the south wall, first panel of the Passion cycle. In an open loggia crowned by two birds on the roof, the twelve sit at table in the hush after the terrible sentence — one of you shall betray me: John has sunk onto Christ’s breast, the apostles murmur and search one another, and the five seated with their backs to us wear their haloes dark, seen from behind like everything else in the room. The same chamber will host the Washing of the Feet in the next panel.</figcaption>
</figure>

The lower register opens on a room, and the room is a trap of quietness. No architecture of the cycle is more graceful than this loggia — slender colonnettes at the corners, carpenter’s Gothic tracery hung from the eaves, inlaid rosettes, a red-tiled roof with delicate finials, and on the ridge two pale birds, perched as if the evening were any evening — and inside the grace, the Passion has already begun. Giotto chooses for his Last Supper neither the institution of the sacrament nor the dramatic unmasking, but the moment John’s Gospel renders as pure suspension: the words one of you shall betray me have just been said, the beloved disciple has sunk onto Christ’s breast, and around the table the twelve have turned to one another — the disciples looked one on another, doubting of whom he spake. Nothing happens; everything has happened. The two birds on the roof keep the world’s innocence overhead while, below them, the company of three years learns that it contains its own ruin.

The table seats the drama in a ring, and the ring is the panel’s revolution. Five apostles sit on the near bench with their backs to us — the device Giotto has used since Joachim’s shepherds, here multiplied into a wall of shoulders: yellow-white, blue-green, a splendid gold-figured mantle at the centre, rose, grey-green — and their haloes, which must logically stand between their hidden faces and the viewer, are painted accordingly: dark translucent discs, gold-rimmed, seen from behind. It is a detail of hallucinatory consistency. The halo, which every painter before had treated as a flat badge pinned to the picture surface, is here an object in space, with a front and a back, obeying the room like the benches and the bread; sanctity itself has been given a position. The viewer is seated, by those turned backs, at the table’s near side — inside the company, in the only place left open, which is to say in the place of the question: Lord, is it I?

Christ does not preside; he occupies the far left corner, almost at the margin of his own supper, and the displacement is the composition’s quiet mastery. From the corner, his cross-haloed profile commands the whole ring diagonally — every face along the far side is strung on the line of sight that begins with him — and against his breast lies John, eyes closed, undone, the one apostle excused from the terrible arithmetic because he has chosen nearness over inquiry. Beside them Peter’s grey head leans in, his face already gathering the protest and the grief of the night ahead. Down the far side the remaining faces take the sentence each in his own register — murmured conference, inward stares, a hand moving on the cloth — Giotto’s full keyboard of contained alarm, played pianissimo: no one rises, no one points, and the betrayer is not marked. Somewhere in the ring sits Judas, indistinguishable — as he was at the table itself — and the panel keeps the Gospel’s own suspense: the viewer, like the eleven, must look from face to face and fail to know.

The room is laid for a meal, not a monument: bread on the white cloth, dishes within reach, the benches plain, the walls behind pale and almost bare, with their two small windows of blue and faint traced roundels. After the crowded skies and streaming crowds of the middle register, the Passion opens in an interior silence that the architecture itself enforces — the loggia’s openwork frames the company like a shrine, and the shrine will serve again: in the next panel, the Washing of the Feet, the identical room returns, bench for bench and rosette for rosette, the supper cleared and the basin brought. It is the cycle’s last great architectural recurrence, and its most intimate: one upper room for the two acts of the one night, so that the viewer, moving along the wall, does not change place — only the hour deepens.

For the chapel’s first users, the panel stood in the closest possible conversation with the building’s purpose: an altar stood a few metres away, and the supper painted at the head of the Passion register is the institution of everything the chapel did daily — though Giotto, characteristically, paints not the liturgical formula but the human night around it, and lets the Mass below supply what the picture withholds. Time has drawn one of its own lines through the scene: a long vertical crack, repaired, runs down the right side of the panel through benches and mantles, and scattered losses fleck the blue and the drapery; the room’s stillness survives both. The register that now begins will not know another quiet hour: from this table the wall proceeds to the basin, the kiss, the tribunal, the column — and the two birds on the roof of the first panel are the last creatures in the cycle to be at peace.

### The Washing of the Feet (Scenes from the Passion of Christ, 2)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-29.jpg" alt="Fresco of the washing of the feet, set in the same open loggia as the Last Supper: white pierced trim, inlaid panels, red-tiled roof with two pale birds. The twelve apostles, their haloes dark blue discs, sit on an orange bench around the room or stand. At the centre, Christ, haloed in gold with a cross, kneels in profile to the right in a red tunic, a towel over his shoulder and arm, one hand open and raised, the other at the raised foot of Peter — grey-haired, in an ochre mantle over a blue-spotted robe, seated, who bows his head toward the washing. A dark basin stands on the floor before them. At the left, a seated elder in green and red bends to untie his sandal; beside him a standing young apostle in rose holds a tall red pitcher. At the right, apostles wait, seated and standing behind Peter; a pair of sandals lies on the floor at the lower right. Pale walls with small windows behind; blue sky above the roof with losses.">
  <figcaption>The Washing of the Feet, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), lower register of the south wall. In the same loggia where the supper was eaten, Christ kneels with the towel at his waist and washes Peter’s feet; the old apostle, submitting under protest, bows his head over the basin — then wash my hands and my head also. An apostle at the left unties his sandals, another stands ready with the pitcher, and on the roof the two birds of the supper keep their place.</figcaption>
</figure>

The room has not changed; the hour has. Bench for bench, rosette for rosette, bird for bird, this is the loggia of the Last Supper, but the table is gone, and in the space it occupied kneels the host. The substitution is the panel’s whole theology, delivered as furniture: where the meal stood, service now stands; the supper’s centre has gone down onto the floor of its own house. John, the only evangelist to record the scene, hangs it on precisely this descent — he riseth from supper, and laid aside his garments; and took a towel, and girded himself — and Giotto paints the verse’s equipment with liturgical exactness: the towel at Christ’s waist, the dark basin on the pavement, the water poured and waiting. The master of the house, in his own loggia, dressed as his own servant.

The exchange at the centre is the Gospel’s argument compressed into two gestures. Christ kneels in profile, both hands at Peter’s lifted foot, with the grave absorption of a man doing a servant’s job seriously; and Peter — grey, monumental, wrapped in ochre over blue — bows above him in the posture of surrender. Thou shalt never wash my feet has already been said and already answered — if I wash thee not, thou hast no part with me — and the bowed head is the old man’s surrender, soon to overshoot into excess: Lord, not my feet only, but also my hands and my head. Giotto, who gave Joachim’s grief a bowed back and Anna’s joy two open arms, gives Peter’s love its signature impetuosity: corrected once, he demands the whole bath. The painter trusts the viewer to hear the sentence in the gesture — and seven centuries of viewers have.

Around the centre, the room prepares and waits. At the left, an elder in green and red bends over his own foot, untying the sandal — obedience already in motion down the bench, each man readying himself for his turn — and beside him stands a young apostle holding the tall red pitcher, the water-bearer of the night, upright as an acolyte. At the right, the seated apostles watch the exchange, others stand behind. And on the floor at the lower right lies a pair of sandals, empty, set aside — the panel’s quietest still-life, bare feet declared by abandoned shoes. The company’s dark-rimmed haloes, which the supper showed from the back, here turn with their owners’ heads; the room’s two small windows hold their blue; and on the ridge of the roof the two birds of the Last Supper keep their perch — the same two, recognisably, carried over with the loggia itself, as if between the supper and the basin no time had passed on the roof at all.

John sets the scene’s weight in its preface, and the fresco carries it: having loved his own which were in the world, he loved them unto the end — and, in the same breath, the devil having now put into the heart of Judas Iscariot to betray him. The feet Christ washes include the betrayer’s; the panel, like the supper before it, declines to mark him, and the charity is thereby painted exact: service rendered to the whole bench, worthiness unexamined.
