For the chapel’s audience the scene had a living double — the Mandatum of Holy Thursday, when bishops and princes knelt yearly with basin and towel — and the fresco returns the rite to its origin, in the room where the example was set: I have given you an example, that ye should do as I have done to you. In a chapel whose program weighs charity against coin on every wall, this panel is charity’s definition — and the next, a few centimetres on, is the coin’s: the hands washed here will be bound in the Kiss, and the night’s two embraces stand side by side on the wall.

### The Kiss of Judas (Scenes from the Passion of Christ, 3)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-30.jpg" alt="Fresco of the kiss of Judas, a dense night crowd filling the panel. At the centre, Christ, haloed in gold with a cross, in blue and red, stands in profile facing right; Judas, in a voluminous yellow mantle that wraps around Christ’s body, embraces him and lifts his face toward Christ’s, their eyes locked, lips not touching. Around them presses an armed crowd: helmeted soldiers, men in red capes, a soldier in gold-figured armour; above their heads a radiating thicket of staves, axes, halberds and two blazing torches against the deep blue sky, with a white horn blown at the centre right. At the far left, the haloed Peter, cut by the frame, drives a knife toward the ear of a man at the panel’s edge; beside him a hooded figure in grey-violet seen from behind seizes the mantle of a figure slipping away. At the right, a bearded priest in a rose gold-bordered mantle extends a pointing arm toward the central pair. The crowd’s rear ranks are damaged, heads abraded to dark silhouettes.">
  <figcaption>The Kiss of Judas (The Arrest of Christ), c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), lower register of the south wall. At the centre of the night’s tumult, two faces: Judas, wrapping Christ in the sweep of his yellow mantle, lifts his mouth toward the master who meets his eyes and does not move. Around them the mob’s weapons and torches burst against the sky, a horn brays, Peter’s knife finds the servant’s ear at the left edge, a hooded figure seizes a fleeing cloak, and at the right a priest’s pointing finger directs the whole machine. The dark twin of the Golden Gate: the cycle’s second kiss.</figcaption>
</figure>

Every road in the chapel has led to this panel, and Giotto declares it by geometry: the whole night converges on two faces. The composition is a vortex — thirty figures, a forest of weapons, torches, a braying horn — and at its dead centre, stillness: Christ and Judas, profile against profile, so close that the traitor’s upturned face and the master’s downturned one seal the space between them, and nothing in the surrounding chaos can enter. The viewer who has walked the chapel from the beginning has seen this composition before, and was meant to. On the south wall’s upper register, two profiles closed the same way over a kiss at the Golden Gate — the embrace of Joachim and Anna from which the whole history flowed. This is its dark twin, built on the same armature: there, two who loved truly, fused into one silhouette; here, the parody — one who loves and one who sells, locked in the same figure. For Giotto stops the kiss: Judas’s lips do not reach. The panel hangs forever in the instant before the sign is given, and the suspension is the judgment — the betrayal eternally in the act of being chosen.

The yellow mantle is the scene’s second protagonist. It sweeps up from Judas’s back and around Christ’s body in one engulfing arc, so that the master is half swallowed in the traitor’s colour — the yellow of the Pact, the yellow that walked beside the demon on the chancel arch, here grown to a wave; the embrace as envelopment, possession’s last disguise. And above the mantle, the faces. Giotto gives the confrontation entirely to the eyes: Christ’s gaze comes down steady, open, terribly unsurprised — the look of one who has known since the supper — and Judas’s goes up into it and is held there, his low forehead and pursed mouth caught between resolve and the beginning of something that is not yet remorse. No painter had ever hung so much on a single exchanged look; the two profiles, eye to eye across a hand’s breadth, are among the sovereign inventions of European painting.

The night, meanwhile, is magnificently loud. Over the heads of the mob Giotto raises a radiating burst of implements — staves, axes, halberds, lanterns on poles, two torches flaring red against the blue — the weapons fanned out like the spokes of a broken wheel with the kiss at its hub; and into the din a man at the centre right blows a white horn, cheeks filled, the arrest announced like a hunt. The crowd itself is all species of the night: helmeted soldiery, red-caped servants of the priests, a centurion in gold-figured armour, faces young and old pressed rank on rank — the rear ones now abraded to dark silhouettes, a damage that reads, almost mercifully, as the mob dissolving into its own darkness. The Gospels’ great multitude with swords and staves has never been more literally painted, nor more precisely organised.

At the edges, the night’s two smaller dramas. At the far left, violence answers violence: Peter himself — the haloed head at the panel’s very rim — drives his knife toward the ear of Malchus, the servant of the high priest, both striker and struck half-amputated by the frame, the apostles’ one blow of the Passion delivered at the picture’s edge as if the painting itself declined to dwell on it; beside them a hooded figure in grey-violet, seen wholly from behind, grips the trailing mantle of someone slipping out of the crowd — the anonymous arrester and the anonymous escaper, in whom readers of Mark have always recognised the young man who fled, leaving his garment in their hands. And at the right, the panel’s coldest figure: a bearded priest in a rose mantle bordered with gold, who does not touch anyone — he points. The extended arm, elegant and dry, directs the whole apparatus toward the pair at the centre: the institution’s gesture, command at the safe distance of a finger, the exact descendant of Herod’s arm on the balcony of the Massacre. Between Judas who must touch and the priest who need not, the panel maps the whole anatomy of the deed: the paid instrument embraces; the purchaser indicates.

The correspondence runs deeper than the two kisses. The Golden Gate stood at the end of a story that began with an offering rejected; the Arrest stands at the head of the sequence in which the true offering is accepted at last — and between the two embraces, high on the chancel arch, money changed hands to make the second one possible. The viewer standing back sees the system whole: kiss answers kiss across the chapel, and coin connects them. From here the register descends without pause — tribunal, column, cross; the crowd that bursts around this embrace will not disperse until the Lamentation. But the centre of the night, and of the whole wall, remains what Giotto made it: two human faces, closer than any others in the chapel, one asking with its silence the question the Gospel gives it words for — friend, wherefore art thou come?

### Christ before Caiaphas (Scenes from the Passion of Christ, 4)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-31.jpg" alt="Fresco of Christ before the high priest, set in a night interior: a chamber with a dark coffered wooden ceiling on carved brackets, rose-pink walls with arched doors shut, and a canopied judgment seat at the right. At the centre-left stands Christ, haloed in gold with a cross, in a red tunic and a blue gold-figured mantle with losses, his hands bound before him, his head turned back over his shoulder toward a man at the left whose arm is raised to strike. Around him press guards: a soldier in a red cape over gold-fringed armour, a helmeted man, an elder in blue and gold at the far left with raised hand, a soldier in green-patterned armour gripping Christ’s mantle, and a young man in brown gesturing toward the throne. On the stepped, canopied seat at the right sit two bearded judges: one in red, gesturing toward Christ; beside him Caiaphas in green, tearing his robe open at the chest with both hands. On the upper wall at the centre, the dark plume of a torch flame, its pigment altered. Blue band of night sky above the chamber.">
  <figcaption>Christ before Caiaphas, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), lower register of the south wall. The night tribunal: in a torch-lit chamber, Christ stands bound among the guards as a servant’s arm rises to strike him, and Caiaphas, enthroned at the right, tears his robe at the blasphemy — his verdict performed on his own clothing. The panel is among the first night interiors of Western painting, its single torch still legible on the wall though its flame has darkened with time.</figcaption>
</figure>

The register’s first interior after the arrest, and the cycle’s first true night. Giotto sets the hearing in a closed chamber — coffered wooden ceiling on carved brackets, rose walls, doors shut on the dark — and lights it from inside: on the upper wall, at the centre of the room, burns a torch. Its flame has turned with the centuries — the pigment altered to a dark plume, fire’s ghost on the plaster — but its office is still legible in the room it once lit, for the illumination of the scene obeys it, and the shadows of the tribunal belong to it. A painted interior lit by its own painted light, at night: Western painting had scarcely attempted the problem before, and the blackened flame at the centre of this wall is one of the quiet firsts of the chapel — the Passion’s darkness not stated by a blue sky but built into the physics of a room.

The tribunal is staged as a collision of two gestures, and neither belongs to Christ. At the right, under the canopy of the judgment seat, the court: two bearded judges share the bench — the one in red leaning forward, arm extended toward the prisoner in accusation or interrogation; and beside him Caiaphas, who has just heard the answer the hearing was designed to extract. I adjure thee by the living God, that thou tell us whether thou be the Christ — and the reply, thou hast said — and now the high priest performs the verdict on his own body: both hands at his collar, he tears his robe open across the chest, the rent garment of the Law’s horror, then the high priest rent his clothes, saying, He hath spoken blasphemy. Giotto paints the rip itself — the green robe splitting, the breast bared — institutional grief as theatre, the one violence the judge may legally commit done to his own clothing while the real violence waits a panel away. It is the third time this cycle has shown the institution’s hands at a decisive moment: they joined the spouses at the marriage, they sealed the bargain over the purse, and here they tear cloth.

The second gesture is rising at the left. A man’s arm lifts behind Christ — the blow of the officer that John records, one of the officers which stood by struck Jesus with the palm of his hand — and Giotto has chosen the instant before it lands, as he chose it for the scourge in the Temple and the kiss in the garden: the Passion’s first blow forever cocked, never falling. And Christ, bound, does what no prisoner in the room expects: he turns his head back over his shoulder, toward the striker — the movement John gives him voice for, if I have spoken evil, bear witness of the evil: but if well, why smitest thou me? The turned head is the panel’s whole christology: not flinching, not appeal — address. The guards press around — gold-fringed armour, a fist in his mantle, a young man in brown relaying between prisoner and bench — the apparatus of custody at its work; the bound hands hold still; the gold-figured blue of his mantle sheds its azurite in the old flakes, the cycle’s costliest garment worn to lace on the night it was seized.

The two judges on the one bench have invited a natural reading: the night hearings of the Passion were two, before Annas and before Caiaphas, and the pair enthroned together — red and green, the questioner and the render — compress the double process into a single frame, the dynasty of the high priesthood sitting as one body. However they are named, their bench performs the panel’s coldest statement: it is a throne. Canopied, stepped, draped — the seat of judgment built with the full vocabulary of honour, and from it the institution condemns its guest. The chapel has shown its thrones before: Mary enthroned in the Adoration’s shed, God enthroned on the arch’s wooden door; this is the counter-throne, authority’s furniture doing authority’s worst, and the registers below and opposite will answer it — the mocked king crowned in the next scenes, and the true Judge enthroned at last on the west wall, where the men of this bench will stand on the other side of the sentence.

From here the night runs downhill without pause: the road to Calvary waits beyond, and the hill beyond that. But the panel’s last word belongs to its light. The torch on the wall presided over the only tribunal in the chapel’s story, and its alteration has made it an accidental allegory that Giotto could not have planned and would, one suspects, have accepted: the light of that courtroom has gone out, and the room condemns on in the dark. The register’s next interiors will not need torches; what follows happens in the terrible daylight of the open wall, on the road, on the hill, where the only remaining light is the gold of a halo over a falling body.

### The Mocking of Christ (Coronation with Thorns) (Scenes from the Passion of Christ, 5)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-32.jpg" alt="Fresco of the mocking of Christ, set in a courtyard hall with slender marble columns, a blue coffered loggia ceiling with gold fleur-de-lis, grey walls with two dark grilled windows and an open door at the right. At the lower left, Christ, haloed in gold with a cross and crowned with thorns, sits wrapped in a gold-figured brocade mantle, a reed in his hands, eyes lowered. Around him: a man in violet-grey pulls his hair and raises a fist; a man in brown-red behind raises an arm to strike; two men in red caps lean in, one stretching a hand to Christ’s cheek; a man in rose extends an open palm toward his face; a kneeling youth in pale blue raises his hands in mock homage. At the centre, a Black man in a white tunic swings a cane back with one raised arm. At the right, a group of standing officials: a bearded man in red with a gold-figured panel, a man in red and white headcloth, and elders in patterned mantles, conferring and gesturing toward the scene. Deep blue band of sky above the loggia.">
  <figcaption>The Mocking of Christ (Coronation with Thorns), c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), lower register of the south wall, last panel of the wall. Crowned with thorns and wrapped in a gold brocade for a royal robe, Christ sits with closed patience under a rain of blows and parody — hands strike his face, pull his hair, a kneeling youth mimes homage, a stick is raised behind him — while at the right Pilate confers with the priests, the authorities discussing what the room is doing. Among the tormentors, a Black attendant: one of the earliest African figures in Italian painting.</figcaption>
</figure>

The south wall ends in a room where cruelty has become a game. The tribunal of Caiaphas condemned; the chamber of this panel amuses itself — and Giotto, who gave the arrest its vortex and the hearing its night, gives the mockery what mockery requires: time, leisure, an audience. The architecture is almost gracious — a courtyard hall under a loggia with a blue coffered ceiling sown with gold lilies, marble colonnettes, grilled windows — and inside the graciousness, at the lower left, sits the panel’s immovable centre: Christ, enthroned in parody. The soldiers have dressed the scene themselves, and Giotto inventories their dramaturgy: the gold brocade thrown over the prisoner for a royal robe — the costliest textile of the whole cycle, figured and glittering, wasted here on derision; the plaited crown pressed into the hair; the reed laid in the bound hands for a sceptre. This is the mock-throne, where kingship is conferred as an insult and happens, invisibly to everyone in the room, to be the truth.

Around the seated figure the blows arrange themselves like spokes. One man grips the hair and draws back his fist; another’s arm rises behind; a third leans his grinning face close and reaches fingers to the cheek — the slap domesticated into a caress’s distance; a fourth presents his open palm; and before the throne kneels a youth in pale blue, hands lifted in the full ceremonial of homage, performing the genuflection as theatre — and they bowed the knee before him, and mocked him, saying, Hail, King of the Jews. Giotto’s tormentors are not monsters; they are men enjoying themselves, their faces lit with the ordinary pleasure of a licensed cruelty, and the horror of the panel is exactly its sociability: a torture conducted as entertainment, with roles, props and an etiquette. At the centre of the group, a Black man in a white tunic swings a cane back with one raised arm — a figure of extraordinary documentary interest, among the earliest Africans in Italian painting, rendered without caricature in features or bearing: Giotto extends to him the same observed humanity as to every tormentor, which is to say the same complicity.

Christ absorbs it all in the cycle’s deepest stillness. Eyes lowered, head inclined under the crown, hands folded on the reed, he neither flinches from the gripping fist nor acknowledges the kneeling parody — the composure that has been his mother’s and his own through the whole program here reaching its extremity: patience as the only sovereignty left, exercised absolutely. Isaiah’s servant stands behind the figure — I gave my back to the smiters, and my cheeks to them that plucked off the hair: I hid not my face from shame and spitting — and Giotto has painted the verse almost clause by clause, the plucked hair, the offered cheek, the unhidden face. The gold mantle pours around the seated body in broad, magnificent folds: the painter lavishes on the robe of mockery the full dignity of his drapery style, so that the parody royal garment hangs, in spite of its givers, like a coronation.

At the right, separated by a column’s interval, stands the panel’s second scene: the officials. A bearded man in red — Pilate, by the authority of his dress and his centrality in the group — confers with the priests and elders, white-wimpled and patterned-mantled, their hands moving in the measured gestures of men discussing procedure; one points back toward the mockery as toward an item on an agenda. It is the third consultation of the Passion sequence — the priests conferred behind Judas at the Pact, behind the pointing finger at the Arrest, and now beside the mockery itself — and the most damning: the violence is no longer commanded from a balcony or a bench but simply permitted, proceeding in the same room while authority talks. Giotto’s indictment of power in this cycle is cumulative and precise: Herod extended an arm, Caiaphas tore a robe, Pilate turns his back and converses.

The position is the program’s own rhetoric: the visitor walking the lower register reaches the corner of the chapel with this image — the king crowned in scorn — and must physically turn, crossing the west end under the Last Judgment, to follow the story onto the north wall, where the road to Calvary begins. The turn is the sentence carried out: between the mockery and the cross stands the corner of the building, and the viewer’s own steps complete the trial’s procedure. Behind, the south wall has run from the supper’s quiet loggia to this loud room in five panels — one night, wall-long; ahead, the day. The gold robe will be exchanged for the seamless tunic, the reed for the beam, and the parody of this throne will be corrected, two panels on, by the only coronation the cycle withholds from no one’s sight.

### The Road to Calvary (Scenes from the Passion of Christ, 6)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-33.jpg" alt="Fresco of Christ carrying the cross, moving to the right. At the left, a city gate with battlemented walls and a pink octagonal tower; from it streams a crowd. At its front edge, Mary, haloed, in a blue mantle with heavy losses over red, presses forward with a hand at her breast, as a man beside her thrusts her back. The crowd follows: men in ochre and rose, elders in patterned headcloths (the surface spotted with dark losses), a young man in a short green tunic reaching forward with a rod toward Christ. At the right of centre, Christ, haloed in gold with a cross, in a red tunic and blue gold-figured mantle with losses, walks to the right bearing the long cross over his left shoulder, both hands gripping it, his head turned back over his shoulder toward his mother. Ahead of him walk two figures seen from behind, one in rose and one in brown; from the brown one’s shoulder rises the faint trace of a lost painted timber. Faint vertical traces of lost spears rise through the pale blue sky; dark losses fleck the crowd’s white headcloths.">
  <figcaption>The Road to Calvary (Christ Carrying the Cross), c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), lower register of the north wall, first panel. Out of the city gate the procession pours toward the hill: Christ, the great beam on his shoulder, turns his head back toward his mother, who presses after him at the gate and is thrust away by the crowd — the glance between them crossing the whole cortège. He carries the cross himself, as John writes, with no Cyrenian to share it; the escort’s spears rise behind, ghosts now in the damaged sky.</figcaption>
</figure>

The north wall opens with a departure, as the register’s south wall closed with a room: the city has finished with its prisoner and pours him out of its gate. Giotto compresses the whole road to Golgotha into the first steps outside the wall — the battlemented gate at the left, the pink octagonal tower above it, and the procession already strung across the panel’s width, driven rightward by the direction every walker, every gesture, every diagonal obeys. It is the cycle’s third great passage through a city gate, and the viewer is meant to hold the series: Anna met her husband at the Golden Gate in an embrace; Christ entered Jerusalem through a gate in triumph; now the same civic architecture performs its last office and expels him. The crowd that streams through this one is, recognisably, the crowd of the Entry with its branches exchanged for rods.

Christ carries the cross himself. Giotto follows John to the letter — and he bearing his cross went forth — and admits no Simon of Cyrene to share the beam: the long timbers ride the left shoulder alone, both hands gripping, the body bent to the load but walking, and the omission is the panel’s christology. The synoptics’ conscripted helper belongs to a story about weakness; John’s unassisted bearer belongs to a story about will — I lay down my life… no man taketh it from me — and the fresco states the doctrine as carpentry and posture: the cross is heavy, the carrier is able, and no hand but his own and his tormentors’ touches the wood. Ahead, two figures seen from behind — one in rose, one in brown — lead the way out of the frame; from the shoulder of the brown-clad leader rises the trace of something once painted and now effaced, a timber climbing at an angle — most plausibly a piece of the execution’s equipment, the ladder or a stake borne ahead to the hill, gone the way of the escort’s spears. Behind, a young man in green reaches forward with his rod, prodding — the procession’s machinery of haste, pressing a pace that the central figure, as always in this cycle, declines to adopt.

But the panel’s event is not the carrying; it is the turn. Christ’s head swings back over his shoulder, against the whole current of the march — and at the other end of the crowd, at the gate’s very mouth, is what he turns to: his mother. Mary presses forward at the procession’s edge, one hand at her breast, her gaze on her son, and a man beside her thrusts her back — the crowd’s elbow against the mother, painted with the same unsentimental exactness as every push in this chapel — and between her reaching and his turning runs the panel’s true axis: a glance, thrown over the heads of the entire cortège, from the first face of his story to its last witness. Giotto has used the device before — the gaze that crosses a composition and annuls it — but never at such range or cost: mother and son, who exchanged their first look across a manger on the register above, exchange here the look that the next panel will make their last. The apocryphal and devotional traditions placed this meeting on the road and wept over it for centuries; Giotto reduces it to its optical minimum, two turned heads and a shove, and loses nothing.

The crowd between them is a procession of damage as much as of figures. The white headcloths of the elders are spotted with dark losses, the sky has shed its upper blues, and — most tellingly — the escort’s spears survive only as faint vertical ghosts rising through the pale air: the shafts, painted a secco, have fallen away, leaving the armament of the state as a row of shadows. The accident is almost exegesis: the panel’s violence has evaporated, and what remains legible is exactly what Giotto built to last in the plaster itself — the gate, the walk, the beam, the turned head. Among the walkers the observed world persists as ever: the short-tunicked youth with his rod, the patterned wimples, the bare feet of the two leaders on the rising ground — the road to Golgotha paved with the same documentary attention as the road to Egypt one register up, where another procession carried the same person, bound to his mother then by a striped sling, separated from her now by a crowd’s width.

The panel is the Passion’s shortest text and its purest movement: nothing is said, nothing concludes, the frame’s right edge simply receives the cross and promises the hill. For the chapel’s visitors, walking the lower register as the procession walks the panel, the identification was physical — the Via Crucis devotion, then taking its medieval shape, made every church floor a road to Calvary, and this wall gave Padua its monumental station. What the next panel holds, every walker knew; what this one holds is the interval that devotion has always found hardest, the time of carrying.

### The Crucifixion (Scenes from the Passion of Christ, 7)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-34.jpg" alt="Fresco of the Crucifixion. At the centre, Christ hangs dead on a tall cross against deep blue: head sunk to his right shoulder, cross-inscribed gold halo, body pale and elongated, a translucent white loincloth at his hips, blood running from hands, feet and the wound in his side; above, a tablet with an inscription. The cross stands on a rocky mound at whose base, in a cavity, lie a skull and bone. In the sky, ten small angels in gold, white, green and rose: two at the top flanking the tablet with spread wings, others flying with hands outstretched or tearing their garments, and three holding chalices to the wounds of the hands and side. At the left, a group of haloed mourners: the Virgin in blue, fainting backward into the arms of a woman in gold and the young John in rose; at the foot of the cross kneels Mary Magdalen, red-haired, in rose, her hands at Christ’s feet. At the right, a crowd of soldiers and elders: in front, four men hold and dispute a rose-red robe, one raising his arm, a young soldier in gold-figured armour seen from behind; among them a bearded man with a gold halo looks up at the cross. Spears and a dark banner rise behind, with losses in the blue.">
  <figcaption>The Crucifixion, c. 1303–1305, fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Padua, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), lower register of the north wall. The cross rises at the exact centre, dividing the world: at the left, the Virgin collapses into the arms of John and the holy women; at the foot, the Magdalen embraces the pierced feet; at the right, the soldiers cast lots for the seamless robe while the haloed centurion confesses. Ten angels storm the sky around the dead Christ, some catching the blood of his wounds in chalices, one tearing open the breast of his garment; beneath the rock of Golgotha lies the skull of Adam.</figcaption>
</figure>

The centre of the panel is the centre of the program: a dead man on a cross, at the exact midpoint of the wall’s width, dividing the picture — and the chapel’s whole account of humanity — into the two companies that will face each other again, enlarged to a wall, in the Last Judgment opposite. Giotto builds the division with the cross itself: to its left, grief; to its right, business; beneath it, love; around it, heaven in disorder. And on it, the body. The Christ of this Crucifixion consummates what the painter had begun on his great painted crosses in Florence and Rimini: a crucified figure whose every weight is mortal — the head fallen to the shoulder, the arms drawn long by the hanging mass, the ribcage lifted, the belly hollowed, the knees buckled sideways, the feet overlapped under one nail — death rendered as physics, with the translucent loincloth, a veil of white through which the hips remain visible, as the last, almost immaterial dignity left to the flesh.

At the summit of the cross the titulus is painted as a real thing — a plank of grained wood, its knots and growth-rings rendered with the same relish as the chapel’s fictive marbles — and its golden Gothic letters spell out, with medieval abbreviations,

> Hic est Iesus Nazarenus Rex Iudeorum.

The formula is a conflation: Hic est… Rex Iudeorum is Matthew’s wording (27:37), Nazarenus is John’s (19:19), fused into a single full reading as the age liked its charges complete. The zeta of Naçarenus wears the cedilla of northern Italian spelling, and a plaster loss has carried away part of the first line without touching the sense.

And in the rock, a skull. The mound of Golgotha — the place of a skull — is split at its base into a dark cavity, and in the cavity lie a cranium and a long bone: Adam’s, by the tradition the whole Middle Ages received, which buried the first man under the hill of the cross so that the blood of the second could fall upon his bones. Giotto paints the legend as geology — the relic in its grotto, directly on the picture’s central axis, the lowest point of the vertical that rises through the bleeding feet, the dead body, the titulus, to the angels — and the axis is the doctrine: the new Adam planted on the old, death’s remedy rooted in death’s first casualty.

For the angels have come, and they have come undone. Ten of them storm the blue around the cross — not the serene adorers of the Baptism but a heaven in grief: they dive, wheel, wring their hands; two, flanking the titulus with spread golden wings, bow over the crossbeam. Among the ten, one angel tears open the breast of his garment while his companions wheel, clasp their faces, or stretch out the chalices to the wounds. The motif of the blood-gathering angels, Byzantine in origin, becomes in Giotto’s hands a liturgy of emergency — heaven’s deacons at the dying of God, administering the first Eucharist at its source — and their small storm of gold and rose and green against the deep blue is the panel’s upper music, pitched exactly between lament and adoration.

At the foot of the cross, the two loves. The Magdalen kneels against the wood itself, red hair unbound down her back, her hands at the pierced feet she had once washed with tears and will reach for again, one panel on, in the garden — the cycle’s great continuity of touch, here at its station of blood. And at the left, the Virgin completes what the Road to Calvary began: the mother who was thrust back at the gate has reached the hill, and it has unmade her — she collapses backward, eyes failing, her blue mantle pouring off her shoulders, into the arms of the young John and the holy women, who stagger under the weight. The swoon of the Virgin was contested ground for theologians — did Mary stand, as John writes, *stabat iuxta crucem*, or fall? — and Giotto takes the side of the body: his Mary has stood until standing ended, and the group that receives her, four figures fused into one buttress of grief, is the left half’s whole architecture — the compassio of the mother, her share of the Passion, painted as a second deposition before the first.

The right half belongs to the executioners’ economy. The garment at the stake’s right is held up whole, its dark neck-opening gaping toward the viewer — the seamless tunic of John 19:23–24, *non scindamus eam*, displayed intact at the very moment the soldiers resolve to cast lots rather than divide it. It is the Passion’s last transaction: the cycle that has weighed money and charity on every wall ends the ledger here, with soldiers contending for a dead man’s clothing under the thing they have done. Above the soldiers a dark banner hangs from its staff, the escort’s standard brought to the hill — the state present at its own judgment. And among them, Giotto plants the right half’s redemption: a bearded officer whose head alone in the crowd wears gold — the centurion, haloed already, looking up at the dead face with the recognition the Gospels give him at this instant: truly this man was the Son of God. One man in the business half of the picture has crossed, where he stands, to the other side; the Judgment opposite will formalise what this glance begins.

Above it on the same wall, water became wine at a feast; one register up, the Jordan parted its light; across the nave, the two kisses face each other; and straight ahead, on the west wall, the crucified of this panel sits enthroned amid the angelic hosts, showing his wounds to the saved and the damned — among the latter, hanging by its purse-strings, the man who sold him. The visitor who stands before the Crucifixion stands at the still point of that system: every road of the chapel, narrative, moral and liturgical, passes through this single panel of wall, and the altar’s daily sacrifice stood in its sightline. Giotto knew where he was painting. The blue around this cross is the deepest of the register, the angels the most urgent, the division of mankind the cleanest; and the dead man at the centre, hanging in the silence after the last word, holds the two halves of the picture — and of the program — together by the spread of his arms.

### The Lamentation (The Mourning of Christ)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-35.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. A barren rocky ridge slopes down from upper right to lower left, where the dead Christ lies across his mother’s lap; the Virgin pulls his face close to hers. Two monumental figures seen entirely from behind support the body, Mary Magdalene in red holds the wounded feet, and St John bends over the group with both arms flung out behind him. To the right stand two haloed elders, one carrying a white winding-sheet; to the left the holy women cluster in grief. In the blue sky ten small foreshortened angels wheel and plunge, tearing their cheeks and garments, and a solitary leafless tree crowns the ridge.">
  <figcaption>The Lamentation (The Mourning of Christ), c. 1303–1305. Fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Scenes from the Passion, lower register, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The dead Christ is mourned by the Virgin, the holy women, St John and the men of the burial at the foot of a rocky slope, beneath ten grieving angels and a single bare tree — the emotional summit of the cycle.</figcaption>
</figure>

The rock decides everything. A barren ridge enters at the upper right and drives diagonally down across the field, and every eye in the painting — human, angelic, the viewer’s own — slides down that incline to the lowest corner, where the dead weight of the scene has come to rest: the face of Christ held against the face of his mother. Giotto has built the composition like a tilted plane, and grief obeys gravity.

At the bottom of that slope the Virgin has gathered her son into her lap and pulled his face to hers, searching it at a distance of inches. The Scrovegni cycle began its Christological register with her reaching to take the newborn from the midwife’s hands in the Nativity; here the gesture returns inverted, the same arms receiving the same body, and the chapel’s long symmetry closes like a ring. Around the pair the mourners divide the body among themselves: two figures seen entirely from behind — anonymous, monumental, Giotto’s boldest invention here — bear the shoulders and hold the hands, their massive backs blocking our view precisely so that we must mourn over them, drawn into the circle as the last rank of the company. Mary Magdalene, in the red she has worn since Calvary, sits at the feet she once anointed and studies the wounds in them.

Above the body St John bends forward and flings both arms out behind him like wings broken mid-flight — the most famous gesture of grief in Italian painting, a pose with ancient roots in the meleager sarcophagi that Giotto could have known from Roman reliefs, here stripped of rhetoric and returned to pure shock. Behind him stand the two men of the burial, the elder with the white winding-sheet ready over his arms and his companion at the edge of the field — the tradition reads them as Joseph of Arimathea and Nicodemus, the men of John 19:38–40 who asked Pilate for the body. To the left the holy women cluster into a single wailing mass, hands at cheeks and temples.

In the sky ten small angels wheel and plunge, foreshortened as no angels had been before, and their grief is bodily: they tear their cheeks, rip open their garments, clutch their own heads, trail off into cloud as if dissolving. They are the celestial echo of the human chorus below — heaven mourning with the same want of composure as earth — and their counterpart, ten for ten, to the angels who gathered the blood at the cross in the preceding scene.

And at the crest of the ridge, exactly where the diagonal begins, stands a single leafless tree against the blue. It is the painting’s one word of hope, spoken in a whisper: the dry tree of Eden, dead since the first sin, which legend promised would flower again when the sin was paid. The branch above declares what the bodies below cannot yet know — that the slope of this grief runs onward, past the frame, into the scene that follows.

### The Resurrection (Noli me tangere)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-36.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. At the left, an open sarcophagus of pink-veined marble stands empty; two angels in white and gold sit on its rim, one gesturing toward the vacancy. Below them five soldiers in helmets, armour and patterned brocade sleep in a tangled heap. At the right, Mary Magdalene in her red mantle kneels with both arms outstretched toward the risen Christ, who leans away along the frame’s edge in a white robe, his lowered hand opened in refusal, bearing a tall staff with a white pennon charged with a dark cross and lettered VICTOR MORTIS. Bare stems rise on the hill behind the tomb; green leafing shrubs grow at Christ’s feet under a deep blue sky.">
  <figcaption>The Resurrection (Noli me tangere), c. 1303–1305. Fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Scenes from the Passion, lower register, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. Two angels seated on the empty pink-marble tomb, the watch of soldiers asleep below, and the risen Christ withdrawing from the kneeling Magdalene with the banner of victory — John’s Easter morning compressed into a single field.</figcaption>
</figure>

No one saw the Resurrection, and Giotto does not pretend otherwise. The tomb stands open and already empty, its lid gone, and the event itself has slipped between the frames: what the panel shows is the morning after the unseeable. The sarcophagus is a slab of pink-veined marble rendered with the same loving mineral fiction as the chapel’s painted revetments — the quarry of Giotto’s imagination supplying even the grave.

On its rim sit two angels in white, calm as the guards below are oblivious, one lifting a hand toward the vacancy they advertise. Two angels, not one: the choice is John’s (20:12), the pair seated “where the body of Jesus had lain,” preferred over Matthew’s single angel of the earthquake. Beneath them the watch of soldiers lies in a heap, helmeted heads lolling on shoulders and shields, armour and brocade tangled together — Matthew’s custodes (27:66; 28:4), who became “as dead men” at the very moment the dead man rose. The painting’s wit is quiet and total: the living sleep like corpses at the foot of a grave that could not hold its corpse.

The right half belongs to the garden. Mary Magdalene — the same red mantle that knelt at the cross and cradled the feet in the Lamentation, the third station of her colour across this wall — has recognised the supposed gardener and fallen to her knees, both arms at full stretch. And Christ withdraws. He leans away along the picture’s edge, his lowered hand opened toward her in the gentlest refusal ever painted: noli me tangere, “do not cling to me, for I have not yet ascended” (John 20:17). The entire panel hinges on the few inches of air between her fingertips and his hand — a gap as charged as the almost-touch of lips in the Kiss of Judas on the facing wall, there a contact that damned, here a distance that saves.

In his other hand Christ bears the banner of the Resurrection, a white pennon charged with a dark cross and lettered, fold by fold, VIC-TOR MOR-TIS — “the conqueror of death.” It is the luminous answer to the dark standard that hung over the soldiers at Calvary two scenes back: the state’s ensign eclipsed, the victor’s raised. Even the landscape keeps the ledger. On the hill above the tomb stand bare, wintry stems; at Christ’s side and under his feet the shrubs are green and leafing. Death’s side of the panel and life’s, divided down the middle of one dawn.

The Magdalene kneels at the border between the two halves, the first witness, forbidden to hold what she alone has found.

### The Ascension of Christ

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-37.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. At the centre Christ ascends in strict profile, wrapped in a white robe within a golden aureole of fine rays, standing on a bank of cloud; his raised hands are cut off by the upper border of the field. Two hovering hosts flank him on clouds — winged angels together with haloed, wingless figures, all with hands lifted toward him. Below, on bare ground, two white-robed winged messengers stand at the centre raising their hands upward; the Virgin in deep blue kneels at the left, and the eleven apostles kneel in two groups to either side, one wrapped in a gold-patterned damask mantle. All faces are turned up toward the rising Christ against the blue.">
  <figcaption>The Ascension of Christ, c. 1303–1305. Fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Scenes from the Passion, lower register, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. Christ rises in profile on a shelf of cloud within a golden radiance, his hands already passing beyond the upper frame, escorted by airborne hosts of angels and blessed souls, while below the two white-clad messengers of Acts address the kneeling Virgin and apostles.</figcaption>
</figure>

Giotto’s boldest idea in the whole chapel may be the simplest: Christ’s hands have already left the painting. He rises in strict profile, borne on a shelf of cloud, wrapped in a golden gloria of fine rays — and his fingers pass the upper border of the field, cut off by the frame as by the floor of heaven. What the fresco cannot contain, it declines to fake. The event exceeds the medium, and the painter says so with the frankest device available: he lets his protagonist climb out of the picture.

Everything else in the panel streams upward after him. Two airborne hosts flank the ascent, treading cloud: angels and, among them, haloed figures without wings — the natural reading sees in them the just of the old covenant, the souls Christ harrowed out of Limbo between Friday and Sunday, now carried heavenward in his wake as the first fruits of the victory the Resurrection banner proclaimed. Every hand among them is raised, a double thicket of adoration pointing along the diagonal of his rising.

Below, on the bare ground of Olivet, the earthbound company kneels in two wings. At the left the Virgin, in her deep ultramarine, lifts her face in a gaze that has run the length of this chapel — the mother who looked into the manger, who met her son’s eyes over the heads of the Calvary procession, who held his dead face to hers in the Lamentation, now watching him leave the earth in the body she gave him. Around and opposite her kneel the apostles — eleven of them, and the number is doctrine: Judas is gone, Matthias not yet chosen, and Giotto counts the interval exactly. Among the eleven, one kneels wrapped in a splendid gold-patterned damask — another voice in the conversation of precious stuffs that has run through the cycle since the Temple hung its Eastern silk in the Bringing of the Rods: even on Olivet, at the last hour of the visible Christ, the painter’s eye finds the woven world.

Between the two kneeling wings stand the pair of men “in white garments” of Acts 1:10–11, here frankly winged, each raising a hand toward the vanishing Lord. Their written lines are the scene’s whole script: Viri Galilaei, quid statis aspicientes in caelum? — “Men of Galilee, why do you stand looking into heaven? This Jesus… shall so come, as you have seen him going.” The gesture that points up also points forward: he who leaves by this trajectory will return along it. In a chapel whose west wall holds the Last Judgment, the promise is not abstract; the viewer who turns from this panel toward the exit walks straight into its fulfilment.

The composition is all verticals and updraft — the one scene of the cycle with no architecture, no rock, almost no ground, nothing but bodies, cloud and the blue. After the terrible gravities of the Passion register, where every weight fell and was carried, the last movement of Christ in this chapel is pure release.

### Pentecost (The Descent of the Holy Spirit)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-38.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. A white Gothic pavilion with slender columns, trefoil arches and a marbled base fills the field, drawn in oblique perspective against a blue sky. Inside, the twelve haloed apostles sit on benches around the room: eight face the viewer across the far side, four sit with their backs turned in the foreground, their golden haloes seen from behind. Thin straight red rays of fire descend from above the architrave through the arches, one reaching toward each apostle; the seated men lift their faces and stir their hands. No dove appears — the Spirit arrives as lines of flame alone.">
  <figcaption>Pentecost (The Descent of the Holy Spirit), c. 1303–1305. Fresco, 200 x 185 cm. Scenes from the Passion, lower register, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The final narrative field of the cycle: the twelve apostles seated in a white Gothic loggia as thin red rays of fire descend upon them through the arcade — the birth of the Church, set at the threshold of the Last Judgment on the west wall.</figcaption>
</figure>

The cycle ends indoors, and in order. After thirty-eight scenes of journeys, hillsides, processions and tumults, the last panel is a single quiet room: a white Gothic pavilion, trefoil-arched, slender-columned, drawn in the same crisp oblique perspective as the painted coretti beside the chancel arch — Giotto closing his story with one more demonstration of the fictive architecture that frames it. The loggia is kin to the one that housed the Last Supper at the head of this register, and the kinship is doctrine as much as design: tradition set both events in the same upper room, the Cenacle, so that the register which began with a meal in that chamber ends with a fire in it.

Within, the Twelve sit along the benches — twelve again, not eleven, for between Ascension and Pentecost the lot fell on Matthias and the broken number was made whole (Acts 1:26), as the eleven of the Ascension, one field back, silently predicted. Eight face us across the room; four sit with their backs turned in the foreground, completing the ring. These back-turned apostles reprise the great invention of the Last Supper, where Giotto first dared to seat witnesses between us and the event — and here, as there, the device does its silent work of enclosure: the circle has no empty seat except the one the viewer occupies.

Down through the arches slide the rays — thin red lances of fire descending from above the architrave, one to each man, “parted tongues as it were of fire” that “sat upon every one of them” (Acts 2:3). Giotto gives no dove and no wind made visible; the Spirit arrives as pure geometry, straight lines of flame threading a building without disturbing it, and only the lifted faces and stirring hands of the apostles register the arrival. It is the most reticent theophany in the chapel — after the blazing gloria of the Ascension, a Pentecost of almost domestic stillness.

The placement is the final stroke of the programme. This is the last narrative field on the north wall, hard against the chancel arch. The sequence is deliberate: Pentecost is the birth of the Church, the moment the story of Christ becomes the story of everyone, and Giotto sets it at the exact point where the painted narrative hands over to the viewer’s own life. The apostles will rise from these benches and go out to preach; the visitor rises from this chapel and goes out beneath the Judgment that preaching announced.

So the great clock of the chapel completes its dial. From Joachim’s expulsion at the top of the opposite wall, the story has spiralled down through three registers and two testaments of one family’s flesh, to end in a white room where fire falls gently on twelve seated men.

## The Scrovegni Chapel: The Vices and the Virtues

The lowest zone of the Scrovegni Chapel pretends not to be painting at all. Beneath the three registers of sacred history runs a dado of fictive architecture — veined marble revetments, polished and panelled — and set into it fourteen niches that appear to hold statues: seven Virtues along the south wall, seven Vices along the north, each facing its opposite across the nave. Nothing is carved. The figures are grisaille, paint declaring itself stone, the first great monochrome cycle of Italian art — and a quiet manifesto, for with these fourteen “sculptures” Giotto claims that the brush can do everything the chisel does, and then does twice what no chisel could: Inconstancy slides on her rolling wheel and Hope lifts clear off the plinth, the two impossible statues that bracket the fiction they break. Where the registers above tell what happened, the dado tells what everything that happened was for: it is the chapel’s moral key, set at the height of the visitor’s own body, walked between rather than looked up at.

The two files are built as one system of pairs. Prudence at her desk, compass set to the book, answers the gaping Fool; Fortitude, planted behind the shield in which five spent shafts stand broken, answers the woman the wheel is throwing; Temperance, bitted and binding her own sword, answers Wrath tearing her garment open; crowned Justice, enthroned in intact architecture against the dado’s one blue sky, answers Injustice in his ruined gate with the forest through the floor; Faith on bare rock, idol and books beneath her heel, answers the believer haltered to the figurine on his own hand; Charity handing her heart up to God answers Envy on fire, the serpent of her tongue striking back at her own brow; and airborne Hope, reaching for the crown an angel lowers, answers Despair hanging from the beam that bends under her weight. Beneath the two central thrones run the dado’s twin predellas, a single road painted twice — dancers, song and the hunting soldier where Justice reigns, robbery and the dragged woman where she is driven out — the whole argument of Italy’s communal palaces stated half a century before Siena, in two strips of grisaille a hand high.

Each figure once explained itself. Along the plinths run Latin tituli in leonine verse — written a secco, by a hand contemporary with Giotto though not his own — which six centuries of decay reduced to three legible voices: Hope, Charity and Justice. Their near-complete recovery is recent, owed to the palaeographer [Giulia Ammannati](/scholars.html#ammannati-giulia), whose edition (Pinxit industria docte mentis, 2017) restored to the dado a running commentary that audits the images attribute by attribute: the Fool’s verses inventory paunch, plumes and cudgel; Envy’s enumerate horn, serpent and probing ear. A document of Paduan pre-humanism, the verses also harbour the programme’s two glances at itself: Envy is “painted by the industry of a learned mind,” Fortitude’s likeness “subtly painted” — invention praised on one wall, execution on the other, and the identity of that docta mens, Giotto’s own or his theological adviser’s, remains the open question at the centre of Arena studies.

Text and image, moreover, do not always agree — and the disagreements are the dado’s most revealing seam. The verses give Prudence a second face turned toward the past; the wall shows none. They arm Fortitude with transitive verbs — she “lays all things low” — while the painting gives her stillness and a lowered club. They tell Temperance only to “compose the hand”; Giotto invents what the composed hand does, binding the sword into its sheath. They promise Hope a crown “from Christ”; the painter sends an angel to carry it, since the giver himself is not yet to be shown. Four negotiations between programme and brush, all tilting the same way: the verses legislate, the figures think. Even the prosody takes sides. The four cardinal virtues share a single alternating metre, the measured tread of reason, while the three theological virtues each break rank into verse-forms otherwise found across the nave — and at the door the twin inscriptions of Hope and Despair share one mould and one vocabulary, firma answering firmetur, the flight answering the rope.

The dado is also where the chapel reads itself. Charity’s heart, passed upward into God’s hands, is the donor’s own gesture in miniature — steps away, on the west wall, Enrico Scrovegni hands his chapel to the Virgin in exactly that motion, between a file of virtues that tramples money-bags and a file of vices where the only purse is clenched in Envy’s fist. And the two rows run the same direction as every register above them: from the altar toward the Judgment. The visitor leaving the chapel walks the whole moral gauntlet at body height — Folly to Despair on one hand, Prudence to Hope on the other — and passes, last of all, between a woman hanging with her fists shut and a woman flying with her hands open, into the west wall where crowns and ropes are distributed at scale. Fourteen fictive statues, one choice, posed at the door.

### Stultitia (Foolishness)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-45.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a marble statue in a niche. A corpulent man stands in profile facing left, bare-legged and barefoot, wearing a crown of upright feathers and a short tunic with ragged dagged hems and a plaited belt hung with small pendants; a long tail of cloth hangs behind him. He raises a knotted club in both hands and gapes upward open-mouthed. The painted niche has a dark speckled ground; the titulus above and the inscription below are largely effaced.">
  <figcaption>Stultitia (Foolishness), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Vices, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The first of the seven Vices, facing Prudence across the nave: a heavy, feather-crowned fool in a ragged dagged tunic, raising a crude club — folly as the body ungoverned by mind.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Hic signatur homo stultus, / al[vu]m gerens, pennis fultus, / ore hians, risu [.]ult[us], / [ ~ ~ ] lignum arens. / [ ~ ~ ~ ~ ~ ~ -este] / [ ~ ~ ~ ~ ~ ~ -este] / [t]a[nt]o pilo, tali veste / gaudet, sensu carens.

“Here is depicted the foolish man: with his paunch, propped on his feathers, mouth agape, […] laughter, […] a withered stick. […] He delights in such a club and such a dress, void of sense.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

The row of Vices opens, nearest the altar, with Folly. He is the only comic figure in the chapel: a fat man in a ruined parody of festive dress, his tunic hacked into rags, his belt plaited and hung with baubles, a train of tattered cloth dragging behind him like a tail. On his head stands a crown of feathers — the fool’s diadem, weightless plumage where wisdom should sit — and in his hands a club, the brute argument of those who have no other. He gapes upward at nothing. Across the nave, Prudence sits at a desk with book and mirror, seeing double into past and future; Folly sees nothing at all, and the pairing needs no caption.

The figure descends from the insipiens of Psalm 52 — “the fool hath said in his heart: there is no God” — who appears crowned and clubbed in psalter illumination throughout the thirteenth century; Giotto gives the tradition flesh, and the flesh is the point. The body is heavy, well fed, planted on bare legs; folly here is not madness but appetite walking, the creature who has everything except government of himself. His dress looks forward, too: the dagged rags, bells and feathers anticipate by a century the livery of the court jester, and more than one historian of costume has come to this niche for the prehistory of motley.

The verses on the plinth certify every attribute the eye registers — belly, plumes, gaping mouth, withered stick, rags — two whole lines lost to the wall but the sentence intact at either end. And the operative verb is the cruellest available: the fool *gaudet*, he enjoys his equipment, delighting in club and costume alike, *sensu carens* — void of the one thing that would tell him what he looks like. The sequence the verses open is legible as architecture itself.

### Inconstantia (Inconstancy)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-44.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating sculpture in a marble niche. A young woman in a loose belted gown balances unsteadily on a large disc or wheel that rolls across a steeply tilted floor of red-veined marble. Her body arches backwards, one arm thrown high, the other flung down and back; her gown swings with the motion. Behind her is a dark porphyry-ground panel; the titulus above the niche is entirely lost.">
  <figcaption>Inconstantia (Inconstancy), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Vices, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The second Vice, opposite Fortitude: a young woman flailing on a rolling wheel, sliding across a tilted floor of veined marble — the only “statue” in the chapel that cannot stand still.</figcaption>
</figure>

Every other figure of the dado at least pretends to be a statue. Inconstancy cannot: she is falling. Giotto sets a young woman on a wheel — Fortune’s own instrument, demoted from cosmic engine to carnival prop — and the wheel is rolling, and the floor beneath it slopes. Her arms windmill, her gown swings wide, her weight is nowhere; the sculptural fiction that holds the whole zone together breaks down in this one niche, because inconstancy is precisely the state that cannot be fixed in stone. The allegory is enacted by the medium itself: a statue of Inconstancy is a contradiction, and Giotto paints the contradiction.

He gives her the only coloured floor in the fourteen niches. Where her companions stand on level grey plinths, she slides across a slab of marble veined in violent red — polished, beautiful and treacherous, the one surface in the dado a prudent person would not walk on. The chapel’s great lesson in fictive marble, begun in the revetments and perfected in the coretti, here turns moral: the same painted stone that elsewhere promises solidity is tilted into a ramp. Even her setting is unreliable.

Across the nave sits her answer. Fortitude stands square in her niche, armoured, wrapped in a lion’s pelt, shield planted — mass, verticality, immobility. The two women are the dado’s clearest antithesis of statics: one figure all load-bearing weight, the other all tangent and spin. The medieval schoolmen defined constantia as a steadiness of soul that neither fear nor desire could tip; Giotto, who argued in bodies, simply shows what its absence looks like when gravity gets involved.

Of her titulus nothing survives. The scholarship that recovered the dado’s Latin verses almost in their entirety had to pass over this one niche — beneath Inconstantia nothing more can be read — so that the most unstable of the Vices is also the one whose caption time itself has shaken off. The figure does without it. No verse could say more plainly than that backward-arching body: whoever rides the wheel does not steer it.

### Ira (Wrath)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-43.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a marble statue in a niche with a dark speckled ground. A heavy-set woman stands with her head thrown back and eyes closed, gripping the neckline of her gown with both hands and wrenching it apart at the breast. Her long hair falls loose down her back in a thick waving strand. The inscription on the plinth below is largely effaced.">
  <figcaption>Ira (Wrath), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Vices, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The third Vice, opposite Temperance: a woman tearing open her garment at the breast, head thrown back — the gesture Caiaphas performs in the Passion register across the nave, anger made statuary.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Clari[t]ate ration[is] / Ira privat hominem / [ ~ ~ ~ ~ ~ ]ctionis / modum fere neminem. / Vestis [a]ct[us] hic [sc]issure / signant hoc et facies / Sol[em] [con]tra sic figure, / fert quem visus acties.

“Wrath deprives man of the light of reason; […] almost any measure. This is what the act of tearing the garment signifies here, and the figure’s face set thus against the Sun — a Sun her gaze withstands.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Wrath does not strike anyone. Giotto’s deepest insight in this niche is that anger’s first victim is the angry: the woman attacks her own clothing, wrenching the gown open at the breast with both fists, head thrown back, eyes shut, mouth working. The violence has no object but herself. Her loose hair — the only Vice whose hair streams free — falls down her back like something already torn. It is a figure of pure discharge, energy with nowhere to go, and it stands in a niche pretending to be marble: rage carved, which is to say rage trapped, forever at the top of the breath it cannot release.

The pose has a long memory. The rending of garments is the Hebrew Bible’s oldest gesture of grief and outrage, and antiquity knew its theatrical twin in the frenzies of Medea; Giotto fuses the two into a body that is both liturgical and pagan, a priestess of her own temper. And he will use it again. Across the nave, in the Passion register, in Christ before Caiaphas, the high priest tears his robe at the prisoner’s blasphemy — the same two-fisted grip, the same wrenching apart. Three times the chapel plays the chord: wrath in allegory, wrath in history, and its inversion in heaven’s mourning at the Crucifixion. The viewer who has stood before this niche recognises Caiaphas instantly, and the recognition is a verdict: the judge of Christ acts out, in narrative, the Vice painted beneath his feet.

Across the nave stands the answer, and it is a bridle. Temperance binds a sword into its scabbard and wears a bit in her mouth — restraint as armament.

The recovered verses on the plinth do two things at once. They certify the image’s own vocabulary — the act of tearing the garment, they say, is what marks her — and then they add the one thing no eye would guess: the figure’s face is set against the Sun, a Sun her gaze withstands. The thrown-back head is not blind paroxysm but something stranger — a stare the light cannot correct, because what the light would appeal to is already gone: the verses open with the claritas of reason that wrath strips away and close with the Sol her eyes endure, the same radiance lost at one end and defied with impunity at the other. Whoever can look at the sun has nothing left to burn. Of all the Vices, Ira is the one later centuries would copy most — her sisters descend through Tuscan painting into the very grammar of depicted fury — because Giotto solved here a problem that would occupy painters for two hundred years: how to show an emotion whose essence is motion, in a body that cannot move.

### Iniustitia (Injustice)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-42.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. A bearded man in a fur-trimmed judge’s cap and ample mantle sits enthroned in profile within the arched gateway of a fortress whose battlements are broken and whose jambs are cracked crags. He holds a tall staff ending in a hook in his right hand and the hilt of a sword in his left. Leafy trees have grown up around and over his throne. In a narrow strip below, small figures enact violence on a road: a riderless horse, armed men, a traveller seized, a woman dragged to the ground, two soldiers with round shields.">
  <figcaption>Iniustitia (Injustice), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 60 cm. Dado of the Vices, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The central Vice, opposite Justice: a lordly judge enthroned in a ruined city gate overgrown by forest, hooked staff and sword in hand, while in the predella below his realm travellers are robbed and killed on the road.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Iniustitia / que fiducia / malis datu[r]! / Cre[scunt] nemora; / iuris anchora, / pax fugatur. / F[ig]u[n]t spolia / ho[micid]ia, / fraus [et] dolus; / in itinere / vadit libere / predo solus. / I, [Iu]stitia! / Gaude[n]t vitia, / sta[t] [t]yramn[u]s: / rapit manibus, / rodit dentibus: / est u[t] [r]a[m]n[u]s.

“What confidence Injustice gives the wicked! The woods grow; peace, the anchor of law, is put to flight. Murders, fraud and deceit hang up their spoils; on the road the robber ranges free, alone. Begone, Justice! The vices rejoice, the tyrant stands: he snatches with his hands, he gnaws with his teeth: he is like the bramble.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Injustice is the only Vice with a government. Where his six companions are solitary states of soul, Giotto paints him as a ruler — bearded, furred, capped like a doctor of law, enthroned in the gateway of a city — because injustice, unlike anger or envy, is a public sin: it needs jurisdiction. And the jurisdiction is rotting. The battlements above his head are broken, the gate’s jambs have split into raw crags, and a forest has grown up through the court itself, trees crowding the throne the way vegetation takes an abandoned ruin. He sits in profile, looking away, lordly and unconcerned, and holds his two instruments of office corrupted: the staff of command ends in a hook — a weapon for seizing, not a sceptre for ruling — and the sword stays reversed in his grip, justice’s blade kept for private use.

Then, in the narrow strip along the plinth, Giotto shows what the allegory costs. A road runs through the predella — the only Vice given a predella — and on it the consequences play out in miniature: a horse stripped of its rider, armed men at their work, a traveller seized by the cloak, a woman dragged to the ground, soldiers idle behind their shields. It is the forest road of every fourteenth-century traveller’s fear, the place where the merchant’s goods and the woman’s body are forfeit the moment the ruler above stops looking. The logic is stated with terrifying economy: injustice is not the robbery; injustice is the enthroned indifference that licenses it. The crimes are small because, from the gate, that is exactly how they look.

Across the nave, Justice answers panel for panel: a crowned queen enthroned beneath intact architecture, a pan in each outstretched hand, and in her predella dancers and riders moving safely along the same road. The two predellas are the chapel’s treatise on government. For the merchants of Padua and for Enrico Scrovegni above all, whose fortune moved on exactly such roads, the choice of this pair as the central axis of the dado was not abstract moral furniture: it was the condition of their world.

The recovered verses turn out to be the scene’s own key. Crescunt nemora, they begin — “the woods grow” — certifying that the forest choking the throne is no painter’s fancy but the program’s first symptom of misrule; and the predella is captioned line for line, in itinere vadit libere predo solus, “on the road the robber ranges free.” Then the closing image: the tyrant who snatches with his hands and gnaws with his teeth est ut ramnus — “is like the bramble.” The word reaches back to Jotham’s parable (Judges 9), where the trees, seeking a king, crown the worthless thornbush after every noble tree declines — and if the allusion holds, the vegetation surrounding this throne is that very assembly, and the enthroned lord among his trees is the bramble made king. One cry in the verses even crosses the nave: I, Iustitia! — “Begone, Justice!” — flung by the inscription at the crowned queen enthroned directly opposite, the only titulus in the dado that addresses its facing Virtue. At the midpoint of the wall that carries the Passion overhead, Giotto sets the sin that makes passions lethal and gives it the best seat in the ruin.

### Infidelitas (Infidelity)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-41.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a marble statue in a niche with a dark ground. A man in a broad-brimmed helmet and a voluminous mantle stands holding a small female idol upright on his raised right hand; the idol holds a leafy sprig, and a cord runs from her down to a loop around the man’s neck. At the upper right, a half-figure of God leans from the corner with a scroll, unseen by the man, whose helmet shields his eyes. At the lower left rises a tongue of flame. The inscription below is worn, with scattered words legible.">
  <figcaption>Infidelitas (Infidelity, Unbelief), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Vices, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The fifth Vice, opposite Faith: a helmeted man haltered by a cord to the little idol standing on his own hand, deaf and blind to the figure of God calling him from above.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Infidelis claudicat, / ydolo se dedicat, / spernit qui se predicat / clauso visu. / Trahit Ydolatria / hunc ignis ad atria, / p[u]ls[u]m celi patria / Christi nisu. / [C]a[p]ut tectum [g]a[l]ea / gerit mens erronea, / sperans so[l]a terrea, / digna [ris]u.

“The unbeliever limps; he devotes himself to the idol and, closing his eyes, scorns the one who announces himself. Idolatry drags him to the halls of fire, expelled from the heavenly homeland by Christ’s effort. His erring mind bears its head covered by a helmet, for it hopes only for earthly things — worthy of laughter.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Unbelief, in Giotto’s analysis, is not emptiness but misdirection: the man believes — intensely, totally — in something he made himself. On his upraised palm stands a little female idol, a figurine hardly taller than his forearm, holding her sprig of leaves like a sceptre; and from the idol a cord runs down and loops around the believer’s neck. The hierarchy could not be drawn more savagely. He carries her, yet she leads him; the creature of his hand has become the halter of his will. Giotto has compressed the whole psalmic polemic against idols — “they have mouths and speak not” — into one loop of rope: whoever worships the work of his hands ends up on its leash.

What he cannot see is painted anyway. From the upper corner of the niche, God himself leans into the field with a scroll, calling — and the call falls on a helmet. The man’s broad-brimmed casque, worn indoors against no weapon, shields exactly the angle from which grace arrives; his eyes stay level, locked on the idol at their own height. Faith, the scholastics said, enters through hearing; Giotto gives unbelief headgear. At his feet, in the lower corner, a flame rises — the fire that attends idolatry through the dado (his neighbour Envy will stand in it), licking already at the hem of a mantle its wearer has not noticed.

The costume carries its own argument. The ample draped mantle and the helmet make a figure deliberately foreign and faintly antique — belief gone wrong dressed as the old world that had not known, or had refused, the new dispensation.

The titulus of this niche was long a philologist’s crux — its earliest readers already produced divergent transcriptions, sign of early decay — but the recovered verses repay the wait. They open with a diagnosis no eye would have supplied: infidelis claudicat, “the unbeliever limps” — the Vulgate’s own verb for divided worship, Elijah’s taunt on Carmel, “how long will you go limping between two sides?” (1 Kings 18:21). They give the cord its destination: trahit Ydolatria hunc ignis ad atria — Idolatry drags him, by that leash, “to the halls of fire,” so that the flame in the corner is not an attribute but an address. And they gloss the helmet outright: the erring mind keeps its head covered because it hopes for earthly things only — digna risu, “worthy of laughter,” the verdict that hands this believer over to the same derision that crowned the Fool at the head of the row. One cord, one helmet, one unseen scroll, and now one limp: unbelief anatomised by a painter who understood that the opposite of faith is not doubt but a smaller, more convenient god.

### Invidia (Envy)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-40.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a statue in a niche with a dark red porphyry ground. An old woman stands in profile facing left, a horn-like peak rising from her headcloth and an enormously enlarged ear exposed. From her open mouth issues a thick serpent that curls back on itself to strike at her forehead between the eyes. Her left hand clutches a drawstring money-bag with hanging fringes; her right hand reaches forward, fingers crooked into a claw. Red flames rise around her from the base of the niche, engulfing the lower part of her gown.">
  <figcaption>Invidia (Envy), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Vices, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The sixth Vice, opposite Charity: an old woman with a monstrous ear and a serpent issuing from her mouth to strike back at her own brow, clutching a money-bag as flames rise around her legs — the most famous of the seven.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> [P]atet hic Invidi[a], / sua ferens propria, / quam pinxit industria / docte mentis. / Bona terit cornibus, / serpe[n]tin[i]s morsibus / m[o]rdet, rapit manibus / pie g[en]tis. / Aure cuncta sciscitans, / [sem]p[er] ardet inhians: / non impletur, obvians / i[n] eventis.

“Here Envy stands revealed, bearing her own attributes, painted by the industry of a learned mind. With her horns she wears down the goods of honest folk, with serpent bites she bites them, with her hands she snatches them. Probing all things with her ear, she burns, forever agape with craving: she is never filled — and recoils upon herself in the outcome.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Envy is the panel in which Giotto’s allegorical imagination burns hottest — literally: hers is the most violent eruption of colour in the dado’s monochrome, the red of the flames that rise around her like a garment of fire, consuming the statue the niche pretends to hold. On the Vices’ side of the nave colour invades a figure only here, in the flame beside Infidelity and in the demon of Despair, and the sentence is the same each time: hellfire and its courier, the two things grisaille refuses to pass off as marble.

Every organ of her body has been re-engineered by her sin. The ear is monstrous, dilated beyond nature — envy lives by hearing of others’ good. A horn rides her brow. The hand not holding reaches forward, crooked into a claw, toward whatever the neighbour has. And from her mouth comes the masterstroke: a serpent, the slandering tongue made flesh, which leaves her lips, curls in the air, and strikes back — at her. The fangs close on her own forehead, between the eyes. Fourteenth-century moralists taught that livor, envy’s venom, blinds the envier before it harms anyone else; Giotto turns the doctrine into a single terrible circuit, the tongue that bites its speaker, malice completing itself at home.

In her left hand she throttles a money-bag, fringed and fat, held not to spend but to keep. The purse ties this niche into the chapel’s deepest current: in the Last Judgment, steps away, the usurers hang in Hell with just such purses at their necks, and the whole building, by its founder’s design, answers for a fortune made in coin. Among the seven Vices it is Envy — not avarice, which the programme pointedly omits as a titled figure — who carries the money, grasping with one hand what she clutches with the other: the condition of wanting that no having cures. In a banker’s chapel, the choice of where to hang the purse is the programme’s quietest act of self-knowledge.
