The recovered verses audit her organ by organ — horns that wear down “the goods of honest folk,” serpent bites, snatching hands, the ear that “probes all things” — and close by certifying both of the figure’s deepest truths at once: non impletur, she is never filled, and obvians in eventis, she recoils upon herself in the outcome — the serpent’s trajectory stated as doctrine. But the opening strophe does something no other titulus in the dado dares: it speaks of the painting itself, quam pinxit industria docte mentis — Envy “painted by the industry of a learned mind.” For one verse, the program signs its work and keeps the signature anonymous. Of all the images Giotto left at Padua, this old woman on fire has travelled furthest — into Dante’s generation, which knew envy as eyes sewn shut, and into every later attempt to paint the sin that cannot look at happiness without bleeding.

### Desperatio (Despair)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-39.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a statue in a niche with a dark speckled ground. A woman hangs by the neck from a horizontal beam that bows under her weight; the cord, painted a secco, survives mainly as a dark vertical trace above her head. Her head slumps to her left shoulder, her arms hang wide with both fists clenched, and her gown falls in long vertical folds to pointed shoes. At the upper left a small winged demon, painted in red, plunges toward her head with hooked claws. Above the niche runs the title DESPERATIO; on the plinth below, lines of the Latin titulus remain partly legible.">
  <figcaption>Desperatio (Despair), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 60 cm. Dado of the Vices, north wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The last of the Vices, opposite Hope and hard against the Last Judgment: a woman hanged from a bending beam, fists clenched, as a small winged demon swoops to seize her soul.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Instar cordis desperati, / Sathan ductu suffocati / et Gehenne sic dampnati / tenet hec figura. / [In adv]ersis spe firmetur / mens alata, n[e] dampnetur. / Non d[es]peret: nam tolletur, / spe sic [conc]essur[a].

“This figure holds the likeness of a heart in despair, suffocated at Satan’s leading and thus damned to Gehenna. In adversity let the winged mind be made firm with hope, lest it be damned. Let it not despair: for it shall be lifted up — hope will so grant it.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

The row of the Vices ends at the west end of the wall, beside the Last Judgment — beside Hell — and Giotto closes it with the one sin that has no sequel. Despair is a woman who has hanged herself. The niche that everywhere else holds a fictive statue here holds a fictive corpse: head fallen to the shoulder, arms dropped wide, and both fists still clenched — the last contraction of a will that refused, to the end, to open. A statue that hangs is as impossible as a statue that falls: Giotto breaks the sculptural fiction exactly where the sin breaks the order of things.

The painter’s realism does not flinch even at the gallows’ physics: the beam she hangs from bows visibly under her weight. It is a detail of astonishing audacity — painted timber flexing under a painted body, the trompe-l’œil of the dado pushed to its limit — and it makes the allegory corporeal: despair has mass; it bends what carries it. The cord itself was laid in a secco and has mostly perished, surviving as a dark trace climbing from her neck to the beam, so that she now hangs, like the lost spears of the Road to Calvary in the register above, from a ghost.

At the upper left arrives the sentence. A small demon, winged and clawed and painted in red, plunges toward her head to seize the soul leaving her body. The chapel has shown this traffic before, in the opposite direction: in the Sacrifice of Joachim, at the top of the opposite wall, a little soul rises with the smoke of an accepted offering. Here the dark twin of that transaction closes the system — what despair offers, only the devil collects. Theology stands behind the horror: despair is the sin against the Holy Spirit, the one refusal mercy cannot reach, because it is the refusal of mercy itself.

The recovered verses sharpen both halves of the image. The first strophe gives the demon his true office: the desperate heart hangs Sathan ductu, “suffocated at Satan’s leading” — the red hunter above her head is not collecting the wreckage but conducting it, author of the knot before he is owner of the soul. Then the second strophe turns from the figure to the viewer. In adversis spe firmetur mens alata — “in adversity let the winged mind be made firm with hope, lest it be damned; let it not despair, for it shall be lifted up.” The winged mind is painted twelve feet away: across the nave Hope rises toward a crown held out from heaven, and the verses seal the pairing with a single verb, tolletur, “shall be raised,” the exact counter-motion to the rope. Steps away, around the corner of the Judgment, Judas hangs in Hell, the one figure in the chapel who repeats her pose, history confirming allegory. The visitor leaving the chapel passes her last of all the Vices — and the programme’s final word before the door turns out to be not only a diagnosis but, uniquely here, a prescription: of all the ways to be lost, only this one locks from inside, and only here does the wall itself call through the door.

### Prudentia (Prudence)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-46.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating sculpture in a niche. A woman appears half-length behind a tall panelled desk, seated in a chair with ornate scrolled metalwork arms. She is seen in profile facing right, hair bound in a cloth band, setting a pair of compasses to the open book on the desk with her right hand and raising a round convex mirror in her left. The niche ground is dark and speckled.">
  <figcaption>Prudentia (Prudence), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 60 cm. Dado of the Virtues, south wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The first of the seven Virtues, facing Foolishness across the nave: a scholar at her desk, compasses set to the open book and convex mirror raised — the past remembered, the present measured, the future scried — knowledge of the past and foresight of what comes.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Res et tempus summa cura / agit[a]t Prudentia, / spec[u]letur [ut] futu[ra] / [su]a providentia. / Memoratur iam elapsa, / o[rd]inat pres[en]tia; / mens ruinam non est [p]assa, / hac do[n]ata gratia. / Sextu[s] [circu]mgi[r]at p[er]gens; / sc[it] f[u]turum spe[c]ulum / et antiqua vultus [v]ergens / [qu]o transiit [s]e[c]ulum.

“Prudence weighs deeds and time with utmost care, that her foresight may scry what is to come. She remembers what has passed, orders what is present; the mind suffers no ruin, once granted this grace. The compass circles round, holding its course; the mirror knows the future, and the face turned back to where time has gone knows the things past.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

The row of Virtues opens with the one that governs all the others. Prudentia, in the scholastic order Giotto’s programme follows, is not caution but the charioteer of the virtues — auriga virtutum — the intelligence that sees situations truly and chooses the means; and Giotto paints her, with perfect Paduan logic, as a scholar at work. She sits behind a massive panelled desk, a piece of furniture rendered with the same cabinet-maker’s relish as the chapel’s fictive marbles, in a chair whose scrolled metal arms would do credit to a chancellor. One hand sets the compass to the open book; the other raises a mirror.

The verses on the plinth, recovered after long illegibility, assign the instruments their offices — and the assignment is stranger and finer than any guess. The mirror, they say, knows the future: not the glass of self-inspection but a scrying instrument, the speculum in which foresight reads what has not yet happened. The book and the working desk hold the present, which Prudence “orders”; and the past belongs to a face — vultus vergens quo transiit seculum, “the face turned back to where time has gone.” Here, for once, text and image part company: the verses describe the Janus-face of the antique tradition, but the wall shows none — behind the bound hair the head is simply a head. Either the second face was entrusted to a finish now lost, or Giotto declined the monster and let the mirror and the book divide the offices between them; in any case the discrepancy is precious. One figure, three tenses — two painted, one only promised.

The verses name the instrument in her working hand: the sextus, the geometer’s compass, which “circles round, holding its course” — and the phrase is a definition of the virtue itself: regulated turning, the circle governed from its centre. It also completes the dado’s geometry. One niche along, across the nave, Inconstancy rides her wheel — the circle nobody governs — and the pair resolve into a single diagram: the same rotation, with and without a fixed point. Prudence holds the centre; Inconstancy is what rotation does to those who don’t.

And the furnishing has a local accent. A figure half-hidden behind a tall desk, writing and checking — every Paduan merchant who entered the chapel knew that silhouette: it is the posture of the counting-house, of the ledger kept and the entry verified. In the oratory of a banking family, the first Virtue is painted as the act of keeping honest books. The dado will return to the theme at its centre, where Justice weighs, and near its far end, where a purse hangs from Envy’s fist; Prudence opens the ledger that the rest of the wall audits.

### Fortitudo (Fortitude)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-47.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a statue in a niche with a dark porphyry ground. A stout woman stands facing right in a mail-sleeved tunic and cuirass, a pelt knotted across her chest and a crested cap on her head. In her right hand she grips a short mace or broken club at her side; with her left she steadies an immense elongated shield, taller than her shoulder, bearing a lion rampant in relief. Five broken shafts of darts and spears remain embedded.">
  <figcaption>Fortitudo (Fortitude), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Virtues, south wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The second Virtue, facing Inconstancy: a woman in mail and cuirass, a lion’s pelt knotted at her breast, planted behind a great shield blazoned with a rampant lion and studded with the broken weapons that have failed against it.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Cuncta sternit Fortitudo / pugna[n]do viril[iter], / s[i]c[ut es]t similitudo / depicta subtiliter. / Cu[m] leone sc[utum] [fe]rens, / sic adve[r]sa repri[mit]; / et armata clavam gerens, / prava q[ue]que deprimit. / En occidit vi leonem, / eius pelle tegitur; / omnem superat agonem / et in nullo frangitur.

“Fortitude lays all things low, fighting manfully, just as her likeness is subtly painted. Bearing a shield with a lion upon it, she thus represses adversities; and, armed, wielding a club, she crushes every wickedness. Behold, she has slain a lion by force and is clothed in its pelt; she overcomes every contest and in none is she broken.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Fortitude does not attack. Giotto’s entire conception of the virtue is defensive: she stands behind a shield nearly as tall as herself, planted like a door, and the shield tells the story her stillness cannot — embedded in its surface are the stumps of the weapons that have been thrown at it. Five shafts, each broken off at the point of failure, left in the field like trophies in reverse. Courage, the image argues, is not the delivering of blows but the absorbing of them; the virtue is measured by what has shattered against it.

The recovered verses push the other way, and the push is instructive. Cuncta sternit Fortitudo — “Fortitude lays all things low, fighting manfully,” she “crushes every wickedness”: the programme hands the painter the militant virtue of the tradition, all transitive verbs. What Giotto paints is their aftermath. The club the verses have her wielding hangs gripped but lowered; the five broken shafts in the shield — her chief glory on the wall — the text never mentions; the only narrative the titulus tells, en occidit vi leonem, “behold, she has slain a lion and is clothed in its pelt,” is already over, worn as costume. Text and image negotiate, as they do at Prudence’s missing second face: the verses say what she does, the figure shows what she is.

Her equipment is a short history of strength. The mail sleeves and cuirass are contemporary soldier’s issue; the pelt knotted across her chest is older — the lion-skin of Hercules, antiquity’s strong man conscripted into the Christian scheme, its knot tied exactly where his was. And the lion returns, rampant, as the charge on the shield: the beast worn and the beast borne, strength as second skin and strength as heraldry.

Mass is the argument. Of the seven Virtues she is the heaviest, square-shouldered, wide-based, her weight poured down through the skirts into the plinth — a figure designed to the statics of a load-bearing column. Across the nave her opposite number, Inconstancy, flails on her rolling wheel, the one figure in the dado that cannot stand; it is no accident that Giotto, builder of campaniles, states constancy in an engineer’s terms: a structure is virtuous when it holds.

She looks right, out of the niche, toward the west — toward the direction from which, on the facing wall, the row of Vices runs down to Hell. The watch she keeps is oriented. In the dado’s long dialogue across the nave, Fortitude is the sentry of the Virtues’ line: first after Prudence has seen the danger, standing where the seeing must be held.

### Temperantia (Temperance)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-48.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a statue in a niche with a dark speckled ground. A woman stands frontally, mantled and veiled, her head slightly inclined with a faint smile. A thin strap runs across her mouth like a horse’s bit, tied back over her cheeks. In her hands she holds a sword still in its scabbard, point down, and winds a cord about the hilt, binding blade and sheath together. The inscription on the plinth below is faint, with scattered lines legible.">
  <figcaption>Temperantia (Temperance), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Virtues, south wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The third Virtue, opposite Wrath: a veiled woman wearing a bridle-bit in her own mouth, binding a sheathed sword fast with a cord — force kept, and kept unusable.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> [Hac virt]ute prediti / temperati moris / [eius legi] subditi / sun[t] in cunctis horis. / [Semper lin]gue, gustui / frenum sic imp[o]nunt; / [parcunt p]ravo actui, / manum tunc [c]o[m]p[on]unt.

“Those endowed with this virtue, temperate in conduct, are subject to its law at every hour. Thus they set a bridle always on the tongue and on the palate; they refrain from evil deeds, and so keep their hand composed.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Temperance is the only Virtue who disciplines herself in front of us. The others display their attributes; she performs hers, and the performance is double. In her mouth she wears a bit — the horse’s bridle transferred to the human face, the tongue reined like an animal — and in her hands she holds a sword that she is making unusable, winding a cord around hilt and scabbard until the blade cannot be drawn. Neither gesture destroys anything. The tongue remains; the sword remains; what the virtue adds is the fastening. Temperance, in Giotto’s analysis, is not the absence of force or appetite but their custody — power kept on purpose just out of its own reach.

The bridle is the image’s most audacious stroke, and its logic is scriptural: the Epistle of James on the tongue as the little member that defiles the whole body, the untameable thing “full of deadly poison” that only a bit can govern. Giotto takes the metaphor literally and straps it to a face — and then, with a subtlety that redeems the cruelty of the device, gives the face a faint smile. She is not muzzled; she has muzzled herself, and is content. The constraint is worn like an ornament, tied back over the cheeks as neatly as a ribbon.

Across the nave the pairing explains itself with the economy the dado has made its grammar. Ira grips her own garment and tears it apart; Temperance grips her own weapon and ties it shut. Both women act upon themselves — the dado’s two great reflexive figures — but one discharges and one fastens. Wrath’s head is thrown back, eyes shut against the Sun her verses say she stares down; Temperance inclines hers, eyes level, the posture of someone listening. Between the rending and the binding runs the nave, and the whole moral psychology of the programme: the same energy, governed or not.

The sheathed and bound sword casts a long shadow forward. Directly above this dado, in the Passion register, Peter will draw his blade in the garden and be told to put it up — the one Gospel scene where temperance and violence contest the same night — and the viewer who has seen this quiet woman winding her cord reads the arrest overhead with her gesture in mind. The recovered verses, alone in the dado, describe no figure at all: they speak of the virtue’s possessors — “those endowed with it are subject to its law at every hour,” bridling tongue and palate, refraining from evil and keeping the hand composed. The sword goes unmentioned; manum componunt, “they compose the hand,” is all the programme supplies, and the bound scabbard is Giotto’s own answer to what a composed hand might be doing. It is the quietest of the dado’s negotiations between text and image and fitting that it should fall to Temperance, the one virtue shown not as emblem but as practice.

### Iustitia (Justice)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-49.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. A crowned woman sits enthroned and frontal within an elaborate Gothic aedicule with a trefoil arch, pinnacles and panelled sides, against a blue ground — the one blue field among the Virtues’ niches. Her arms extend symmetrically, each hand supporting a shallow pan: on the pan at her right stands a small winged figure bending toward a seated man as if to crown or reward him; on the pan at her left a small winged figure raises a sword over a kneeling man. In a narrow predella strip below, small figures dance in a ring to music beneath trees while riders pass on either side.">
  <figcaption>Iustitia (Justice), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 60 cm. Dado of the Virtues, south wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The central Virtue, facing Injustice: a crowned queen enthroned in an intact Gothic aedicule against a blue ground, a living statuette in each outstretched pan of her balance — one crowning, one striking — and below, a predella of dancers and riders on the safe roads of her realm.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

>Equa lance cuncta librat / perfecta Iusticia: / coronando bonos, vibrat / ensem contra vicia. / Cuncta gaudent libertate / ipsa si regnaverit; / agit cum iocunditate / quisque quod voluerit. / Miles probus tunc venatur; / cantatur et luditur; / mercatori via da[tu]r: / p[re]do [t]u[n]c a[bs]c[on]ditur.

“Perfect Justice weighs all things with an even balance: crowning the good, she brandishes the sword against the vices. All things rejoice in liberty where she reigns; each pursues with gladness the work he chooses. The good soldier then goes hunting; there is song and play; the merchant is given the road: then the robber goes into hiding.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Justice is the dado’s queen, and Giotto builds her a palace. Alone among the fourteen figures she receives true architecture — a Gothic aedicule with trefoil arch, pinnacles and panelled flanks, intact to the last crocket — and alone among the Virtues she sits against blue: the ground behind her throne is blue, the sky-blue of the chapel itself breaking into the monochrome zone. The exception is doctrine. Blue is the colour of the vault overhead, of heaven’s order; where Justice reigns, the dado opens to the same sky under which the sacred histories unfold. Her counterpart across the nave sits in a gate with broken battlements, crags for jambs and a forest through the floor; the two niches state the political theology of the pair before either figure moves: rule is architecture, misrule is ruin.

Her balance is the image’s great invention, for there is no balance. No beam, no chains: her own outstretched arms are the instrument, a pan steadied on each open hand, and in the pans stand not weights but deeds. On one side a small winged genius bends to crown a seated man — reward; on the other a winged executioner raises a sword over a kneeling one — punishment. Justice does not weigh metal; she weighs persons, and the verdicts are enacted the instant they are measured. The conceit of living statuettes standing upon a statue is the dado’s trompe-l’œil raised to the second power: sculpture bearing sculpture, the allegory given moving parts.

Below her runs the proof. The predella — only she and her opposite have one — shows her realm at ground level: a ring of dancers moving to music beneath the trees, riders passing unarmed and unafraid along the road. The verses caption the strip line by line, and sharpen every figure in it. The riders are miles probus — the good soldier, who under Justice’s reign “goes hunting,” his sword given over to sport; the ring and the musician are cantatur et luditur, song and play; and the road itself has an owner: mercatori via datur, “the merchant is given the road” — in a banker’s chapel, the programme names the patron’s own class as the first beneficiary of good rule. Then the closing word answers the facing wall outright: predo tunc absconditur, “then the robber goes into hiding” — the very predo who, in the verses across the nave, “ranges free upon the road.”

Her verses are the best preserved of the dado — equa lance cuncta librat perfecta Iusticia, “perfect Justice weighs all things with an even balance,” crowning the good while her sword is brandished contra vicia, against the vices themselves, as if aimed across the nave at the painted row — and her position seals the programme’s symmetry: fourth of seven, keystone of her row as her enemy is of his, enthroned at the midpoint of the wall that carries, in the registers above, the life of the Virgin and the ministry of Christ. The pairing at the heart of the dado is the whole argument of the building in miniature: what is rendered to each, and what the rendering costs. In the Last Judgment on the west wall the arithmetic is completed at scale — and the viewer who stands before this niche has already seen, in two hand-held pans, exactly how that larger weighing will go.

### Fides (Faith)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-50.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a statue in a niche with a dark ground; two small sculpted busts occupy the upper corners. A woman stands rigidly frontal in a long mantle and a tall pointed cap, holding upright in her right hand a slender staff topped by a cross with a reddish tint, and in her left an unrolled scroll with lines of text. A key hangs at her waist. She stands on bare rock; beneath her feet lie a shattered figurine at the left and torn, overturned books at the right.">
  <figcaption>Fides (Faith), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Virtues, south wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The fifth Virtue, facing Infidelity: a frontal, hieratic figure in a pointed cap, cross-staff planted in her right hand, the scroll of the creed unrolled in her left, a key at her belt — standing on the smashed idol and torn books of the old error.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Figurata / Fide rata / presentatur homini / quod conscissa / manet fixa, / Christi [fide]ns nomini. / [Sanctam] Crucem / Christi ducem / amplectendo sequitur; / cuius actus / – va[l]e, tactus – / approbando loquitur. / Conculcavit, / subiugavit / ydola viriliter; / coronatur / et fundatur / supra petram firmiter. / Angelorum / supernorum / confortata nu[min]e, / manet recta / et perfecta / pro tanto solamine.

“By sure Faith here figured it is shown to man that, though rent, she remains fixed, trusting in the name of Christ. Embracing the holy Cross of Christ, she follows it as her guide; his acts she professes, approving them as true — and you, touch: begone! She has trampled, subjugated the idols manfully; she is crowned, and founded firmly upon the rock. Strengthened by the power of the angels on high, she stays upright and perfect by so great a support.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Faith is the dado’s most hieratic figure — and the rigidity is the meaning. While her sisters sit, stride, bind and fly, she stands in strict frontality, symmetrical as an icon, planted like the cross-staff she holds: the virtue of what does not bend. Giotto, who could set a statue spinning on a wheel when the allegory demanded it, here does the opposite and withdraws every trace of contrapposto. Faith is the fixed point; the whole figure is drawn with the plumb-line.

Her attributes read as a short creed. The staff in her right hand bears the cross — touched, almost alone in the niche, with colour, a reddish cast that the eye catches like a pulse — and plants it as one plants a standard on taken ground. In her left the scroll unrolls, inscribed: the symbolum, the articles of belief displayed open. The verses add the renunciation that makes the scroll sufficient: Faith professes Christ’s acts in words, *approbando loquitur* — and dismisses the lower proof outright, *vale, tactus,* “farewell, touch!” Across the nave, in the Passion register, the risen Christ holds the Magdalene’s fingers at bay; the dado has already stated the doctrine of that gap in two Latin words. At her belt hangs a key — the power of opening what belief alone unlocks. And under her feet lies the evidence of victory: a smashed figurine at one side, torn and overturned books at the other. The texts of the old error are not burned but trampled, kept beneath the heel as a pedestal of refuse — and the verses sharpen the irony: Faith herself, they concede, is conscissa, rent by her enemies, yet “remains fixed”; the torn one tears, and only her wounds stay closed.

The pairing with Infidelitas is the most completely engineered of the seven, prop answering prop across the nave: his idol upright on the palm, hers shattered at the foot; his scroll unread in God’s extended hand, hers unrolled and legible; his flame waiting at the hem, her cross raised like the thing that lit it; his helmet shutting out the call from above, her tall cap rising open toward it. Even the cord finds its answer in the key — he is haltered to what he made; she carries what opens what she did not. Two believers, the dado insists, not a believer and a sceptic: the question the wall poses is never whether to worship but what.

The pointed cap and long veil give her a sacerdotal cast that the verses tilt decisively toward Ecclesia: she is *coronatur*, crowned — the pointed cap a tiara — *et fundatur supra petram*, ‘founded firmly upon the rock’ of Matthew 16:18 — and the two small busts in the spandrels above her niche, unique in the dado, are named by the verses themselves: the *angeli superni* by whose power she is “strengthened” and “stays upright and perfect — by so great a support.” The rigidity is thus given its doctrine in the text: Faith stands plumb because she is held — the one statue in the row whose stillness is declared to be assistance. Her verses below have worn faint. The figure holds the doctrine without them: one hand grounded, one hand open, the wreckage of the alternative underfoot — belief painted not as feeling but as stance.

### Karitas (Charity)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-51.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a statue in a niche with a dark ground. A young woman stands turned slightly to the right, looking up, her hair dressed with a crown of flowers from which three small red flames rise — one at either side and one above the crown. In her lowered right hand she balances a wide bowl heaped high with flowers and fruits; her left arm stretches up to the corner of the niche, where a half-figure of God leans down to take the small heart she holds out. Beneath her feet lie sacks and drawstring purses, trodden flat.">
  <figcaption>Karitas (Charity), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 55 cm. Dado of the Virtues, south wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The sixth Virtue, facing Envy: a young woman crowned with flowers, small flames at her head, a bowl heaped with offerings in one hand while the other reaches up to hand her heart to God — her feet on the money-bags she has refused.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Hec figura Karitatis / sue sic proprietatis / gerit formam. / Cor, quod latet in secreto, / Christo dat: hanc pro decreto / servat normam. / Set terrene facultatis / est contempt[r]ix, vanitatis / color aret. / Cuncta cunctis liberali / offert manu, spetiali / zelo caret.

“This figure of Charity bears thus the form of her properties. The heart, which lies hidden in the inmost self, she gives to Christ: this is the rule she keeps for law. But of earthly wealth she is the despiser; the colour of vanity withers. All things to all she offers with a liberal hand, and knows no partial zeal.”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

Charity is the dado’s one unbroken circuit of giving. Everything about her moves in two directions at once: the right hand holds the heaped bowl down and open toward the world — *cuncta cunctis*, say the verses, “all things to all, with a liberal hand,” and with no partial zeal: love that does not shortlist — the left stretches up and surrenders her heart to the God who leans from the corner of heaven to take it. Love of neighbour and love of God, the two commandments on which the law hangs, assigned to the two hands of one body — and nothing kept between them. Even her head burns outward: from the crown of flowers rise three small flames, touched with red — one to either side and one at the summit, a number no medieval eye would read as accident on the brow of the virtue that binds the theological three.

For the pairing across the nave is the fiercest in the dado, organ for organ. Envy hoards; Charity heaps and offers. Envy’s free hand crooks into a claw toward what others have; Charity’s lifts her own heart away. Envy’s fire rises from below and devours her; Charity’s sits lightly on her brow and crowns her. And at the figures’ feet the argument closes with the dado’s driest stroke of wit: the purse that Envy throttles in her fist lies here on the ground, trodden flat — money-bags underfoot, the one place in a banker’s chapel where coin is shown exactly where the programme wants it, beneath the heel of love. Enrico Scrovegni, whose fortune those purses figure, knelt daily between the two women; the dado leaves no doubt which one the building was built to resemble.

The exchange at the top of the niche is the tenderest passage in the whole monochrome zone. God appears half-length, as he does across the nave to be ignored by Infidelity — but here the extended hand finds its answer: the little heart passes from her fingers toward his, the smallest object in the dado and its whole point - and the verses say why it is small: the heart is quod latet in secreto, “what lies hidden in the inmost self,” the one gift no witness can audit, which the painter has done the impossible and shown in transit.

One verse keeps its secret: *vanitatis color aret*, “the colour of vanity withers.” Whether the phrase has a painted referent — a bloom gone dry in the garland, the deadened tone of the trodden purses — or states a law without an image, the wall must answer; either way it is the dado’s most painterly line of Latin, a programme that thinks in pigment.

She is young, garlanded, almost a spring figure — the only Virtue who smiles upward — and her verses below, worn like her sisters’, open by presenting the figure and her attributes in the dado’s now-familiar manner of the self-describing image. No description was ever less necessary. A bowl held down, a heart held up, money underfoot and fire that harms nothing: the virtue that the whole chapel exists to purchase, painted as if it cost nothing at all to give everything away.

### Spes (Hope)

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-52.jpg" alt="Grisaille fresco by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, imitating a statue in a niche with a dark porphyry ground. A winged young woman rises diagonally in profile, her whole body airborne, feet trailing above the plinth, gown streaming behind her. Both arms stretch up at full length toward the upper right corner, where a small winged half-figure of an angel leans from the frame holding out a crown. Old incised graffiti — small figures and devices scratched by past visitors — are faintly visible in the dark ground behind her. On the plinth below, lines of the Latin titulus remain partly legible.">
  <figcaption>Spes (Hope), c. 1303–1305. Fresco in grisaille, 120 x 60 cm. Dado of the Virtues, south wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The last of the Virtues, opposite Despair and beside the Last Judgment: a winged woman rising clean off the earth, arms at full stretch toward the crown held out to her from the corner of heaven — the only figure in the dado in flight.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Spe depicta sub figura / hoc signatur, quod mens pura / spe fulcita non clausura / terrenorum clauditur; / set a Cristo coronanda / sursum volat: sic beanda / et in celis sublimanda / fore firma redditur.

“By Hope depicted in figure this is signified: that the pure mind, upheld by hope, is not shut within the prison of earthly things; but, destined to be crowned by Christ, it flies upward: thus, destined to be blessed and raised on high in heaven, it is rendered firm […].”
*Text: G. Ammannati, “Pinxit industria docte mentis. Le iscrizioni delle allegorie di Virtù e Vizi dipinte da Giotto nella Cappella degli Scrovegni”, Pisa, Edizioni della Normale, 2017.*

The row of Virtues ends as the row of Vices does, beside the Last Judgment — and with the same rupture of the sculptural fiction, inverted. Despair hangs: a statue with its weight given to the rope. Hope flies: a statue with no weight at all. Giotto closes the two files of fictive marble with the two states no marble can hold, a body pulled below the plinth and a body lifted off it, and sets them facing each other across the nave like the two ends of a scale the whole dado has been weighing.

Everything in the figure is vector. She rises in pure profile along the diagonal, gown streaming back, wings half-spread, both arms thrown up at their fullest stretch — the posture not of prayer but of reach, every joint of the body enlisted in one direction. And the direction has an object: from the upper corner of the niche an angel leans out of heaven, lowering the crown toward her fingertips. The gap between hand and crown is the panel’s entire theology. Hope is not possession; the crown is promised, visible, and not yet hers — *spes quae videtur non est spes*, hope that is seen is not hope — and Giotto measures the doctrine in the few painted inches her reach has still to close. The verses conjugate the same doctrine: *coronanda… beanda… sublimanda*, three gerundives in a row — “destined to be crowned, to be blessed, to be raised” — hope’s own tense, the future passive of what is promised and not yet possessed. The grammar hangs in the air exactly as the figure does.

The crowning angel belongs to a traffic the dado’s upper corners have conducted all along the wall: God leaning out to call the unbeliever, God taking Charity’s heart — and now the crown. The verses assign it higher: *a Cristo coronanda*, the mind is “to be crowned by Christ” — yet the painter sets an angel in the corner, bearing what Christ awards. It is the dado’s last negotiation between programme and brush, and the most doctrinally exact. The south wall’s upper angles are a narrative of their own.

She is the last thing the two files of the dado say before the west wall says everything. The dark ground behind her bears the scratched graffiti of centuries of such visitors, small incised figures and devices left where the wall promised most; defacement, but of an eloquent kind — generations reaching toward the same corner. The programme could not have ended otherwise: after thirty-eight histories and thirteen of the fourteen moral statues, the final figure before the Judgment is the one that has already left the ground.

## The Scrovegni Chapel: The Last Judgment

The west wall is the only wall in the Scrovegni Chapel that the visitor cannot avoid facing twice: once on entering, when it stands behind them unseen, and once on leaving, when the whole painted universe they have just walked through turns out to have been flowing toward it. Every register of the chapel runs from the altar to the door — Joachim's story, the Virgin's, the ministry, the Passion, the two files of Virtues and Vices — and all of them discharge onto this single surface, painted around the real triple-lancet window and above the real exit, where the cycle's last scene and the building's actual threshold occupy the same wall. Completed around 1306, after the consecration of March 1305, the Judgment was the chapel's final campaign of work as well as its final word: the counter-façade finished last, closing the book it ends.

### The Last Judgment

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-53.jpg" alt="Fresco by Giotto filling the west wall of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, around a real three-lancet window above the door. At the centre Christ sits enthroned within an oval mandorla rimmed with rainbow bands of light, in a rose-red robe, his right hand opened downward in welcome and his left turned away; twelve haloed apostles sit on either side on a long row of thrones. Above them, two dense phalanxes of armed and winged angels fill the upper wall, and at the top corners two figures roll back the sky itself like a scroll, revealing jewelled gates behind at either corner. Four angels sound trumpets, two beneath the window and two beside the mandorla. From below the mandorla a river of fire pours down to the right into Hell, a dark chaos where a huge grey-blue Lucifer devours the damned amid demons, serpents and small tortured figures. On the left, ranks of the blessed and haloed elect rise in ordered processions, with the dead emerging from the earth at the lower edge. At the centre bottom, two angels support a tall cross, beside which a kneeling layman in violet offers a model of the chapel to three haloed figures, assisted by a religious who bears the building on his shoulder.">
  <figcaption>The Last Judgment, c. 1306. Fresco, c. 1000 x 840 cm. West (counter-façade) wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The entire inner façade of the chapel, around and above the entrance door and its triple-lancet window: Christ the Judge in a rainbow mandorla between the enthroned apostles and the celestial militias, the elect rising at his right, Hell unrolling from the river of fire at his left, and, at the foot of the great cross, Enrico Scrovegni offering the model of the chapel itself.</figcaption>
</figure>

Giotto accepts the wall as he finds it, triple-lancet window and all: the real Gothic opening becomes part of the picture’s architecture, a breach of actual daylight in the painted heaven, and the composition closes around it as if the window were the one part of the Judgment that could not be depicted because it is the light itself.

The scale is hierarchy made visible. Christ in his mandorla is vast — larger than any figure in the chapel, larger than whole crowds of the resurrected below — and the wall is ordered by sheer size: apostles smaller than the Judge, angels smaller than apostles, mankind smallest of all.

Around him, heaven is an army and a court. Twelve apostles flank the mandorla on a continuous bench of thrones, six to a side, the jury of Matthew 19:28 “judging the twelve tribes”; above them the angelic cohorts mass in depth, rank behind rank of haloes receding into the blue, with the archangel-captains armoured at their head and standards — white gonfalons charged with red crosses, the Resurrection banner multiplied into an army’s colours — lifted over the host. Four angels blow the trumpets of the summons — two beneath the window, aimed left and right along the wall, and two at the mandorla’s flanks, aimed steeply down toward the opening earth — the horns of 1 Corinthians 15 sounding in both axes, across mankind and into its graves. And at the very top, in the corners of the vault, two angels roll up the sky, a detail that will repay its own page.

Below the Judge the wall divides, and the division is the picture. From beneath the mandorla, on Christ’s left, a river of fire bursts out and pours diagonally down the wall, dividing as it falls into four branches — the *fluvius igneus* of Daniel 7:10 issuing from the throne, parted like the single river of Eden into four heads (Genesis 2:10), paradise’s geography inverted into irrigation by fire — and everything it touches is Hell: a darkened chaos without ranks, architecture or horizon, where the damned tumble among demons, serpents and punishments sorted by sin. Hell, too, will have its page. On Christ’s right, by contrast, all is order: the elect rise in calm processions, rank upon haloed rank led upward by angels, while at the lower edge the earth gives up its dead — small bodies climbing from fissures in the ground, some still half-buried, the resurrection as a landscape waking.

And at the centre of the lowest zone, where the two destinies almost meet, Giotto sets the wall’s hinge: the great cross, borne upright on a stand by two angels, planted between Paradise and the river of fire like a boundary-stone — the instrument of the Passion displayed as the title-deed of the whole proceeding. Beside it, on the side of the blessed, kneels a man in violet: Enrico Scrovegni, offering a scale model of this very chapel. The dedication, the heart of the chapel’s whole argument, takes its own page.

Read from the nave, the wall completes every promise the cycle has made. The two men in white of the Ascension said he would return as he was seen to go: here he is, in the same glory, come back along the same trajectory. The *Desperatio* and *Spes* of the dado posed the choice in two statues; here the choice is executed upon all flesh. And then, below the whole tremendous accounting, stands the real door, with the real light of the window above it. The visitor leaves through the wall on which they have just been judged — passing under Enrico’s offering, between the trumpets, out of the painted universe at the one point where Giotto let the actual world shine through.

### Christ the Judge in the Mandorla

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-54.jpg" alt="Fresco detail by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Christ sits enthroned at the centre of an oval mandorla whose rim is built of overlapping scale-like bands — fiery orange-red outside, a white leaf-garland band, blue-green within — against an inner field of golden rays. He wears a rose-red robe with a gold-embroidered neckband; a slit in the fabric at his right side reveals the wound beneath. His bearded face, framed by a radiate golden halo bearing three small dark circular insets, turns toward his right; his right arm extends with the palm opened downward in welcome, the nail wound visible in it and in both bare feet, his left hand turns its back outward in refusal. Dimly visible within the mandorla, the four living creatures flank the throne, the lion, eagle and ox still recognisable. Outside the rim, files of haloed angels press close on either side, and at the lower flanks two angels sound long trumpets aimed downward; at the lower right the river of fire begins.">
  <figcaption>Christ the Judge in the Mandorla, detail of The Last Judgment, c. 1306. Fresco. West wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The centre of the Judgment: Christ enthroned within a rainbow-rimmed mandorla of light, his right hand opened to the elect and his left turned away, the side wound shown through a slit in his rose-red robe — and in his halo, the housings of the small convex mirrors discovered in the restoration of 2001–02.</figcaption>
</figure>

The Judge is built out of light. His mandorla is not the usual almond of cloth-of-gold but an engine of optics: three concentric bands of overlapping scales — fire-coloured without, a wreath of white leaf-strokes between, cold blue-green within — that read, from the nave, as a single iridescence, a rainbow bent into a ring. The source is exact: around the throne of the Apocalypse “there was a rainbow… like unto an emerald” (*iris erat in circuitu sedis*, Rev 4:3), and Giotto takes the verse literally, seating his Judge inside the iris itself. The choice carries its own mercy, for the rainbow is also Genesis’s sign of the covenant after the flood — the promise that destruction would not be total — so that the frame of the Last Assize is, on inspection, the oldest emblem of restraint in Scripture. Within the ring, gold rays stream from the figure; and dimly, flanking the throne inside the glory, the four living creatures of that same verse keep their stations about the throne — the lion, the eagle and the ox still legible in the worn red of the glory, the man all but faded — Revelation’s tetramorph attending the Judge from inside his own light, as they attend the Evangelists whose books this Judgment executes.

The body is the verdict. No scales, no book, no sword: Christ judges with his anatomy. The right arm reaches down and open, the palm turned out in the oldest gesture of reception; the left hand turns its back on its own side of the wall, fingers dropping, a door closing. His face — stern, wide-eyed, nothing of the Passion’s suffering left in it — is set toward his right, toward the saved: even his attention is a sentence, and the damned fall outside it. The chapel has schooled its viewer in this grammar of hands for thirty-eight scenes — hands that seal a bribe, bind a sword, offer a heart — and here the grammar reaches its final clause: one body, opened on one side and shut on the other, the fork in the road made flesh.

And the flesh shows its credentials. At the Judge’s right side, Giotto has cut a slit in the rose-red robe — not a fold but an opening in the garment itself — through which the lance wound is displayed. The Judge presents the evidence of his own execution: *videbunt in quem transfixerunt*, “they shall look on him whom they pierced” (John 19:37, after Zechariah), the verse that medieval exegesis assigned precisely to the Second Coming. The wound seen through the parted cloth is the wall’s legal exhibit, and it binds the Judgment to the register the viewer has just walked: the body glorified here is the body flogged, nailed and lamented one wall away, and the robe is slit so that no one can mistake the continuity. Hands and feet carry the rest of the evidence: the nail wounds are plainly shown on the open right palm and on both bare feet — the welcoming hand welcomes through its wound, and the gesture of reception is also the exhibit of the price.

The halo holds the wall’s most astonishing secret. Three small circular insets sit in the radiate gold around his head — and the restoration of 2001–02 identified them: housings for tiny convex mirrors of real glass, set into the plaster so that the Judge’s nimbus would catch the chapel’s actual light and return it, glittering, to the nave. The painted rays are supplemented by genuine reflection; of all the figures in the building, only this one was engineered to shine. And their placement is itself iconography: the three insets sit where the arms of a cruciform nimbus would lie — above the brow and at either side — so that the cross traditionally painted into Christ’s halo is here constituted by light itself, three points of real radiance standing in for the painted sign. On the west wall, above the real window, the man who built his heaven out of ultramarine reserved for its king the one material that is not paint.

Around the rim, heaven crowds as close as the fiction allows: files of haloed angels press against the mandorla’s edge on either side, cheek to the light, like courtiers against a throne-rail, while at the lower flanks two of their number aim long trumpets downward past the ring — the summons blown along the wall’s lower zones, where the earth is giving up its dead. And at the lower right, directly from beneath the glory, the river of fire takes its source: damnation, like salvation, flows from the same seat of light. The mandorla touches, at its base, both of the wall’s destinies; the Judge sits where the two roads actually part, and the rainbow around him holds the balance of the whole tremendous wall: fire on its outer band, the cold of deep water within, and between them a garland of white leaves, the victor’s wreath, binding the extremes.

### The Dedication of Enrico Scrovegni

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-55.jpg" alt="Fresco detail by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. A young clean-shaven man in a violet-mauve robe and white cap kneels in sharp profile facing right, his right hand touching the façade of a pink scale model of the chapel, his left raised toward three haloed figures standing above it. The first of the three, in cream over red, extends her right hand downward toward him; the second stands between in red with gold embroidery; the third, wrapped in a green and gold brocade mantle, rests a hand on the model's roof. The model shows the chapel's gabled façade with its portal and trifora window, and the long flank pierced by six small lancets and two larger ones. Behind it kneels a tonsured cleric in a white habit, both hands raised to steady the building on his shoulder. Dark blue ground; at the upper left, the wing and drapery of an angel supporting the great cross.">
  <figcaption>The Dedication of Enrico Scrovegni, detail of The Last Judgment, c. 1306. Fresco. West wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. At the foot of the angel-borne cross, on the side of the blessed: Enrico Scrovegni, in profile and at full sacred scale, kneels to offer the model of this very chapel to three haloed figures, the Virgin among them, while a tonsured cleric bears the building's weight on his shoulder — a donor portrait of unprecedented rank: a lay banker at the centre of a Last Judgment.</figcaption>
</figure>

The donor group kneels at the base of the cross, on the side of the blessed — and the audacity lies not in the type of the image but in who performs it, and where. A living layman, portrayed from the life, occupies the Last Judgment itself, at the right hand of the Judge, at the same scale as the heavenly figures he addresses: only the kneeling marks his lower station, not, as convention long demanded, a diminished body. Giotto grants him the sharpest profile portrait Italian painting had yet produced — young, clean-shaven, the hair dressed in the fashion of the day under its white cap, the robe a penitent violet — and the face is the most carefully individualised in the chapel.

It is also, remarkably, one of three. The building holds Enrico thrice: painted here at the height of his enterprise; carved as the standing statue now in the sacristy; and laid, grown old, as the effigy on his tomb in the choir. The statue bears the inscription *Propria figura domini Enrici Scrovegni militis de l'Arena* — "the very likeness of lord Enrico Scrovegni, knight, of the Arena" — a wording of striking secular self-assertion, attributed by some to the sculptor Marco Romano; and Laura Jacobus has argued that fresco and statue are "facsimile portraits," their exactness fixed from casts taken from life. Whatever the technique, the programme of likeness is itself the message: a man determined that his face, and not merely his name, should survive — and the chapel obliges him at three ages of one life.

What he offers is the building the viewer is standing in. The model is painted the rose-pink of the chapel's real brick, and it is drawn with a draughtsman's intelligence that [Giovanna Valenzano](/scholars.html#valenzano-giovanna) has described as virtually an axonometry before its time: façade, flank and eastern parts gathered into a single legible image, reviving the convention of the church-models in Rome's early medieval mosaics and renewing it with a builder's precision. The portal, the trifora above it — faithful to the façade the visitor has just entered through — and the flank with its six small lancets and two larger: the model can be checked against the wall it hangs on, and the front checks out exactly. It is the east end that does not. The little church in Enrico's hands preserves a stage of the project — before the protest of the neighbouring Eremitani in January 1305 forced its ambitions down, the same contraction that may have left the painted *coretti* beside the chancel arch as ghosts of unbuilt spaces. Its value, then, lies doubly: as the emblem of a gift, and as a document — the one surviving elevation of the chapel as first intended.

The offering is received by three. That the transaction belongs to the Virgin is beyond doubt — the chapel is hers, dedicated to Santa Maria della Carità and consecrated on her feast of the Annunciation — and the central figure is usually identified as Mary, her companions read in various ways, as personifications or as saints associated with the double dedication; the question remains open in the scholarship. What no reading disputes is the choreography: the foremost figure bends her hand down toward Enrico's, her companion on the far side lays a hand flat on the model's roof — heaven taking delivery — and the donor's own fingers still touch the façade: the moment of transfer held forever incomplete, the chapel forever passing from his hand into theirs. Behind the model, bearing its whole weight on his shoulder, kneels the scene's second portrait: a tonsured cleric in a white habit, hands braced against the little building, face turned toward the group that receives the gift. Tradition reads him as an Augustinian; attempts to name him remain conjectural. His office in the composition, though, is lucid: the layman gives, the Church carries — patronage and custody, the purse and the burden, with the harder posture given to the cleric.

One claim, however, must be firmly qualified: this is not the first donor to offer a model of his church, and the priority sometimes asserted for it should not be repeated. The type had a long history. Bishop Ecclesius holds a model of San Vitale in the sixth-century apse mosaic at Ravenna; Pope Paschal I offers his churches in the ninth-century apses of Santa Prassede and Santa Cecilia in Rome; Byzantine founders appear in the same act — Theodore Metochites, a decade later at the Chora in Constantinople, would present his church to Christ in the same tradition. Chiara Frugoni has observed that this iconography belonged properly to popes and sovereigns — and there the novelty of Padua stands exposed. What is new is not the type but the rank and the setting: a lay banker, neither ruler nor prelate, appropriates the gesture of popes, places himself at the centre of a Last Judgment, among the saved, in a likeness apparently intended as exact. Not the first donor portrait — something stranger: the moment the formula of sovereigns passed into the hands of money.

The gesture completes a figure the chapel has been drawing all along. Across the nave on the dado, *Charity* hands her heart up into the hands of God — the smallest gift in the building — and here, at scale, her motion returns as the donor's: a man handing upward the largest gift he has, the building itself. The two images gloss each other across the church: the heart is what the chapel means, the chapel is what the heart built. Whether one reads the scene as restitution for the family's usury, as the tradition long held, or — with the more recent scholarship — as the self-presentation of a man purchasing memory, status and salvation in a single instrument, the wall states his wager with perfect candour: he has painted himself into the verdict he hopes for, and hung the receipt where no one can miss it.

### Hell

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-56.jpg" alt="Fresco detail by Giotto in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Against a nearly black ground, branches of a broad orange-red river of fire sweep down from the upper left, carrying naked falling figures; grey winged demons seize the damned along its banks. At the centre sits a giant grey-blue horned Lucifer, two serpents issuing from his ears, a soul disappearing into his mouth, another gripped in his fist, while two dragons at his flanks each devour a victim and a figure emerges beneath him. Around him the damned suffer in groups: figures strung up from poles and beams, some hanging by the hair or by hooks, a row suspended from a horizontal bar at the right; at the left, three figures hanged together from a great tree-trunk, a money-bag hanging with them; a pale disembowelled figure in a white garment hangs alone; in the lower corners demons drive and drag crowds of the naked. At the upper left, on firm blue ground, a white-robed angel supports the great cross.">
  <figcaption>Hell, detail of The Last Judgment, c. 1306. Fresco. West wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The lower right quadrant of the Judgment as the viewer faces it, at Christ’s left hand: the four branches of the river of fire deliver the damned into a dark chaos where a colossal grey-blue Lucifer devours and voids the souls, demons sort the sinners punishment by punishment — and three usurers hang together from a great tree-trunk, the money-bag hanging beside them — in the chapel a banker built.</figcaption>
</figure>

Hell is the only place in the chapel without architecture. For thirty-eight scenes Giotto has housed every event — loggias, temples, porticoes, the fictive marbles of the dado, heaven itself ranked like a court — and here, at the end, he paints what the absence of all that looks like: no ground line, no horizon, no building, no rank. The quadrant is a darkness into which the four branches of the fire-river pour from the throne above, each delivering its freight of small naked figures head-first; the damned are the tiniest humans on the wall, the hierarchy of scale run to its bottom, and their world has no shape except the one the fire gives it in passing. Order, the wall argues, was always a grace; this is matter with the grace withdrawn.

At the centre of the shapelessness sits its parody of a king. Lucifer is the largest figure on the wall after the Judge — the symmetry is the sentence — but where Christ is rose and gold inside a rainbow, the adversary is the grey-blue of a drowned body, bloated, horned, enthroned not on light but on dragons that feed at his sides. And he is all intake. A soul vanishes into his mouth; his fists clamp others for their turn; two serpents issue from his ears — the organ by which Envy, on the dado, “probed all things,” here breeding its own hunters — and the two dragons of his throne each work at a victim of their own; beneath him, a figure emerges — swallowed and voided, the old Byzantine conceit of the devil as digestion, consumption without nourishment, forever. The Judge above speaks with two hands, one opening and one refusing; Lucifer’s only speech is the mouth, and the mouth only eats. He is the final term of a sequence the chapel has been building: Envy devoured by her own fire, Despair strangled by her own hand, and at the bottom of the wall the appetite itself, enthroned.

Around him, the chaos turns out to have one law: the punishment studies the sin. The damned hang — from poles, beams and hooks, singly and in rows — and they hang by something: by the hair, by the tongue, by the organ that offended, each body suspended from the member that led it here. It is the logic the age called the contrapasso a decade before Dante gave the word its immortality — and gave this very Hell its strangest afterlife, for when the Inferno reached Padua, readers found Reginaldo Scrovegni, Enrico’s father, seated among the usurers of the seventeenth canto with his purse at his neck. Giotto’s wall came first. The poet sharpened what the painter had hung: Giotto strings the purse beside the men, Dante ties it to their necks and blazons it with arms — the azure sow of the Scrovegni among them.

For the purse is here. At the left, three usurers hang together from a great tree-trunk, and with them hangs the bag itself, beside its owners. Giotto hangs the money as a fellow felon, strung from the same wood as the men it ruined — and with its appearance the chapel’s longest thread arrives at its knot.

The quadrant’s last cruelty is geographic. Hell occupies the lower right of the Judgment as the viewer faces it — which is to say, it hangs directly beside the door. The crowds of the damned in the lowest registers, driven and dragged by demons toward the dark, are the figures nearest the visitor’s own body at the moment of leaving; one step through the doorway passes within arm’s reach of the harvest. On the other side of that step, around the corner, Hope flies toward her crown. The chapel’s whole teaching compresses into the width of its own exit: Enrico’s offering above the lintel, the fire at the right shoulder, the winged mind at the left — and the visitor, for one step, standing exactly where the wall has spent itself insisting that everyone stands.

### The Angels Rolling Up the Sky

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-57.jpg" alt="Two fresco details by Giotto from the upper corners of the west wall of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua, mirror images of each other within the curved spandrels of the vault. In each, a haloed angel in a soldier’s fringed cuirass, with dark wings, stands on white cloud and grips the edge of the blue sky, rolling it back in a great curling volute whose inner face is rose-red; behind the retreating sky appears a tall golden door-leaf studded with vertical bands of gems — red and blue stones and rows of pearls. The left angel is seen full-length with booted feet; the right is half-hidden behind the roll, the lower body lost. Above each spandrel runs the chapel’s painted border of acanthus, on red at the left and on blue at the right.">
  <figcaption>The Angels Rolling Up the Sky, details of The Last Judgment, c. 1306. Fresco. Upper corners, west wall, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. In the two spandrels at the summit of the Judgment, an armoured angel takes the firmament by its edge and furls it back like a scroll — sicut liber involutus, the heaven of Revelation 6:14 — exposing the red reverse of the sky and, behind it, the gem-studded gates of the city that needs no sun.</figcaption>
</figure>

This is where the chapel ends — not at its door but at its own undoing. In the two highest corners of the Judgment, tucked into the spandrels where wall meets vault, Giotto paints the verse that closes the visible universe: "the heaven departed as a scroll when it is rolled together" (Revelation 6:14, *sicut liber involutus*). An angel at each corner has taken the firmament by its hem and is furling it back, calmly, gripping it with one hand only, as lightly as a workman taking down a hanging — the universe needing, at the end, no more force than that.

For the detail's deepest shock is chromatic. The inside of the roll — the back of the sky — is red. The blue that has arched over every scene in the chapel, the blue of the vault's starry barrel, of thirty-eight histories, of the Judgment itself, turns out to have a reverse side, rose-red like the lining of a garment, and Giotto shows it. No painter had thought the thought before, and few have dared it since: the firmament rendered as a made thing with a front and a back, a tent-cloth — "who stretchest out the heavens like a curtain," says the Psalm, and here the curtain comes down and we see the side that always faced away from us. And the curtain carries its lamps away with it. Beyond the frame of this detail, in the left spandrel’s blue, burns the sun — a disc of real gold leaf, radiate with fine spiked rays, its core now darkened; in the right floats the moon, a pale disc bearing a human profile; and around them, faint in the retreating blue, small stars. Sun to the left, moon to the right, as they hang at the sides of every Crucifixion — but here they are not witnesses: they are freight. Revelation orders the sequence exactly so — sun stricken, moon changed, stars fallen, then the sky departing as a scroll (6:12–14) — and Giotto compresses it by furling the luminaries inside their own vault, the lights put away with the ceiling they hung from. It is the most radical sentence in the building: what can be rolled up was never the final reality.

What the rolling reveals is the reality. Behind each retreating volute stands a tall golden door-leaf, studded floor to summit with vertical bands of gems — red stones, blue, rows of pearls — the gates of the New Jerusalem, whose walls are "garnished with all manner of precious stones" and whose twelve gates are twelve pearls (Revelation 21). The city needs no sun and no firmament, "for the glory of God did lighten it": the sky was only ever the veil over its doors. Giotto's economy here is absolute — no city skyline, no crowds of the glorified, just the jewelled jamb glowing where the blue has been peeled away, enough to say that heaven was behind the sky the way a wall is behind a tapestry, all along, at arm's length.

The angels themselves belong to the army below. Each wears a soldier’s scalloped cuirass with its fringed skirt — field dress of their own, distinct from the parade armour of the militias massed around the mandorla below: not the ranks drawn up for the Judge, but the two sent ahead on the last fatigue-duty, the decommissioning of the cosmos. Giotto gives them no exultation and no strain; they stand on the white clouds and work, faces turned out toward the nave with the wide-eyed gravity of sentries, as if rolling up the universe were a task like striking a tent at the end of a campaign — which, in the wall's theology, is precisely what it is. The campaign is the one the viewer has just walked: thirty-eight scenes fought under this blue, from Joachim's expulsion to the empty tomb, and now the theatre itself is being cleared.

And the self-reference bites deepest here, at the seam between wall and vault. The furled sky is starred: painted stars still spot the blue of both spandrels — most surviving only as star-shaped ghosts in reddish earth, their finish fallen like so much of the chapel’s a secco work — and these spandrels touch the chapel’s actual ceiling, the barrel of deep blue sown with gold stars that Giotto stretched over the whole building. The angels furl, then, not a generic heaven but this one: the same starred blue the visitor stands under, taken down at its own edge. The painter of the coretti, who opened fictive chapels in a solid wall, closes his work with the inverse illusion — the dismantling of his own grandest fiction, performed by figures who look the viewer in the eye while they do it. Every illusionist conceals his machinery; Giotto, in the last corners of the Arena Chapel, rolls his back and shows the red underside — and behind it, not the bare plaster, but the doors.

## The Vault, the Framing System and the Cosmology of Blue

### The Starry Vault

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-58.jpg" alt="Photographs looking straight up at the barrel vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. A field of intense blue is covered with regular rows of gold eight-pointed stars, bordered by ornamental painted bands with quadrilobe busts and foliage above the uppermost register of scenes; iron tie-rods cross the vault, and grey patches mark losses of the blue. In the centre of the field a round medallion holds the blessing Christ within a feathered rainbow ring, with four smaller prophet medallions at the corners.">
  <figcaption>The Starry Vault, field of the blessing Redeemer, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The chapel's ceiling: a barrel of deep ultramarine sown with ranks of gold eight-pointed stars, divided into two fields; this one carries the blessing Redeemer at the centre, attended by four prophets — the painted heaven under which the entire cycle unfolds.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-64.jpg" alt="Photographs looking straight up at the barrel vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. A field of intense blue is covered with regular rows of gold eight-pointed stars, bordered by ornamental painted bands with quadrilobe busts and foliage above the uppermost register of scenes. In the centre of the field a round medallion holds the frontal Madonna and Child within a flat gold band, with four smaller prophet medallions at the corners, whose white scrolls flutter out of their roundels into the blue.">
  <figcaption>The Starry Vault, field of the Madonna and Child, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The chapel's ceiling: a barrel of deep ultramarine sown with ranks of gold eight-pointed stars, divided into two fields; this one carries the Madonna and Child at the centre, attended by four prophets — the painted heaven under which the entire cycle unfolds.</figcaption>
</figure>

Everything in the chapel happens under this blue. The vault is a single barrel of ultramarine sown with gold stars in disciplined ranks — not a naturalistic night but a diagram of heaven, the ordered firmament of the medieval cosmos — and it is the largest continuous field of colour Giotto ever painted. The blue is the building's binding agent: it pours down from the vault into the ground of every narrative scene, so that Joachim's hillside, the Virgin's chamber and Calvary all open onto the same sky, and the chapel reads as one world under one weather.

Technique explains both the splendour and the wounds. The blue is azurite laid a secco over the finished plaster — the only way to keep the mineral's depth, for lime destroys it — and what a secco gives in intensity it pays in fragility: the grey patches that map the vault today are the places where the azurite has fallen, the same mortality that took the spears of the *Road to Calvary*, the tituli of the dado and the stars of the furled sky. The eight-pointed stars survive better, gilded constellations in rows — and their eight points are no idle choice, for eight is the number of resurrection and of the world to come, the octave beyond the week of creation: a heaven counted out in the arithmetic of eternity. The iron tie-rods cross the sky without apology — the building's structural candour left on view, as everywhere in a chapel that never pretends its marble is not paint without also proving that paint can be anything.

Ten medallions interrupt the stars, and they turn the ceiling into a genealogy. The barrel is divided into two fields, and at the centre of each a great clipeus: the blessing Redeemer in the field toward the sanctuary — his blessing falling, among all things, on Enrico’s tomb in the choir below — and the Madonna and Child in the field toward the door, facing down the length of the nave toward the west wall where Enrico offers her this very building. The chapel’s two dedicatees at the two poles of its sky, each stationed over the transaction that concerns them: the Son above the grave that trusts him, the Mother above the gift addressed to her. Every bust floats on gold; but the two courts are framed differently, and the difference is a programme. Around the Redeemer, feathered rings of graded colour, light bent into a wreath; around the Madonna, flat goldsmith's bands of pseudo-script, chevron fillets and quadrilobe medallions — the treasury rather than the spectrum. Heaven's optics for the Son's court, heaven's metalwork for the Mother's; and the prophets of both turn their heads and lift their hands toward their centres — the ceiling's own conversation, conducted in glances across the stars.

The two courts even speak differently. The four seers around the Redeemer — three nameless elders and the Baptist, knowable only by his camel-hair — carry no legible word: one holds a scroll of veiled pseudo-script, venerable and unreadable, the old tongue as texture. The four around the Madonna unfurl Latin that any cleric below could read, their white scrolls breaking out of the clipei and fluttering loose into the starred blue: Isaiah's virgin who shall conceive, Baruch's God seen upon earth, Malachi's Lord come suddenly to his temple, Daniel — the one figure in the whole sky who bears his own written name — with the kingdom that shall not be destroyed. Around the Word himself, the word stays veiled; around the Mother, whose mystery was argued from texts, the texts themselves are published — the Incarnation's dossier filed in the sky about its instrument.

The form has a long memory. The clipeus — the portrait in a round shield, borne aloft — is antiquity's device for the honoured dead carried into apotheosis, and this chapel has quoted it before: the *imago clipeata* carved in the tympanum of the house of Anna, one register down. Here the device returns at full scale and in its true element, the honoured of both covenants raised into the painted ether. And the whole arrangement states the chapel's doctrine at a glance, before a single scene is read: above every event of the cycle — annunciations, betrayals, burials — the same fixed order holds, Christ and his Mother enthroned in the firmament, the prophets attending with their proofs, the stars in their ranks. History happens below; the vault is what history is *for*.

### The Blessing Redeemer

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-59.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Bust of Christ on a gold ground within a circular border of overlapping feather-like strokes shading from orange-gold to white. He wears a red tunic with an embroidered gold neckband and a blue mantle over his left shoulder; his right hand is raised in blessing with two extended fingers, his left supports a grey-green book with bossed studs and a central stamped rosette. His halo is radiate gold with a dark inscribed cross. Four gold eight-pointed stars occupy the blue corners outside the ring; an old vertical crack runs through the tondo.">
  <figcaption>The Blessing Redeemer, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The central clipeus of one of the vault's two fields: Christ half-length on gold within a feathered rainbow ring, a cross inscribed in his radiate halo, blessing with the right hand and holding a studded, rosette-stamped book in the left, among eight-pointed stars.</figcaption>
</figure>

At the summit of the chapel's heaven, Christ appears as the Pantocrator of the oldest tradition: frontal, hieratic, half-length on gold, the right hand raised in the Greek blessing and the left presenting the book. It is the one image in the building that belongs to the icon rather than to the story — no episode, no before or after, the Christ who simply *is* — and Giotto places it exactly where such a Christ belongs, at the zenith, looking straight down.

The painting of it, though, is anything but archaic. The ring is the vault's finest piece of optics: row upon row of overlapping feather-strokes, orange deepening to gold and breaking into white, the same graded iris that Giotto will enlarge into the Judge's rainbow mandorla on the west wall — so that the visitor who looks from vault to counter-façade sees the same glory at two scales, the icon of heaven and its enactment at the end of time, rimmed with one light. The halo carries the cross inscribed dark on its radiate gold — the sign the Judgment's halo will translate into three points of real mirror-glass — and the book in his hand is a piece of pure still-life: grey-green boards, bossed studs, a stamped central rosette, a binding so particular one could order its twin from a Paduan stationer. Even at the apex of abstraction, Giotto's Christ holds a real object.

The red tunic and blue mantle are the Redeemer’s livery in the vault — the red recurs in the robe he wears in the mandorla of the Judgment — and the four eight-pointed stars that attend the tondo's corners tie the clipeus into the firmament around it: this is not a picture hung on the sky but a presence surfacing through it. The feathered ring reads, from the floor, as a wreath of wings — as if the medallion were borne, like the antique clipeus it descends from, by bearers who have become pure light.

And the gaze completes the architecture. The prophets in the surrounding tondi turn their heads and hands toward him; he alone looks neither left nor right but down, out, at whoever stands beneath. In a chapel where every wall tells, the ceiling watches.
