The visitor who follows the spiralling registers from Joachim to Pentecost walks, the whole time, under this vertical regard — the blessing falling from the zenith on every scene at once, on the Golden Gate and on Gethsemane equally, the hand raised over the betrayal as over the kiss. The walls take sides and take turns; the vault, like heaven, is simultaneous.

### A Prophet with a Scroll

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-60.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length figure of an old man on a gold ground within a feathered ring shading red to white with green inner edge. He has a long white beard and balding white hair, a radiate gold halo, and wears an orange-red mantle over a blue tunic whose collar carries a band of pseudo-script embroidery. Both hands hold an unrolled scroll inscribed with lines of illegible pseudo-writing; his head turns in three-quarter view, eyes raised to his right.">
  <figcaption>A Prophet, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. One of the eight prophet-clipei attending the central tondi: a white-bearded seer in an orange mantle over blue, holding an unrolled scroll of pseudo-script, his gaze lifted across the stars toward the centre of his field.</figcaption>
</figure>

The prophets of the vault are the cycle's silent chorus. Eight of them ring the two central clipei, and Giotto builds them all on one scheme — half-length on gold, feathered iris, radiate halo — and then differentiates them entirely by temperament: this one is the reader. He holds his prophecy open with both hands, the scroll unrolled across his chest, and looks up from it toward the centre of the field — the glance travelling from the written word to its fulfilment enthroned among the stars. The gesture is the whole theology of the arrangement compressed into a turn of the neck: prophecy is text until it looks up.

The scroll itself is lettered in pseudo-script — ordered lines of characters that resemble writing and resolve into none — and the same invented alphabet runs as embroidery along his collar. The device is deliberate and double. The illegibility is historical tact: these are the words of the old covenant, Hebrew to the eye of a Paduan congregation, venerable and veiled at once — script as texture, like the pseudo-Eastern patterns Giotto wove into the Temple's hangings in the *Bringing of the Rods*. And it is also doctrine: the prophecy, by itself, cannot quite be read. Its meaning is not on the scroll but in the direction of the prophet's eyes.

Without an inscribed name — none of the four seers around the Redeemer carries one — identification is a game the tondo declines to settle; the attributes are generic by design, the seer as office rather than individual. What Giotto individualises is the face: the long cascade of white beard painted strand over strand, the raised brows, the mouth just parted — a particular old man caught in the general act of recognition. The feathered ring around him grades from deep red through rose to white with a cold green inner edge: each prophet's iris is tuned to its own chord, eight variations on the rainbow that no two tondi repeat exactly.

### A Prophet in a Red Mantle

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-61.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length figure of an old man on a gold ground within a feathered white-and-pink ring. He is seen in near profile facing left, with long grey-white hair falling to his shoulders and a full white beard; a worn radiate halo surrounds his head. He wears a red mantle clasped at the chest over a dark tunic; his right hand is raised open beside his face, his left rests at his breast.">
  <figcaption>A Prophet, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. One of the eight prophet-clipei: an aged seer wrapped in a red mantle, in near profile, the right hand lifted open in the orator's gesture — prophecy as speech, turned toward the centre of the starry field.</figcaption>
</figure>

If the scroll-bearer is the vault's reader, this is its speaker. No text, no attribute at all: only the body's oldest rhetoric — the profile set hard toward the centre of the field, the right hand lifted open at the height of the face, palm out, the *adlocutio* of the antique orator passed down to the prophets of Christian art. He addresses. Across the painted sky, over the ranks of stars, the old man is forever in mid-sentence toward the clipeus at the centre — and the open hand is aimed, with him, along the line of his own gaze, so that gesture and glance arrive together.

The profile is the choice to notice. Giotto reserves the pure profile, throughout the chapel, for figures locked onto an object — Enrico before the Virgin, the Judge's averted face, the kiss at the Golden Gate — and here it gives the prophet the urgency of a man who has seen something and turned bodily toward it. Nothing else in the tondo competes: the red mantle falls in broad, quiet folds, the left hand lies at the breast, the halo's radiance has worn thin on the gold.

The anonymity, as throughout the series, is structural: no name survives, and the office outweighs the man. But the pairing of types is Giotto's commentary on prophecy itself. One seer holds the word written; this one performs the word spoken — *scriptura* and *vox*, the two modes in which the old covenant reached its hearers — and the vault seats them in the same heaven, equally feathered in light, equally turned toward the same fulfilment. The chapel below will stage the consequences of both: scrolls held open in the Temple, voices crying in the wilderness.

He hangs among the stars like what he is: a direction. Stripped of book and emblem, the tondo reduces the prophetic office to its grammar — an old man, a turn, a raised hand, and the centre of the sky for an object. The congregation beneath, following the hand, performs the exegesis with its own eyes.

### A Prophet with Raised Hands

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-62.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length figure of a man on a bright gold ground within a feathered ring of deep red shading to white. He has short greying hair, a high forehead and a trimmed grey beard, with a worn radiate halo. He wears a violet-red mantle over a blue-grey tunic, its edges and central band richly bordered in gold embroidery; both hands are raised open at chest height, and his head turns in three-quarter view, eyes lifted to his right.">
  <figcaption>A Prophet, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. One of the eight prophet-clipei: a seer in a violet-red mantle hemmed with broad gold embroidery, both hands raised toward the centre of the field — prophecy as adoration, the seer become witness.</figcaption>
</figure>

The third temperament of the vault's chorus is neither reading nor speaking: it is beholding. This prophet raises both hands, open and empty, at the height of his chest — the immemorial posture of the *orans*, older than the Church itself, the body's acknowledgment before something that exceeds address — and lifts his eyes along the same diagonal, toward the clipeus at the centre of his field. The scroll-bearer verifies; the orator proclaims; this man simply sees, and his two raised palms are the sight registered in flesh, the gesture of one who has stopped arguing.

Giotto dresses him the most richly of the three: a violet-red mantle whose hems and central band carry broad gold-embroidered orphreys, almost ecclesiastical in their splendour — the woven gold catching the tondo's own gold ground, as if the prophet's clothing had begun to take the colour of what he looks at. The face, high-browed and trim-bearded, is younger than his companions'; the radiance of the halo has worn to a ghost on the gold, as throughout the series, the a secco rays surrendering while the leaf endures.

The eight tondi are not eight portraits but one office refracted; and the refraction is arranged so that every mode, text, voice and vision, converges by gaze and gesture on the same two clipei, Christ and his Mother, enthroned in the ridge of the sky.

Among the ordered stars, his open hands have one further office. They are the vault's own instruction to the viewer, hung directly overhead: this — the lifted face, the open palm, the suspended argument — is the posture the ceiling asks of whoever stands beneath it. The prophets model the congregation they float above; and of the three temperaments, it is the beholder, not the reader or the speaker, whom the visitor most resembles, neck bent back, hands idle, eyes raised to the centre of a painted heaven.

### St John the Baptist

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-63.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length figure of a man on a gold ground within a feathered ring of dusky rose shading to white with a blue-green inner edge. He has long dark hair falling past his shoulders, a dark beard and moustache, and a radiate halo worn to traces on the gold, which is spotted with small losses. He wears a shaggy camel-hair tunic under a rose mantle with a gold-bordered hem; his right hand is raised in blessing, his left gathers the edge of his mantle.">
  <figcaption>St John the Baptist, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The one nameable figure among the vault's eight satellite clipei: the Forerunner in his camel-hair tunic under a rose mantle, blessing with one hand and gathering his mantle with the other — the hinge between the prophets' heaven and the Gospel unfolding on the walls below.</figcaption>
</figure>

One figure in the vault's chorus needs no name because he wears his biography. The shaggy camel-hair tunic, the dark unbarbered hair: this is the Baptist, the only one of the eight satellite clipei whom the attributes identify beyond argument — and his presence among the prophets is itself the point. He is the office's terminus: "the law and the prophets were until John" (Luke 16:16), the last of the old chorus and the first voice of the new, and Giotto seats him in the ring of seers as their closing member, the prophet who did not foretell the Lamb but pointed at him across a river.

His gesture splits the difference between his companions'. Not the scroll, not the orator's address, not the orans' double surrender: the Baptist blesses — the two-finger benediction that is almost Christ's own in the central tondo — while the other hand gathers the edge of his mantle. Among the eight he is the only one who wears his biography.

And he is the chapel's quiet second patron of continuity. The visitor has met him on the north wall, gaunt in the Jordan, handing the story to his cousin through the water of the *Baptism*; here he presides over that same handing-over from the sky, hung in the firmament directly above the register where he acts in time. Prophet on the ceiling, protagonist on the wall: no other figure of the vault leads this double life.

The tondo has suffered: the gold ground is spotted with losses, the halo's rays survive as shadows. But the rose of the mantle still answers the dusky rose of his feathered ring, the ring's cold green inner edge still sets off the gold, and the face — dark, grave, unkempt — still carries the wilderness into the ordered heaven. Among the white-bearded elders of the chorus he is conspicuously the youngest and the wildest, desert amid the stars.

### The Madonna and Child

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-65.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length frontal figure of the Virgin on a gold ground, wearing a rose veil over a dark blue mantle, her radiate gold halo behind the veil. She holds the Child on her left arm, her right hand laid across her body toward him; the Child, in a pale gold-hemmed tunic with a radiate halo, sits frontally, one hand at his breast. The ring is a broad gold band ornamented with lines of pseudo-script, crossed by paired chevron fillets and set with four quadrilobe medallions at the cardinal points. Gold eight-pointed stars surround the tondo in the blue.">
  <figcaption>The Madonna and Child, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. The central clipeus of the vault's second field: the Virgin frontal with the Child on her arm, both gazing straight down, within a broad gold ring banded with pseudo-script and quadrilobe medallions — the chapel's dedicatee enthroned in its sky, ringed by the four prophets of the Incarnation.</figcaption>
</figure>

The second summit of the chapel's heaven belongs to its dedicatee. In the clipeus at the centre of the vault's other field the Virgin appears with her Son — and like the Redeemer in the facing field, she is given in the icon's mode, not the story's: strictly frontal, hieratic, both faces turned straight down at whoever stands below. This is not the Mary of the walls — the girl who climbed the Temple steps, the mother who met her son's eyes over the Calvary crowd — but the Theotokos of the oldest formula, the God-bearer presented as a fact of heaven.

The pairing of gazes is the tondo's quiet power. The Child does not turn to his mother nor she to him; both look down, out, together — four eyes vertical on the visitor, doubling the regard that the Redeemer casts alone from the facing field. Her right hand crosses her body toward him, the vestige of the Hodegetria's pointing gesture — "the way" indicated — calmed here into something more like custody; his small hand lies at his own breast. Nothing happens. In a building where her life is told in six scenes and her grief in a dozen more, the vault grants the Virgin the one thing narrative cannot: permanence.

The ring sets her apart as much as the pose. Where the Redeemer and his attendant prophets float in feathered irises of graded colour, the Madonna's clipeus — and those of her four prophets — wears architecture: a broad flat band of gold laid with lines of pseudo-script, crossed by chevron fillets, and set at the four cardinal points with quadrilobe medallions, the same quadrilobe that frames the small busts of the chapel's ornamental borders. The pseudo-script running round her ring is the vault's now-familiar veiled language, the unreadable venerable, here promoted from a prophet's collar to a frame for the Queen.

And the court around her is chosen with doctrinal precision. The four prophets of her field — alone in the vault — carry legible Latin on fluttering scrolls. The visitor below stands, in effect, inside the fulfilment: above them the promise in four texts, around them on the walls the promise kept, scene by scene, from the Golden Gate to the empty tomb.

### The Prophet Isaiah

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-66.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length figure of an old bald-crowned man on a gold ground, within a ring of red bands bearing pseudo-script, white chevron fillets and quadrilobe rosettes. He has a long forked white beard and a radiate gold halo, and wears a rose mantle clasped over a crimson tunic with an embroidered collar. His right hand is raised open beside his face; his left holds a long white scroll that passes behind his shoulder and falls, unbroken, out of the tondo’s frame, inscribed in Gothic letters with the opening of Isaiah 7:14.">
  <figcaption>The Prophet Isaiah, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. One of the four prophets attending the Madonna's clipeus, identified by his scroll: Ecce virgo concipiet et pariet filium… (Isaiah 7:14) — the Annunciation's own prophecy, in the chapel consecrated on the Annunciation's feast.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Ecce v[ir]go [con]cipie[t] / et pari|et filiu[m] | et voca|bit no[men eius…] — "Behold, a virgin shall conceive and bear a son, and shall call his name [Emmanuel]" (Isaiah 7:14).

Of all the texts the old covenant could send to this ceiling, this is the chapel's own. *Ecce virgo concipiet*: the verse read at the Annunciation, the feast on which the chapel was consecrated on 25 March 1305 (a first dedication is probable for the same feast in 1303) — and the event painted at the summit of the chancel arch, at the sanctuary end over which the vault’s other field presides. The prophet who speaks it is Isaiah by his own words, and Giotto gives him the fire the words deserve: bald-crowned, fork-bearded, the right hand flung up beside his face in the orator's heat, a man announcing rather than musing. The prophecy of the virgin birth is delivered, in this sky, at full voice.

The scroll performs the tondo's boldest act: it escapes. Unrolling from the prophet's left hand, the white band of text drops past the ring and out of the clipeus altogether, fluttering loose into the starred blue — the word overflowing its medallion, prophecy refusing the frame. All four prophets of the Madonna's field share the device.

The verse is cut off exactly where every medieval reader could complete it: *…vocabit nomen eius* — and the name, Emmanuel, "God with us," stays off the scroll, left for the viewer to supply — and for the clipeus at the centre of Isaiah’s own field to depict, where the Child sits on his mother’s arm: the prophet unrolls the promise a few feet of sky from its keeping. The amputated citation is the vault's theology of reading in miniature: the prophecy ends where the fulfilment begins, and the fulfilment is not written but painted, a few feet of sky away.

Isaiah's station in the Marian field is the anchor of its programme. His conception-text opens the sequence that Baruch, Malachi and Daniel continue. And his gesture, the lifted open hand of proclamation, repeats across the vault's two fields in the anonymous orator of the Redeemer's court: the same rhetoric, there unnamed and unlettered, here signed by the most quoted sentence in prophetic literature. The vault, like the chapel, moves from the veiled to the explicit — and Isaiah is where it chooses to speak plainly.

### The Prophet Baruch

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-67.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length figure of an old man on a gold ground, within a ring of red pseudo-script bands, white chevron fillets and quadrilobe rosettes. White hair frames his head like a cloth, over a full grey-white beard; his radiate halo is worn. He wears a salmon-pink mantle with gold-embroidered borders over a red tunic, and points a finger at his chest with his right hand while his left grasps a white scroll that unrolls out of the tondo, inscribed in Gothic letters.">
  <figcaption>The Prophet Baruch, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. One of the four prophets of the Madonna's field, identified by his scroll: Hic est Deus noster… (Baruch 3:36) — the verse whose continuation, "afterward he was seen upon earth, and conversed with men," made Baruch a prophet of the Incarnation.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Hic ē D[eu]s n|oster, et nō | [estimabit]ur a[lius…] — "This is our God, and there shall no other be accounted of [in comparison of him]" (Baruch 3:36).

Baruch is the quietest of the four, and carries the most audacious text. *Hic est Deus noster* — "this is our God" — points, in its scriptural home, toward the verse that follows: *post haec in terris visus est, et cum hominibus conversatus est*, "afterward he was seen upon earth, and conversed with men." No sentence in the old books sounded more like the Gospel before the Gospel, and the Middle Ages prized Baruch for it above all his other words: the scribe of Jeremiah promoted, on the strength of one verse, to eyewitness-in-advance of the Incarnation. Giotto's prophet holds the demonstrative itself — *hic*, "this one" — on a scroll that flutters out of his medallion toward the sky where, one clipeus away, the God in question sits on his mother's arm.

He alone of the four lays a finger to his chest while the other hand unrolls the scroll — the word kept within and the word published, as if the prophet held his whole corpus shut and had chosen the one sentence worth unrolling. The pairing makes him the Marian field's archivist: where Isaiah proclaims, hand in the air, Baruch presents — the book hugged, the citation extended, a scholar's economy of gesture.

The painting matches the temperament. The face is the mildest in the vault's chorus: white hair lying close about the head like a cloth, the beard full and soft, the eyes level rather than lifted — a reader's gravity, not a seer's fire. The salmon mantle with its gold-embroidered borders falls in the broad quiet folds Giotto reserves for his elders, and the ring around him repeats the Marian field's goldsmith-work: red bands of pseudo-script, chevron fillets, quadrilobe rosettes — the unreadable ornament framing the readable word, the vault's two languages in one circle.

In the field's four-text programme, Baruch holds the centre of the argument. Isaiah promises the conception; Baruch names its consequence — God on earth, among men — and his verse is the sequence's hinge: the moment the prophecies stop describing an event and start pointing at a person. *Hic* — the shortest word on the vault, and the one on which the whole ceiling turns.

### The Prophet Malachi

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-68.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length figure of an old man on a gold ground, within a ring of red pseudo-script bands, white chevron fillets and quadrilobe rosettes. He is balding with short grey hair and a grey-white beard, his radiate halo on worn gold; he wears a blue mantle over a green tunic with a gold-embroidered collar. His right hand is raised before his chest, the forefinger pointing upward; his left holds a white scroll that unrolls downward out of the tondo, inscribed in Gothic letters.">
  <figcaption>The Prophet Malachi, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. One of the four prophets of the Madonna's field, identified by his scroll: Et statim veniet… (Malachi 3:1) — "the Lord whom you seek shall suddenly come to his temple," the prophecy of the Presentation painted twice on the walls below.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> Et stati[m] / veniet a[d templum suum…] — "And [the Lord, whom you seek,] shall suddenly come to his temple" (Malachi 3:1).

Malachi is the last voice of the written prophets, and the vault gives him the last word's privilege: the verse of arrival. *Et statim veniet ad templum suum Dominator quem vos quaeritis* — "and the Lord whom you seek shall suddenly come to his temple" — is the text the liturgy fixed to the feast of the Presentation, Candlemas, the day the forty-day-old Christ was carried into the sanctuary and old Simeon knew him. The chapel below has painted that arrival twice over: the Virgin presented in the Temple in her register, Christ presented in his — both under the same painted ciborium — and Malachi hangs in the sky above them holding the sentence that both scenes obey.

His gesture is the subtlest of the four. No orator’s flourish, no book: the right hand rises before the chest with one finger extended upward — toward the Lord who is to come, along the axis of his own text — while the eyes, heavy-lidded, look out from under their brows with the weariness of the long watch. The scroll falls, the finger climbs: between the two hands the prophecy’s whole motion is drawn, the word descending to the temple below, the Expected still above. *Quem vos quaeritis*, “whom you seek” — Giotto paints the seeking in the face, the suddenness in nothing at all; of the Marian field’s four, Malachi is the prophet of patience, the arrival announced by the man who looks as if he waited longest for it.

The scroll performs the field's signature escape, unrolling past the ring into the starred blue, and its truncation is as pointed as Isaiah's: the text breaks off at *ad* — "to…" — with the temple itself left off the parchment. The destination, like Emmanuel's name, is supplied by the building: the viewer who looks down from this tondo finds the painted Temple on the walls, ciborium, steps and all. The vault cites; the chapel completes.

Within the four-text catechism of the Madonna's court, Malachi supplies the third movement: conceived (Isaiah), come among men (Baruch), brought to his temple (Malachi) — before Daniel's kingdom closes the sequence in eternity. And there is a fitness in his presence that a Paduan canon would have relished: Malachi's chapter continues, past the painted citation, into the purifying of the sons of Levi and the judgment on those who oppress for gain — the fire of the refiner, two verses downstream from this scroll, waiting for the same wall that holds the usurers. The vault quotes the arrival; the west wall, in due course, paints the refining.

## The Scrovegni Chapel: The Decorative Bands

The Scrovegni Chapel keeps a second cycle in its margins. The painted bands that frame the narrative walls — fictive Cosmatesque inlay, candelabra, veined marbles, a whole architecture of stone pretended in pigment — are studded throughout with small openings, and through each opening a figure or a scene looks out. The system has three populations. Along the Gospel registers, quatrefoils hold miniature histories: Old Testament and bestiary scenes stationed beside the New Testament events they foreshadow. Along the upper registers of Joachim and the Virgin, and across the three ornamental arches of the starry vault, the openings hold busts instead — the crowned company of the holy, elders and youths, attending without acting. And at the end of the north wall, past the last history, the margins show their own makers: evangelists at their desks, one composing, one rereading. Taken together, the frames constitute the chapel's commentary — the glosses written, as in a manuscript, in the border of the text — and no ornament before them had been asked to think so hard.

The narrative quatrefoils are the system's scholarship, and they work in two directions at once. Horizontally, each panel glosses its neighbours with a precision the sources themselves dictate: Moses striking water from the rock between the *Baptism* and the *Marriage at Cana*, under Paul's own caption — "that Rock was Christ"; Jonah swallowed between the *Crucifixion* and the *Lamentation*, the one sign Christ claimed for himself; the bestiary lion breathing his still-born cubs to life on the third day, posted at the *Lamentation*'s other side — so that the tomb-scene is bracketed by its own before and after, the descent and the rousing, and the Resurrection the register never paints is held, twice over, in the frame. The Creation of Adam stands beside the raising of Lazarus, first creation beside second; Elijah's fiery chariot beside the *Ascension*, the servant conveyed beside the Lord who simply rises; Moses receiving the Law beside the *Pentecost*, the two fiftieth days, stone tablet and tongues of fire. Vertically, the same bands build columns through the masonry: Moses's spring stacked over Jonah's deep, Adam's animation over the lion's breath, and — finest of all — the succession of the prophets in two storeys, Elisha gripping the master's mantle on the last road above, and below, the chariot climbing, the mantle streaming from Elijah's hand toward the young disciple who reaches up from the lobe's edge. The two readings lock each panel into place like a stone in bond; and across them all one gesture recurs — Isaac's arm toward the face above the knife, Adam's toward the Creator's hands, Elisha's toward the descending cloth — the reach that does not yet touch, the margins' own figure for how promise is held.

The system runs on rules, and they can be stated like a grammar. The histories are compressed to their nouns — two figures and a withheld knife, a prophet reduced to two kicking legs, an old man, a rod, a rock — with no attendants, no architecture, nothing the four lobes do not require. The haloes obey Scripture rather than rank: the Hebrew actors go uncrowned — Abraham, Jonah, Moses at the rock, the prophets of the succession — and a nimbus kindles only where the text itself kindles one: the Creator, who wears Christ's face and Christ's cruciform crown at the making of Adam and again at Sinai; Moses on the mountain alone, his face alight from the audience; Elijah, the one man of the old covenant who does not die. Past the Pentecost the demography turns: the evangelists are crowned by office, and the bust-bands of the upper registers and the vault halo every head — for those figures stand not in history but in glory, and the difference between the two band-systems is the difference between the road and the arrival. Even the frames keep the hierarchy: plain quatrefoils for the histories, barbed and flowered ones for the crowned, and a lapidary family — fictive green marble, gemmed lozenge-crosses — for certain young saints set into the wall like medallions into a binding.

The bust-bands give the commentary its congregation. Beside the Birth of the Virgin, in Joachim’s register and beside the Virgin’s wedding, single saints and pairs of elders attend; along the vault’s arches, the crowned processions of the just are strung between scrollwork from which winged figures grow. These presences act hardly at all — a blessing, a pointed finger, a staff — and their anonymity is their eloquence: the genealogy of salvation was, for the Middle Ages, precisely a crowd, and the margins render the crowd as it experiences itself — present, crowned, and mostly unnamed.

The sources, counted, are a complete medieval library: Genesis for the creation and the covenant, Exodus for the water and the Law, Kings for the succession and the fire, the prophets for the deep, the bestiary for the breath — the two testaments and the book of nature, each opened at the page the adjacent wall requires. And the system closes by disclosing itself. At the seam where the north wall meets the Judgment, the last quatrefoils hold the evangelists at their desks — the young one with pen lifted, waiting on the next word; the old one reading back what the pen set down — the chapel's own sources shown in production, a wall's width from the court where, in the Apocalypse's words, the books will be opened. Every gloss presupposes a text; here, at the end of the margins, the text's makers appear, and the building admits its nature: a chapel built like a page — histories in the block, commentary in the borders, the scribes still at work in the binding, and the reader standing in the middle, glossed on every side.

### The Prophet Daniel

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-69.jpg" alt="Fresco tondo by Giotto in the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Half-length figure of a beardless young man on a gold ground, within a ring of red pseudo-script bands, white chevron fillets and quadrilobe rosettes. He has dark hair curling to the jaw, a radiate halo on darkened gold, a gold collar at his neck, and wears a pale blue mantle over a dark gold-patterned tunic. His right hand is raised with pointing finger; his left holds a long white scroll that unrolls downward out of the tondo, headed DANIEL and inscribed with further Gothic lines.">
  <figcaption>The Prophet Daniel, vault tondo, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. One of the four prophets of the Madonna's field — and the only figure in the vault who bears his own name, written at the head of his scroll: DANIEL, above the words of Daniel 6:26 on the living God whose kingdom shall not be destroyed.</figcaption>
</figure>

Titulus:

> DANIEL / ip[s]e e[st] e[nim] / Deus vive[n]s / et [aeternus(?)] [in] / s[e]cla; [et re]/gn[um eius] no[n] / dissipab[itur…] — "Daniel. For he is the living God, enduring for ever; and his kingdom shall not be destroyed" (Daniel 6:26).

Daniel is the vault's exception three times over. He is young — beardless, dark-curled, hardly more than the youth the lions refused — among a chorus of white beards; he is dressed like a prince, gold collar at the throat, gold-figured stuff under the pale blue mantle, the courtier of Babylon rather than the hermit of the desert; and he alone, in the whole ceiling, is *named*. At the head of his scroll, before the prophecy begins, stands the word DANIEL — the one proper name written anywhere in the vault's sky, as if the youngest and least canonical-looking of the seers required credentials his elders could carry in their faces.

The text below the name is not, strictly, his own. *Ipse est enim Deus vivens… et regnum eius non dissipabitur* — "he is the living God… and his kingdom shall not be destroyed" — comes from the decree of Darius after the lions' den (Daniel 6:26): a pagan king's confession, extracted by the prophet's survival. The choice is exquisite. The other three scrolls carry what prophets said about God; Daniel's carries what his life made an empire say. His prophecy is testimony at one remove — the kingdom proclaimed indestructible by the very power that could not destroy its witness — and it supplies the catechism’s final clause: *et regni eius non erit finis*, as the chapel's own feast-day Gospel has Gabriel say of the child, in words that lean on Daniel's.

His pointing hand, raised with one finger extended, aims the decree across the stars toward the clipeus of the Mother and Child — the kingdom's heir on the kingdom's lap — and his scroll, longest of the four, cascades out of the tondo in a white fall of lines, the field's device of escape at its most extravagant. For medieval exegesis Daniel was also, more quietly, Mary's prophet in another figure: the stone "cut out of the mountain without hands" that became a great kingdom (Daniel 2) was read for centuries as the Child from the Virgin, conceived without man — so that his very presence in her court carries a second, unwritten citation under the written one.

He closes the vault's census: ten clipei, two icons, eight attendants — four nameless around the Son, four documented around the Mother, and of the documented, one signed.

### The Circumcision

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-70.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua. Within a quatrefoil frame set in a square panel of fictive Cosmatesque mosaic — red-and-white triangular tesserae at the corners, pale foliage sprays in the spandrels — a balding elder with grey hair and beard, wrapped in a mauve-pink mantle, bends his head over a naked infant lying across his arms. His right hand is lowered toward the child’s body, the blade itself not shown; the infant reaches one arm up toward the old man’s face, without touching it. Bands of fictive marble inlay flank the panel; fragments of adjacent quadrilobe panels appear above and below.">
  <figcaption>The Circumcision, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. One of the small quatrefoil scenes set into the painted ornamental bands that frame the narrative cycle: a bearded elder bends over a naked infant held against his side, the blade scarcely visible, while the child reaches up toward his face — the rite of the eighth day, compressed to two figures and one act.</figcaption>
</figure>

This panel shows the rite of the eighth day. An elder bends over a naked infant laid across his lap, his head dropped close, his right hand lowered to the child’s body, his left steadying the small raised leg. The blade itself is nowhere to be seen: Giotto states the act by posture alone — the bent neck, the working hand, the held leg — and spares the viewer the steel, in a panel a few inches high where steel would have shouted. The economy is total: no temple, no attendants, no mother, nothing but the two figures the rite requires, locked into the quatrefoil like a seal into wax. And within that economy Giotto spends his tenderness where he always does, on the exchange the ritual does not require: the child’s free arm reaches up toward the old man’s face — toward the beard, the nearest graspable thing in his world — not touching it, forever arriving. Trust and wound in the same small frame: the whole panel balances on that arm.

Who the child is, the quatrefoil declines to say — and the reticence is its office. Read beside the old covenant scenes of the bands, this is the circumcision of Isaac, whom Abraham circumcised “when he was eight days old, as God had commanded him” (Genesis 21:4): the first son of the promise marked with the first covenant’s sign. Read toward the walls, it is the circumcision of Christ, the one event of the infancy that the great cycle omits — Luke sets it, eight days after the Nativity, before the Presentation in the Temple, and the main register steps directly from Bethlehem to the Magi and the Temple without it. The quatrefoil supplies the missing day in miniature; and the two readings are not rivals but the panel’s very mechanism, for typology is precisely the claim that the first reading contains the second. Isaac’s eighth day and Christ’s are one figure: the margin holds the hinge.

But the pairing the bands intend reaches further than the infancy. For Paul, circumcision was the type not of another circumcision but of baptism — “circumcised with the circumcision made without hands… buried with him in baptism” (Colossians 2:11–12) — and the old exegesis sharpened the figure through Abraham’s two sons: Ishmael and Isaac, the child of the flesh and the child of the promise, the circumcision of the body and the circumcision of the spirit, which is the font. If this quatrefoil stands, as its doctrine suggests, in the orbit of the Baptism of Christ, the margin performs its office perfectly: beside the Jordan, where the new covenant’s water replaces the old covenant’s knife, the bands post the knife itself — the eighth day of Isaac answering the thirtieth year of Christ, blood giving way to water across the width of a painted frame. Even the knife withheld belongs to the argument: the wound of the figure yields to a sacrament that wounds nothing.

### The Lion Reviving His Cubs

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-71.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a full vertical strip of fictive ornament. At the centre, a quatrefoil opens onto a scene: a tawny lion, ribs showing, stands in profile and lowers his maned head, mouth open, over a group of small cubs lying on pale rocky ground — one stretched flat, the others lifting their muzzles toward him — against a blue sky with a tree now darkened to near-black. Above and below the quatrefoil run interlaced bands of fictive Cosmatesque inlay with triangular mosaic panels, and lozenge fields of red, white and blue acanthus scrollwork close the strip at top and bottom.">
  <figcaption>The Lion Reviving His Cubs, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. North wall, lower register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between the Lamentation and the Noli me tangere: the lion of the bestiaries bends over his cubs, born dead, to wake them on the third day with his breath — the figure of the Resurrection, stationed at the exact seam where the cycle leaves the Resurrection itself unpainted.</figcaption>
</figure>

Between the *Lamentation* and the *Noli me tangere* the cycle holds its breath. The register's most important event happens in the gap: the left scene ends with the dead Christ in his mother's arms, the right opens with the tomb already empty and the angels already seated on its lid — the Resurrection itself, following the Gospels' own silence, is never shown. And precisely there, in the painted band that separates the two scenes, a quatrefoil opens onto a lion bending over his cubs. The margin does what the register declines to do: it paints the raising — not as history but as figure, the event entrusted to an animal's breath at the seam between death and the garden.

The story comes from the bestiaries. The *Physiologus* and its medieval descendants open with the lion, and the third of his natures was the one every reader remembered: the lioness bears her cubs dead, and watches them so for three days, until on the third day the father comes, breathes into their faces — some texts say roars over them — and wakes them to life. The moral followed at once: so did the almighty Father, on the third day, raise his Son from the dead. Giotto paints the legend with a naturalist's economy — the lion's lowered muzzle open above the litter, one cub still stretched flat, the others already stirring, lifting their heads toward the breath — the resurrection caught in mid-act, some flesh still asleep, some already answering. Behind the group a tree rises against the blue, now oxidised to near-black — almost certainly green once, like so much of the chapel’s a secco vegetation: a living tree over the waking litter, the panel’s quiet second figure of life returning, dimmed by the centuries to a shadow of itself.

Scripture had prepared the figure long before the bestiaries moralised it. Jacob's blessing calls Judah "a lion's whelp" who couches and sleeps, "and who shall rouse him?" — *quis suscitabit eum?* (Genesis 49:9), the Vulgate reaching for the very verb of resurrection — and the Apocalypse answers across the whole of Scripture: "the Lion of the tribe of Judah has conquered" (Revelation 5:5). The lion of the quatrefoil is thus doubly the Christ: the cub who sleeps and cannot be roused by any other, and the sire whose breath rouses — the Son in the tomb and the Father above it, one animal carrying both offices, as the legend itself requires.

The placement sharpens every detail. A step to the right, in the *Noli me tangere*, the risen Christ holds the banner this panel glosses: VIC-TOR MOR-TIS, victor over death — and the bestiary's lion is exactly that victory in its creaturely dress, the breath of the third day painted a hand's width from the empty sarcophagus. A step to the left, the *Lamentation* shows the cub phase of the figure: the body stretched flat, the watchers bent over it, the three days begun. The quatrefoil between them is the hinge the two great scenes turn on — grief on one side, the garden on the other, and in the margin the mechanism: the Father breathes, and what was borne dead wakes.

It also enlarges the bands' library. The *Circumcision of Isaac* glossed the walls from the old covenant's histories; this panel glosses them from the book of nature — the bestiary, where creation itself was read as a second scripture, every beast a sentence about its maker. And there is a last mercy in the choice. The great walls of this register are unsparing — the kiss, the mockery, the nails, the wailing over the body — but the Resurrection, when the margin finally supplies it, arrives in the gentlest image the Middle Ages owned for it: a father bent over his young, waking them. Doctrine as den; the third day as the oldest scene in nature, a parent refusing the stillness of his child.

### Moses Brings Forth Water out of the Rock

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-72.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a full vertical strip of fictive ornament. At the centre, a quatrefoil opens onto a scene: an elderly bald-crowned man with a white beard, wrapped in an ochre-yellow mantle, stands in profile facing right and extends a slender rod toward a mass of pale rock filling the right lobe. Above and below, dark porphyry-like panels framed in white hold symmetrical acanthus candelabra, each sprouting around a small human face amid the foliage; a pilaster strip of Cosmatesque lozenge mosaic runs up the right edge.">
  <figcaption>Moses Brings Forth Water out of the Rock, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. North wall, middle register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between the Baptism of Christ and the Marriage at Cana: Moses alone, rod in hand, touches the desert rock with his rod — the water-miracle of the old covenant stationed between the two water-miracles of the new, under Paul’s own gloss: “and the rock was Christ.”</figcaption>
</figure>

Between the *Baptism of Christ* and the *Marriage at Cana* the register thinks in water: the Jordan on one side, the six jars on the other — the element consecrated, then commanded. And in the band that separates them the quatrefoil opens onto the water before both: Moses at the rock of the desert, rod extended, the miracle of Exodus caught at the instant of touch. The margin's logic has never been tighter, for this is the one type in the whole system that Scripture glosses in its own voice. Paul, recalling the wilderness, says the fathers "were all baptized unto Moses in the cloud and in the sea; and did all drink the same spiritual drink: for they drank of that spiritual Rock that followed them: and that Rock was Christ" (1 Corinthians 10:2–4). Baptism and the rock's water in one sentence — the apostle writing, twelve centuries in advance, the caption for exactly this stretch of wall.

Giotto reduces the episode to its grammar. Exodus gives Moses an audience — the murmuring people of Rephidim, the elders summoned as witnesses (Exodus 17:5–6) — and the painters of the catacombs onward usually kept at least the drinkers, crowding the spring. The quatrefoil keeps no one. An old man, a rod, a rock — and the rock under the rod’s touch: the four nouns of the miracle, locked into the lobes with the same economy that gave the Circumcision its two figures and the lion his litter. The rod is held in the right hand alone, lightly, and the answer has come: of the margin’s three panels so far, this is the only one that shows its miracle accomplished — the knife of the eighth day withheld, the lion’s breath in mid-act, but here the stone already yielding, the desert already drinking. The type most securely glossed is also the one most confidently shown.

The rod deserves its own glance, for the chapel has been collecting rods all along. The suitors carried theirs to the Temple and Joseph's alone flowered; the angels of Joachim bore the trefoil staff; here is the eldest of the line — the *virga* of Moses, the rod that swallowed Pharaoh's serpents, parted the sea, and now draws water from stone. Medieval exegesis braided these wands together: the rod that strikes the rock and the rod of Jesse that flowers, instruments by which the impossible is asked of matter and matter consents. In the band's little scene the rod is almost weightless, a pen-stroke against the pale mass — Giotto's way of saying that the power is not in the wood.

And the struck rock carried, for Giotto's readers, one further figure that darkens the panel's sweetness. The exegetes read the smitten stone through the smitten side: as water sprang from the rock under the rod, so from the pierced side of Christ on the cross sprang blood and water (John 19:34) — the sacraments issuing from the wound, the font opened by a blow. The quatrefoil thus glosses more than its neighbours: the water it promises flows toward the Jordan at its left, toward the jars at its right, and downward through the register below, where the lance will open the rock itself. Paul's equation holds to the end: the rock was Christ, and the rock, too, was struck.

Between two scenes in which water obeys — receiving its Lord, becoming wine — the band remembers the first obedience, when water came out of stone because a rod asked it to; and the sequence it joins makes one long sentence of the element: struck from the rock, hallowed in the river, changed at the feast — matter rehearsing, miracle by miracle, its readiness for the sacraments.

### Jonah Swallowed up by the Whale

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-73.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a full vertical strip of fictive ornament. At the centre, a quatrefoil opens onto a sea: stylised grey-green waves fill the lower lobes, and from them the pale body of a great fish rears up out of the water in the act of swallowing a man head-first — only his bare legs still kick upward out of its gullet against the dark sky. Above and below the quatrefoil, dark porphyry-like panels framed in white hold symmetrical acanthus rosettes; a pilaster strip of Cosmatesque mosaic runs up the right edge.">
  <figcaption>Jonah Swallowed up by the Whale, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. North wall, lower register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between the Crucifixion and the Lamentation, directly below the Moses of the rock: only the prophet’s legs remain above the waves as the great fish takes him head-first — the sign of Jonah, the one type Christ claimed for himself, posted at the threshold of the three days.</figcaption>
</figure>

This is the one type in the margins that needed no exegete, because Christ wrote the caption himself. Asked for a sign, he offered exactly one: "as Jonah was three days and three nights in the whale's belly, so shall the Son of man be three days and three nights in the heart of the earth" (Matthew 12:40). The bands have quoted Paul for the rock; here they quote the Lord — and they post his sign at the precise seam where it falls due. To the left, the *Crucifixion* ends the dying; to the right, the *Lamentation* begins the three days, the body mourned at the mouth of the tomb. Between them, in the margin, the deep rises to take its man: the fish rears out of the waves and swallows Jonah head-first, as the register’s Christ goes down into the ground.

Giotto's compression here outdoes even his own. The oldest Christian art loved Jonah in cycles — the catacombs and the sarcophagi tell him in three or four episodes, the ship, the casting overboard, the spewing forth, the gourd — for he was, from the beginning, the resurrection hope in miniature. The quatrefoil keeps a single second of all that: no ship, no sailors, no Nineveh, not even the prophet’s face. Waves, the fish rearing bodily out of the water to meet its prey, and two bare legs kicking above the gullet — the most audacious synecdoche in the chapel, a man reduced to his last visible inch, and the sea itself grown active, lunging upward while the prophet drops. Even the bands’ quiet rule about haloes goes untested: the head that might have worn one is already inside the dark.

The upside-down descent is the panel's theology. Head-first into the deep is how the chapel's west wall will send the damned down its rivers of fire — and Giotto surely knew the rhyme, for the whale's gullet was read as the very mouth of hell: Jonah's own psalm cries "out of the belly of hell" (Jonah 2:2), *de ventre inferi*. The difference between this plunge and those is three days. The fish is a devourer under orders — "the Lord had prepared a great fish" — a tomb with an appointment, hell with a return ticket; what it swallows it will be commanded to give back, as death itself, on the far wall, must stand and deliver its dead to the Judge. The margin paints the going-down whole and the coming-up not at all, and trusts the wall beside it: one scene to the right, past the mourned body, the lion is already breathing on his cubs.

For the two quatrefoils flanking the *Lamentation* are a matched pair, and between them they bracket the mystery the register never shows. Jonah entering the fish on one side of the mourning; the lion waking the litter on the other: the descent and the rousing, the belly and the breath, the first hour of the three days and their last — the tomb-scene framed by its own before and after, told in a prophet and a beast. And the vertical axis seals the system: directly above, in the same band, Moses draws water from the rock. Read downward, the strip is a single column of water — the spring that saves, the sea that swallows — and both are the font, for Paul's sentence about the rock began in the same breath with the sea: baptized in the cloud and in the sea, buried with him in baptism. The band between four scenes holds the whole doctrine of water: it quickens, it drowns, and the drowning, on the third day, is the quickening.

No panel in the system works harder with less: two legs above a wave, and in them the descent into hell, the sign of the Son of man, the promise that the deep is obedient. The visitor who stands before the *Lamentation*, the cycle's deepest grief, has the answer already posted in the frame on either side — swallowed at the left hand, wakened at the right.

### The Creation of Adam

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-74.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a full vertical strip of fictive ornament. At the centre, a quatrefoil opens onto a scene: at the left, the Creator — a bearded figure with Christ’s features, a gold radiate halo inscribed with a cross, a red garment under a blue mantle now somewhat darkened — bends forward with both hands extended; at the right, a naked beardless young man, Adam, half-rises from the pale ground, one arm reaching up toward the Creator’s hands. Above and below the quatrefoil, dark porphyry-like panels framed in white hold symmetrical acanthus candelabra sprouting around small foliate faces; a pilaster strip of Cosmatesque mosaic runs up the right edge.">
  <figcaption>The Creation of Adam, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. North wall, middle register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between the Marriage at Cana and the Raising of Lazarus: the Creator, bearing the face and halo of Christ, bends over the naked Adam rising from the ground — the first creation stationed beside the second, where Lazarus is called back from the earth Adam was made of.</figcaption>
</figure>

Beside the raising of Lazarus, the margin posts the raising of Adam. The pairing is the boldest act of typology in the bands, because it reads the Gospel's greatest miracle as a repetition: the man called out of the tomb at the right of this strip is made of the same earth as the man rising from the ground inside the quatrefoil, and the voice is the same voice. Genesis says the Lord God "formed man of the dust of the ground, and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life, and man became a living soul" (Genesis 2:7); John says "he cried with a loud voice, Lazarus, come forth." Between the two sentences the band draws its equals sign: to create and to resurrect are one power, exercised twice on the same clay — once before death existed, once straight through it.

Adam is painted at the one instant the subject offers a painter of bodies. Not the inert clay, not the finished man standing: the rising — the naked figure half-lifted from the pale ground, the torso already alive, one arm already reaching, the legs still trailing in the earth that made them. Giotto gives the first man the unfinished motion of waking, matter in the act of becoming someone; and the Creator wears the face of Christ — and his halo. The bearded features, the red garment under the blue mantle, and above all the cross inscribed in the radiate nimbus: the cruciform halo is the chapel’s reserved sign, worn by the Redeemer of the vault and the Judge of the west wall and by no one else — and here it crowns the God of the sixth day. The identification is doctrine, not licence. “All things were made by him,” John’s prologue says of the Word, “and without him was not any thing made that was made”; the Middle Ages took the sentence literally and painted the Logos as Creator — but the quatrefoil goes further than likeness: it seals the Creator with the instrument of the Passion, the cross already in the sky over Adam’s making, as if the sixth day knew the sixth hour. The man in the stories next door is the maker of the man in all of them; and within the bands’ quiet system he is the exception that proves their rule — Abraham, Moses and Jonah go unhaloed, and the one nimbus the old covenant admits is cruciform, because God, the panel says, was always Christ, and Christ was always the Crucified. The margins keep returning to that small arc of trust between the made and the maker, arriving and never quite closing — the open circuit down which, in Genesis, the breath travels.

For breath is this strip's element, as water was the last one's. Directly below, in the same band one register down, the lion bends over his still cubs and wakes them with his breath on the third day: read vertically, the column is a single doctrine twice told — the *spiraculum vitae* breathed into Adam's face at the top, breathed into the dead litter at the bottom, the first animation and the re-animation, creation and Easter stacked in one seam of the wall. And Paul, as ever, had written the axis its caption: "the first man Adam was made a living soul; the last Adam was made a quickening spirit" (1 Corinthians 15:45). The band holds both Adams — the one receiving life at the top, and below, in the lion's figure, the one restoring it.

So the margin's library opens its first book last. Genesis stood on the shelf already with Isaac's circumcision; now the system reaches back past the covenant to the sixth day itself. The great register tells what happened to one man in one lifetime; the quatrefoils, in their handful of inches, keep reminding the visitor what kind of creature that lifetime was for — the clay that reached up once at the beginning, and is reached for, scene after scene, ever since.

### The Prophet Elisha and the Youths

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-75.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a full vertical strip of fictive ornament. At the centre, a quatrefoil opens onto a scene: at the left, an old man with long white hair and a long white beard, wrapped in a rose-red mantle, bends in profile toward the right; immediately before him, in a darkened zone, a hand grasps the red mantle; beyond, filling the right lobes, four bare-headed youths press close, their faces upturned in a row. Neither elder bears a halo. Above and below the quatrefoil, dark panels framed in white hold symmetrical acanthus candelabra sprouting around small foliate faces; a pilaster strip of Cosmatesque mosaic runs up the right edge.">
  <figcaption>The Prophet Elisha and the Youths (Elijah and Elisha with the Sons of the Prophets), quadrilobe on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. North wall, middle register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between the Raising of Lazarus and the Entry into Jerusalem: an old prophet bends toward four youths while a second elder grasps his red mantle — the last journey of 2 Kings 2, the disciple who will not let go and the prophetic school in attendance, stationed directly above the fiery chariot that will take the master up.</figcaption>
</figure>

The quatrefoil holds more than its title admits. The received name — Elisha with the youths — counts one prophet; the panel paints two. Between the old man in the rose-red mantle and the four boys stands a second bearded elder, and his hands are not idle: they grasp the red mantle itself. In the second chapter of the second book of Kings that grip is the whole story. On the last day, as Elijah walks from Gilgal to Bethel to Jericho to the Jordan, knowing himself summoned, he three times bids his disciple stay behind, and three times Elisha answers with the same vow — "as the Lord liveth, and as thy soul liveth, I will not leave thee" (2 Kings 2:2, 4, 6) — while at each station the sons of the prophets come out to say what both already know: today the master will be taken from his head. The band compresses the journey into its grammar. The master bends forward along his road; the disciple is fastened to his mantle, his face turned not to the road nor to the boys but to the master himself, so that the vow is made visible twice over, in the grip and in the gaze; and the young school of prophecy presses in behind, four upturned faces, the witnesses of the handing-over.

And the mantle the second elder grips is the relic the chapter turns on. It is Elijah's mantle that was cast over Elisha at his calling; the mantle that, rolled together, smites the Jordan dry on the last walk; the mantle that falls from the ascending chariot and is taken up by the disciple, who smites the water with it in his turn and is known: "the spirit of Elijah doth rest on Elisha. And when the sons of the prophets saw him, they bowed themselves to the ground before him" (2 Kings 2:15). Giotto paints the garment at the centre of the transaction — red as the chapel paints its chosen things — with the inheritor's hands already upon it. The quatrefoil is the succession itself: not the miracle before, not the chariot after, but the clinging between, the spirit passing through held cloth from an old man to the next.

The horizontal neighbour gives the column its Gospel key, for Luke frames Christ's own road to Jerusalem in exactly Elijah's word: "when the time was come that he should be received up, he stedfastly set his face to go to Jerusalem" (Luke 9:51) — the *Entry* at this panel's right hand is the last journey of a master who knows he goes up to be taken up, his disciples about him, none of them yet fit to hold what will fall to them at Pentecost.

The four youths bind the type to its scene. Beside the *Entry*, the young company that recognises the departing master answers the Gospel's own chorus — the children of Jerusalem crying Hosanna, whom Christ defends out of the psalm: "out of the mouth of babes and sucklings thou hast perfected praise" (Matthew 21:15–16). Exegesis knew a darker set of boys in the same chapter of Kings — the mockers of Bethel and their bears, the old antithesis to the Hosanna — but the quatrefoil paints no jeering: the faces are lifted, not twisted; the second elder's grip has no place in that story; and the bands choose, here as everywhere, the gentler figure — recognition, not derision; the school that bows, beside the city that sings.

The margins' rules hold and sharpen. The economy stands: no Jordan, no Bethel, no chariot in this frame, six figures and a held garment. The census gains its first *pair* of actors, and with them the system's deepest subject so far. Every panel of the bands has been about transmission, and here it is painted as what it humanly is: an old man walking ahead, a younger one holding on, and the young watching how it is done. The chapel next door will spend two walls on the same lesson; the margin teaches it in four lobes, hand to hem.

### Elijah on the Chariot of Fire

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-76.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a full vertical strip of fictive ornament. At the centre, a quatrefoil opens onto a blue sky: a white-robed, white-haired prophet with a darkened halo stands in a two-wheeled cart, his left arm raised; cart, wheels and the single galloping horse that draws it upward to the right are all painted the same flame-red, with plumes of fire about them; no reins are shown. In his right hand he still holds his pale mantle, which streams away toward the lower left lobe — where the face and reaching right arm of a young man stretch up toward it from the margin's edge. Above and below the quatrefoil run interlaced bands of fictive Cosmatesque inlay, and lozenge fields of red acanthus scrollwork close the strip at top and bottom.">
  <figcaption>Elijah on the Chariot of Fire, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. North wall, lower register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between the Noli me tangere and the Ascension, directly below Elisha holding the master's hem: the prophet rides upward in a two-wheeled cart drawn by a horse of flame, his left arm raised in farewell, the pale mantle still in his right hand and streaming toward a young Elisha who reaches up for it from the lower lobe — the old covenant's rapture stationed beside the new covenant's ascension.</figcaption>
</figure>

The column the margins have been building closes here in fire. One register up, in the same band, the disciple grips the master's mantle on the last road; in this quatrefoil the road ends as the second chapter of Kings ends it: "there appeared a chariot of fire, and horses of fire, and parted them both asunder; and Elijah went up by a whirlwind into heaven" (2 Kings 2:11). Giotto paints the parting at its exact pivot — the car already climbing the blue, the prophet’s left arm raised toward the earth he leaves, and the mantle, the held thing of the panel above, still gripped in his right hand while it streams down toward the lower lobe, where a young face and a reaching arm strain up to receive it. The handing-over is caught *in transit*: the master has not yet released, the disciple has not yet grasped, and the cloth hangs between them, the office airborne. Read as one seam of wall, the two quatrefoils are a single sentence with a register of masonry for its caesura: *I will not leave thee* — and then the leaving, with the one thing that stays stretched between the going hand and the coming one.

The fire is painted as substance, not circumstance. Cart, wheels, and the single straining horse are all one flame-red, the same body of colour — not a vehicle on fire but a vehicle *of* fire, exactly as the text insists (*currus igneus et equi ignei*), combustion given harness and spokes — though Scripture's plural horses are pared to one, the quatrefoil's economy at work even on the miracle. And there are no reins: the passenger of heaven does not drive, the fire knows its road. Giotto, who built this chapel's rivers of fire on the west wall from the same red, here bends the element to the opposite office: the fire that there devours, here conveys. The little cart itself is no celestial invention but a working two-wheeler of the Veneto, plank sides and studded wheel — the painter's constant wager, that the miracle convinces in proportion as the furniture is real. The whirlwind goes unpainted; its work survives in the horse's impossible climb, hooves on nothing, and in the mantle streaming taut between two hands.

Beside the *Ascension*, the type measures its distance from the antitype — and the distance is the doctrine. Elijah is *taken*: the chariot is sent for him, the fire does the carrying, the grammar of Kings is passive, and he goes up as a passenger of heaven. The Christ of the adjacent scene needs no vehicle at all: he rises by his own act, hands already breaching the frame's upper edge, lifted by nothing but divinity — the difference every commentator since the Fathers drew between the servant conveyed and the Lord ascending. The band states the comparison the medieval way, by adjacency, and even aims it: the machine runs rightward, toward the *Ascension* itself, the type galloping at its antitype. The witnesses, too, rhyme at a distance. Below the rising Christ stand the apostles and the two white angels; below the rising prophet there is one young man only — Elisha, reaching up from the lobe's edge for the mantle not yet let go. The column thus paints the succession from both its ends: above, the old grip on the hem, *I will not leave thee*; here, the young reach for the descending office — and between the two panels the same cloth, held, streaming, awaited.

The raised right arm is the panel's human centre: not the Judge's verdict, not the orator's address, but a farewell — the gesture of a man looking back as he is carried forward, the old covenant's valediction to the earth it prophesied over. And it binds the quatrefoil to its left-hand neighbour, for in the *Noli me tangere* the risen Christ makes his own gesture of parting toward the one who would hold him — *do not cling to me, for I am not yet ascended* — the grammar of Elijah's story inverted: there the disciple clung and was right to; here the clinging is forbidden, because this master, unlike that one, goes up to come again in the body. Between a touch refused and a mantle released, the band thinks about how the holy is held, and concludes, twice over, that it is held by letting go. One last sign seals the prophet's singularity: Elijah wears what no other Hebrew head in the margins wears — a halo, darkened now but legible behind the white hair. The bands have been strict: Abraham, Jonah, Moses at the rock, the two prophets of the column above, all uncrowned; glory is admitted only where Scripture itself kindles it — the Creator’s cruciform nimbus, Moses’s face alight on Sinai, and here Elijah, the old covenant’s one exempted man: he does not die. Taken bodily, expected back before the great day, standing beside Moses at the Transfiguration, he belongs already to the economy of glory the nimbus signifies — the margins crown precisely the head that never lay down. Whether the ring is original or a restorer’s hand, the logic, either way, is the panel’s own.

With the chariot the margins’ census reaches its first completed column. What remains constant, from the first panel to this blaze, is the scale of the claim against the scale of the frame: the margins of this chapel ask to be read as Scripture’s own margins were — small script, bearing the whole weight of the gloss.

### Moses on Mount Sinai

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-77.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a full vertical strip of fictive ornament. At the centre, a quatrefoil opens onto a blue sky with pale clouds: from the upper right a bearded figure with Christ’s features bends down, his darkened halo bearing traces of an inscribed cross, and delivers a single white tablet, unmarked by any writing; at the lower left an elderly bald-crowned man with a long white beard and a darkened halo of his own, wrapped in an ochre-yellow mantle, grasps the tablet with both hands. At the right, beside him, rises the bare rock of the mountain, which he does not touch. Above and below the quatrefoil run interlaced bands of fictive Cosmatesque inlay, and lozenge fields of red acanthus scrollwork close the strip at top and bottom.">
  <figcaption>Moses on Mount Sinai, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. North wall, lower register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between the Ascension and the Pentecost: God, bearing Christ’s features, bends down from the sky to deliver the white tablet of the Law into the grasping hands of Moses — the Sinai of the fiftieth day stationed beside the Pentecost of the fiftieth day, the Law in stone beside the Law in fire.</figcaption>
</figure>

Between the *Ascension* and the *Pentecost* the band posts the one Old Testament scene that the feast itself demanded. Pentecost was Sinai's own anniversary before it was the Church's birthday: the fiftieth day after Passover was Shavuot, the feast of the giving of the Law, and the Spirit chose that day to descend — so that the fire on the apostles falls on the calendar date of the fire on the mountain, "and mount Sinai was altogether on a smoke, because the Lord descended upon it in fire" (Exodus 19:18). The exegetes never tired of the symmetry: two fiftieth days, two descents in fire, two laws delivered — one cut into stone on the mountain, one written by flame on twelve foreheads in an upper room. The quatrefoil sets the first beside the second and lets the wall perform the arithmetic: what Moses brings down at the left of this strip, the Spirit brings down at its right, to the same people of God at two ages of its life.

Giotto stages the delivery as the margins’ first completed transaction. God bends down bodily from the upper lobe of the sky; Moses grasps the tablet with both hands — and with that grip the system’s recurring arc, the reach that does not touch, finally closes. Isaac reached toward the face, Adam toward the hands, Elisha toward the streaming mantle, and none of them touched; the Law is the one thing in the margins that is actually handed over, document passing from hand to hand, the covenant in writing — and it is characteristic of the whole system that what finally arrives is a text. A mute one: the single white tablet carries no visible letters, the stone delivered blank — as if, beside the Pentecost, the margin conceded Paul’s point in advance: what matters will not be what is written on the stone. Even the mountain concurs in the strangeness: Sinai stands at Moses’s side as bare rock, and he does not touch it — the receiver braced on nothing, like the giver, the whole transaction conducted in mid-air between two holds on one white slab.

And the Giver, once more, is Christ: the features, the beard, and — legible under the darkening — the cross inscribed in the nimbus, the margins’ second cruciform theophany, matching the Creator’s, three bands back. But this panel doubles the glory, for Moses too is crowned here, a darkened halo behind the white hair — the same Moses who worked the rock two bands away bare-headed. The apparent inconsistency is the system’s finest precision. Exodus itself crowns him only on the mountain: when Moses came down from Sinai, “the skin of his face shone” from speaking with God (Exodus 34:29) — the borrowed radiance that made Israel afraid, the glory Paul would call fading. At the rock he is a prophet working; on the mountain he is a mirror, shining because of what he faces — and the halo, like the radiance it records, belongs to the encounter, not the man.

The pairing also carries its shadow, and Paul had drawn it indelibly. The letter and the Spirit, the tables of stone and the tables of the heart, "for the letter killeth, but the spirit giveth life" (2 Corinthians 3:3–6): the apostle's whole contrast between the two covenants is a contrast between this panel and its right-hand neighbour. The Fathers sharpened it with the two counts of three thousand — at the foot of Sinai, after the golden calf, "there fell of the people that day about three thousand men" (Exodus 32:28); at Pentecost, after Peter's sermon, "the same day there were added unto them about three thousand souls" (Acts 2:41) — the Law's first day and the Spirit's first day balancing their books in opposite columns, death dealt and life added in the same figure. The band, as ever, states none of this; it only places the stone tablet a frame's width from the tongues of fire and lets the two fiftieth days read each other.

So the margins close their account of transmission with the scene that defines it. The strip between the chapel's last two histories holds, fittingly, the old covenant's own last word before the register beside it turns the page: the tablet descends at the left hand of the room where, at the right, the Spirit is already writing on flesh.

### Two Evangelists

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-78.jpg" alt="Fresco detail from the painted decorative bands of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a vertical strip with two barbed quatrefoils on blue grounds, separated by a large panel of fictive rose marble in dark frames. In the upper quatrefoil, a young evangelist with short dark hair, a darkened halo, a pale tunic with an embroidered collar and a red mantle over one shoulder sits behind a wooden writing-desk, his right hand raised beside his face holding a slender pen, another pen lying on the desk. In the lower quatrefoil, an old evangelist with long white hair and beard and a darkened halo bends low over his wooden desk, reading his page, a darkened pen held still in his left hand.">
  <figcaption>Two Evangelists, quadrilobes on the decorative bands, 1304–1306. Fresco. North wall, lower register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the terminal band at the right of the Pentecost, where the cycle ends against the west wall: two haloed evangelists at their writing-desks — a young one pausing, pen raised, an old one bent close over his page — the story the walls have just told, caught in the act of becoming Scripture.</figcaption>
</figure>

Where the story ends, the writers begin. The *Pentecost* at this band's left hand is the cycle's last scene — the Spirit descending in fire on the twelve — and the first consequence of that fire, in the Church's own account of itself, was ink: the Gospels were written because Pentecost had happened, the Spirit turning witnesses into authors. The terminal band takes the sequence literally. Past the final history, in the strip where the narrative wall gives out, the quatrefoils stop showing types and start showing *texts in progress*: evangelists at their desks, pens in hand — the four walls' own sources, depicted in the act of being produced. The chapel, having told its story, shows the story being written down; the cycle ends with its bibliography.

The change of regime is announced by the frames themselves. The Hebrew histories of the margins looked out through plain quatrefoils cut in the Cosmatesque bands; the evangelists sit in barbed quatrefoils — the cusped, flowered form the century's goldsmiths and sculptors reserved for a higher order of relic — on grounds of deep blue, the chapel's own heaven-colour, with panels of fictive rose marble between them. And they are haloed, both of them, the metal now darkened like so much of the bands' glory. The nimbus is theirs by office.

Giotto paints them as the two acts of one labour. Above, the young evangelist pauses mid-composition: the pen lifted beside his face, waiting on the next word, a second pen ready on the desk — the moment the Middle Ages knew best, when the instrument hangs in air and the sentence has not yet come. Below, the old one is not writing at all: he reads — bent close over the page, white beard almost brushing the wood, the pen held still in his left hand while the eyes go back over what it set down. Between the lifted pen and the lowered one the pair states the whole of authorship: the word awaited, and the word verified. It is, among other things, the margins’ driest self-portrait — for a chapel that has glossed its own walls for four registers ends with an author checking his text — and the desks carry the usual furniture-realism: solid carpenters’ work, plank and peg, the scriptorium’s actual equipment hauled into the sky-blue of the quatrefoils.

Their station carries one more weight, for this band does not merely end the north wall: it borders the west. Around its corner hangs the *Last Judgment* — and the books these two are writing are the books that wall will need.
