"And the books were opened: and another book was opened, which is the book of life: and the dead were judged out of those things which were written in the books" (Revelation 20:12). The evangelists write at the exact seam between history and verdict: on their left, the story completed; on their right, around the masonry's edge, the court where its record is produced in evidence. The placement makes the margins' last argument: everything the visitor has walked past — the kiss, the denials, the charity and the betrayals — has been *taken down*; the pens at the end of the wall are the reason the Judge, around the corner, can judge.

The visitor leaves between two kinds of writing: the evangelists setting down what happened, and, overhead on the west wall, the consequence of its having been written. Few buildings have ever said more plainly what they are: a book, opened, with the authors still at work in the binding.

### The First Decorative Band of the Vault

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-79.jpg" alt="Fresco detail of a painted ornamental band crossing the vault of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a vertical strip on deep red ground. At the top, a barbed quatrefoil on blue holds the bust of an old man with long flowing white hair and beard, a radiate gold halo and a red mantle, holding upright a darkened object that widens fan-wise as it rises past his left shoulder, perhaps a scourge, lower right, appears the small head and part of the bust of a child, his face turned up toward the elder. At the centre, blue acanthus scrollwork with gold flowers opens around the bust of a wingèd figure: a youthful face under a pointed leaf-like hood, grey-blue wings spread, a gold mantle clasped at the throat. At the bottom, a second barbed quatrefoil on blue holds the bust of a bearded man with a radiate gold halo and brown mantle, his right hand raised. Cosmatesque mosaic chevrons separate the compartments.">
  <figcaption>Busts on the first decorative band of the vault (detail), c. 1303–1305. Fresco. Barrel vault, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. A stretch of the first of the three ornamental arches that cross the starry vault, at the right of which opens the field of the blessing Redeemer: haloed busts of the old covenant’s holy ones in barbed quatrefoils on blue — an elder with a child at his shoulder among them — alternating with winged figures born out of the acanthus scrollwork.</figcaption>
</figure>

The vault's sky is hemmed by processions. Three ornamental arches cross the starry barrel — one at each end, one at the ridge between the two fields — and along them, compartment by compartment, runs a third population of the ceiling: not the ten great clipei with their enthroned icons and documented prophets, but a continuous ribbon of small busts in barbed quatrefoils, haloed heads on blue, strung between panels of scrollwork like gems on a chain. This stretch belongs to the first band, at whose right hand opens the field of the blessing Redeemer: the holy dead of the old dispensation filing across the sky toward the Christ their histories served — the vault's own version of the elect, rank on rank, that the west wall musters at full scale.

The upper bust states the band’s one riddle. An elder with the flowing white hair and beard of the patriarchs holds upright a darkened object that widens as it rises, fanning out past his left shoulder — not a knife, not a scroll: the shape reads most naturally as a scourge, its thongs spreading from the grip, the surface oxidised to near-black like so much of the chapel’s a secco detail. And at his shoulder, within the same frame, a child: the small head and a sliver of the body, the young face turned up toward the old man — who does not return the look, but gazes out frontally, with the heavy-browed melancholy the band’s painter reserves for its elders. An instrument of affliction and a child in attendance: the pairing resists the easy names. It is not Abraham, whose attribute is the blade; a scourge beside a holy elder speaks rather the language of the prova — the chastisement borne or administered that Scripture hangs about its tried men — while the child at the shoulder may be son, soul, or simply the next generation watching, the band’s recurring theme of transmission compressed to two heads in one frame. What the fresco itself insists on is only this: an old man who looks at us, an instrument of suffering in his hand, and a child who looks at him.

Between the quatrefoils, the ornament comes alive. The scrollwork panels are not interludes but a population of their own: out of the blue acanthus, at the centre of each candelabra, a winged figure grows — here a youthful angel-bust, grey-blue wings spread, a gold mantle at the throat, and on its head a pointed hood that is half cap, half leaf, as if the foliage itself had gathered to a face. The wall-bands below kept small foliate masks in their candelabra; the vault promotes the motif to full angelhood — the ornament generating its own attendants, vegetation flowering into spirit at the threshold of the painted heaven. It is Giotto's quietest statement of a theme the whole ceiling argues: up here, nothing is merely decorative; the frame itself is inhabited.

The lower bust returns to the procession's rank and file: a bearded holy one, radiate halo, the right hand lifted — the band's recurring grammar of blessing and address, each small head given just enough gesture to be a person and not a pattern.

Along the three arches run the anonymous many — the small heads whose names the frames do not trouble to give, because the blue they float on gives the only address that matters. The visitor below, following the stars, crosses these processions three times down the length of the room: the sky of this chapel is not empty between its great medallions, and was never meant to be — it is bordered, like every page of the building, with the company the text was written for.

### Two Busts beside the Birth of the Virgin

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-80.jpg" alt="Fresco detail of a painted decorative band on the north wall of the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a vertical strip. At top and bottom, barbed quatrefoils on grey-blue grounds each hold the bust of an elderly bearded man with a radiate gold halo and a rose-red mantle over a pale tunic. The upper elder, short grey-bearded, lays his right hand flat against his breast; the lower, long-bearded, rests his left hand on a staff. The blue of the grounds survives patchily, the azurite fallen in places.">
  <figcaption>Decorative band with busts, detail, c. 1303–1305. Fresco. North wall, upper register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. From the painted band at the left of the Birth of the Virgin: two haloed elders in barbed quatrefoils, one hand laid to the breast and the other on a staff, separated by a panel of fictive marble and flanked by candelabra strips — the crowned ancestors of the margins, stationed at the threshold of the birth their whole order waited for.</figcaption>
</figure>

Beside the room where the Virgin is born, the margin posts two old men. The band at the left of the *Birth of the Virgin* carries no narrative quatrefoils — no types, no histories — but busts: haloed elders in the barbed frames the margins reserve for the crowned orders, each with one hand raised before his breast, attending. The upper register's bands, it emerges, work differently from the lower walls': down among the Gospel's public events the strips hold scenes, the old covenant acting beside the new; up here, along the register of the Virgin's hidden beginnings, they hold presences — the generations in person rather than their deeds. And the station is exact. What is being born a hand's breadth to the right is the daughter of the whole line: the child of Joachim and Anna, but also of every ancestor behind them, the shoot the old trunk had been growing toward. The quatrefoils beside the birth-chamber hold, in effect, the family — the fathers at the door of the lying-in room, crowned and silent, come to see the generation that ends the waiting.

The pair is built on the band-system’s grammar with its dignities intact — barbed quatrefoils on blue (the azurite now fallen to grey in patches), radiate gold nimbi, the rose mantle the bands’ painter gives his elders — and for once the two are differentiated by more than the head. The upper elder lays his hand to his breast, the gesture of the sanctified in devotion. The lower leans on a staff, the left hand closed over its head — the baculus of the patriarchs, the walking-stick of the long migration, the one attribute in all the band’s crowned company. Between them the pair states the two tenses of ancestry: benediction, which looks forward, and the staff, which remembers the road. And beside this particular scene the staff carries a quiet echo the register will make explicit three frames on, where the rods of the suitors are brought to the Temple and one of them flowers: the line that walked for generations, arriving at the house where its flowering is born. Blessing above, wayfaring below; neither carries scroll or book — beside the birth, these are the attributes enough.

The bands of the Virgin’s register are the wall’s answer to the vault above them: as the ceiling strings its crowned busts along the arches around the Madonna’s clipeus, so the wall strings its crowned elders along the frames around her infancy — one population at two altitudes, the attendance of the just.

Between and beside them, the frame speaks its materials. The square of fictive veined marble separating the quatrefoils belongs to the chapel's great sustained fiction — the painted stone that clads the building from the dado upward — and the flanking candelabra strips on ochre run like carved jambs. The ensemble dresses the scene's edge as the century's sculptors dressed a portal: membered stone and peopled niches — except that every moulding is pigment, and the population needs no niches, floating on the blue that serves, throughout these margins, as glory's own ground.

The visitor reading the great scene follows Anna, the midwives, the swaddled child; the frame, meanwhile, has filled the doorposts with the family's elders — bearing nothing, present at everything, the waiting of Israel compressed to two old faces at the edge of the room where it ends.

### A Young Saint with a Book

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-81.jpg" alt="Fresco detail of a painted decorative band in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a vertical strip of fictive grey-green veined marble, bordered by ochre candelabra strips. At the centre, a barbed quatrefoil on a blue-grey ground holds the bust of a beardless young man with short dark hair and a radiate gold halo, wearing a pale mantle over a tunic, his right hand laid flat on the breast, his left holding a small closed red book, plain and unclasped, against his chest. Above and below the quatrefoil, two white lozenge frames enclose red cross-shaped leaf ornaments, with small gemmed squares at their points.">
  <figcaption>Bust of a young saint with a book, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, c. 1303–1306. Fresco. Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between Joachim Expelled from the Temple and Joachim among the Shepherds (placement uncertain), south wall, upper register: a beardless young saint with a radiate halo, a red book held against the breast, in a barbed quatrefoil set in fictive grey-veined marble between two gemmed lozenge-crosses — a reader of the new covenant among the margins' crowned company.</figcaption>
</figure>

Among the elders of the margins, a young face. The bands' crowned population has been, almost without exception, a senate: white beards, bowed age, the dignity of long waiting — the prophets of the vault, the ancestors beside the Virgin's birth, the patriarch with his staff. This quatrefoil breaks the pattern twice over: the saint is beardless, dark-haired, hardly more than a youth; and he carries the margins' newest attribute — a book, small, red-bound, held flat against the breast under the crossed right hand. In the grammar the system has taught, both signs point one way. Youth among the crowned, in this chapel, belongs to the new order — Daniel in the vault's Marian field, the evangelists at their desks — and the codex, as against the scroll, is the new covenant's own format: the old men of the margins unroll their words or carry none; the young carry books, bound and closed, the testament complete. And if the placement that suggests itself is right — the band between Joachim’s expulsion and his exile among the shepherds — the contrast is the register’s own: beside the old man turned away from the Temple, scroll-less and childless, the margin posts a youth who carries the whole of what the Temple guarded, bound in red against his chest. The old economy shuts its door at the left of this strip; the frame, meanwhile, already holds the book that will come of the shut door.

The gesture is the panel’s quiet centre — and it is two gestures, not one. The right hand lies flat on the breast, the immemorial sign of devotion, of the self presented; the left holds the book, closed, plain, unclasped, a small red volume kept at the side of the heart. He does not display it, as evangelist-portraits display their Gospels open to the first verse, nor does the devotional hand so much as touch it: word and heart side by side, each with its own hand, the figure pledging himself and carrying the text — allegiance and possession stated separately, like a man taking an oath on what he holds. If the elders of the Virgin’s register gave the margins their two tenses of ancestry — blessing and the staff — this young reader gives the third: the word no longer awaited nor travelled toward, but held, bound, against the body.

The setting promotes him. The quatrefoil is not cut into the running Cosmatesque bands of the walls but set into a field of fictive grey-green veined marble, framed above and below by two white lozenges enclosing cross-shaped leafwork with gemmed studs at their points — an apparatus more lapidary than the narrative strips, the margins' equivalent of a jewelled binding. The ensemble reads as goldsmith's work translated to pigment: the saint as the medallion of a precious cover, the two gem-crosses as its bosses — fitting housing for a figure whose attribute is itself a book, the reader set into the wall the way his text would be set into treasure.

Unnamed, he keeps the margins' deepest habit. No inscription survives or was perhaps ever given; the face is individual — the broad brow, the small mouth, the level dark eyes — but the office is generic: a young saint of the written word, one of the crowned company that the chapel's framing system distributes along its arches and jambs, the population that attends without acting.

### Two Busts: a Youth Pointing and an Elder

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-82.jpg" alt="Fresco detail of a painted decorative band in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a vertical strip that narrows slightly toward the top. In the upper barbed quatrefoil, on a dark ground, the bust of a beardless young saint with a radiate gold halo, in a pale mantle over red, his right index finger pointing across his own chest, his left hand holding a darkened upright object. In the lower quatrefoil, on a grey-blue ground, the bust of an old man with a radiate gold halo, unruly white hair and a long forked beard, his right hand raised before him, his left arm drawn across the body. Between them stands a tall plain cream-coloured panel in a painted frame; strips of candelabra ornament on ochre and brown flank the compartments.">
  <figcaption>Decorative band with busts, detail, c. 1303–1305. Fresco. Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. From the chapel’s painted framing system: two haloed busts in barbed quatrefoils — above, a young saint pointing across his chest with his right hand, a darkened object in his left; below, a wild-bearded elder with his right hand raised — separated by a tall pale panel and flanked by candelabra strips.</figcaption>
</figure>

The strip pairs the margins' two ages face to face. Above, a youth — beardless, full-faced, of the young order the system admits so sparingly; below, an elder of the wildest type the bands contain, the white beard forked and untrimmed, the hair in disarray, a desert head among the combed senators of the frames. The pairing itself is the panel's first statement: the framing system, which elsewhere strings its generations along a wall — elders beside the Virgin's birth, a young reader in Joachim's register, old and young evangelists stacked at the cycle's end — here sets them in one strip, the covenant's morning and evening in a single column, each crowned with the same radiate gold.

And for once the young figure acts. The margins' busts attend: hands at breasts, blessings, a staff held still. This youth points — the index finger extended, the arm carried outward across the quatrefoil's lobe, the system's first true deixis: a gesture that does not present the self but directs the viewer, somewhere, at something. In a framing apparatus whose whole office is to gloss what stands beside it, a pointing figure is the gloss made flesh — the margin doing openly what every quatrefoil does covertly, aiming the eye out of the frame and into the programme. What he indicates, the surviving surface does not say; what he holds in the other hand, darkened now like the chapel's a secco metals and greens, keeps its counsel. The wild elder below answers with the older rhetoric — the raised right hand of address, speech without direction — so that the strip reads, top to bottom, as the two grammars of witness: the old man proclaims, the young man points.

Between them, the frame holds its breath. The tall central panel is bare — a plain cream field in its painted moulding, without the veining that marbles its counterparts elsewhere in the system — either a fictive slab whose a secco graining has perished, leaving the ground coat as a ghost of itself, or one of the pale fields the framing system occasionally allows itself, a rest between voices. The distinction belongs to the restorers; the effect, as the wall now stands, is of an empty tablet between two speakers — and it is hard, in this chapel of inscribed scrolls and mute tablets, not to feel the accident (if accident it is) composing: the blank page, the pointing youth, the proclaiming elder, as if the strip held the apparatus of announcement with the text itself fallen away.

Unnamed, unaddressed, the pair still does the system's work. The slight narrowing of the strip betrays a frame that follows the architecture rather than the registers — a band bending with the building — and wherever it stands, it stands as the margins always do: at an edge, peopled, awake.

### A Young Saint beside the Wedding of the Virgin

<figure>
  <img src="/Images/Cappella-degli-Scrovegni/cappella-degli-scrovegni-83.jpg" alt="Fresco detail of a painted decorative band in the Scrovegni Chapel, Padua: a vertical strip of fictive grey-green veined marble flecked with dark markings. At the centre, a barbed quatrefoil on a muted violet-grey ground holds the bust of a young man with shoulder-length light brown hair, a short beard, and a radiate gold halo, wearing a rose mantle over one shoulder and a pale garment on the other, his right hand drawn before his breast. Above and below, two white lozenge frames enclose red-and-gold cross-shaped leaf ornaments with small gemmed squares at their points.">
  <figcaption>Bust of a young saint, quadrilobe on the decorative bands, c. 1303–1305. Fresco. North wall, upper register, Scrovegni Chapel (Arena Chapel), Padua. On the painted band between the Marriage of the Virgin and the Wedding Procession: a long-haired young saint with a radiate halo, his right hand drawn to his breast, in a barbed quatrefoil set in fictive grey-green marble between two gemmed lozenge-crosses — a witness of the margins posted between the vow and the procession.</figcaption>
</figure>

The band between the Virgin's wedding and her wedding procession carries a single young witness. The quatrefoil belongs to the same lapidary family as the young reader of Joachim's register — the field of grey-green fictive marble, the two gemmed lozenge-crosses above and below, the margins' goldsmith-work in pigment — and its tenant belongs to the same young order: long light-brown hair falling to the shoulders, a short beard barely arrived, the radiate crown. But where that figure carried a book and this register's elders carried blessing and staff, this saint carries only his gesture: the right hand drawn before the breast, the margins' oldest sign — the self presented, the heart acknowledged — made here by the youngest kind of face the system owns.

The station does its usual quiet work. On one side of the strip, the vow: Joseph and Mary joined before the priest, the flowering rod in the bridegroom's hand, the suitor's dove. On the other, the consequence in motion: the bridal procession, the Virgin walking home. The quatrefoil stands at the hinge between promise and way — between the word given and the road taken — and its young witness, hand on heart, holds the gesture that belongs to both: the pledge. In a register whose great scenes are all about betrothal — rods brought, prayers made, a husband assigned, a bride led home — the margin posts, at the seam of the two wedding scenes, the body language of the vow itself, abstracted from any particular vower: a figure who simply, permanently, promises.

The face asks a question the crown answers. The long parted hair, the young beard, the grave frontality: the type drifts, unmistakably, toward the Christological — this is the facial formula the chapel will give its Lord, worn here by a saint of the frames. The resemblance need not embarrass; the Middle Ages cultivated it, for the beloved disciple, for the young deacons, for any figure meant to carry the Master's stamp — conformity to Christ written as physiognomy. But the nimbus keeps the grammar straight: radiate, plain, without the inscribed cross that this chapel reserves, with perfect discipline, for Christ alone. A face allowed to approach the Lord's; a crown that stops short of his: the system's little hierarchy of likeness, enforced at the width of three painted strokes.

The register’s rule holds to the end of the wall: along the Virgin's storey the margins carry no histories, only presences — the crowned company, old and young, strung beside her scenes like the attendance at a wedding, which is, at this particular seam, exactly what they are: the frame's guests, posted between the ceremony and the procession, hand on heart as the bride goes by.

## Style, Space and Narrative: Giotto's Revolution

The two painted *coretti*, small feigned chapels on either side of the chancel arch at the level of the lowest register, are the clearest demonstration of what Giotto could do with space. Longhi’s essay "Giotto spazioso", published in *Paragone* in 1952, made these neglected fragments central to the understanding of Giotto. He noted that the painted light inside them falls from that centre, and that the light seen through the painted windows is not an abstract ultramarine but a pale sky blue matching the real sky outside the apse windows. The illusion is so complete, he wrote, that one half expects to see the swallows that dart from the nearby eaves of the Eremitani pass through them. Longhi's point was polemical. Earlier criticism had treated Giotto's space as a function of his figures, and he insisted that Giotto had already conceived space as the subject of representation itself, a century before Brunelleschi.

Later geometric analysis has pursued this insight. Some scholars have argued that the coretti follow a consistent central-point construction anticipating the *costruzione legittima*. Others prefer to speak of a highly sophisticated empirical construction than of a mathematical system. What matters is that in the coretti, as on the chancel arch with its symmetrical Annunciation loggias, Giotto designed painted space in relation to the real space of the building and the real position of the viewer. That is the decisive move, and it is why the coretti, small and incidental as they are, have become emblems of the chapel's modernity. They also support the case for close integration between painter and building, whether or not Giotto was formally its architect.

Giotto's narrative method rests on a few principles applied with great discipline. Figures are solid, weighty bodies whose volume is modelled by consistent light. Drapery falls in broad masses that describe the body beneath. The number of figures in each scene is kept to the minimum the story needs. Settings, whether rock or building, are reduced to blocks that frame action rather than describe a place. Gesture and glance carry meaning: the averted eyes of Joachim's shepherds, the locked gaze of Christ and Judas, the stretched arms of the Magdalene. Figures seen from behind invite the viewer into the picture's space. The same architectural setting recurs across successive scenes, such as Anna's house or the Temple, giving continuity of place across separate panels in the way recurring scenery does on a stage. Cennino Cennini<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-cennino-cennini" class="footnote"><sup>12</sup></a>, writing near the end of the fourteenth century in a workshop tradition that descended from Giotto, summed up the effect in a phrase that has never been bettered: Giotto "rimutò l'arte del dipignere di greco in latino, e ridusse al moderno". He changed the art of painting from Greek into Latin and brought it to the modern.

The phrase "Greek into Latin" should not be read as a simple rejection of Byzantine art. The Paduan chapel shows a deep knowledge of Byzantine iconography and composition. The split Annunciation on the chancel arch, the dispatch of Gabriel, and the treatment of many Marian scenes have Byzantine and Byzantinizing precedents, which would have been available in Venice and its hinterland. What changed was the relation between image and viewer. The Byzantine icon addresses the viewer from a timeless plane. Giotto's scenes present events unfolding in a space continuous with the viewer's own, under a single light and a single sky. That transformation, rather than any particular motif, is what fourteenth-century writers recognized and what later historiography canonized.

The question of workshop participation has become more pressing since the restoration made close study of technique possible. A programme of this size, painted in so short a time, required many hands. The figure of "about forty" assistants, which circulates in popular literature, has no documentary basis and should not be repeated as fact. Jacobus has argued that "some of the paintings now seen in the Arena Chapel, which have always been attributed to Giotto, are not in fact by him", and that several independent masters worked under his direction in a flexibly organized workshop. Other specialists accept extensive collaboration but keep a stronger sense of a single guiding design and a single authorial hand in the key passages. The evidence of the giornate and the variations in execution confirm a team. They do not confirm individual personalities, and attempts to assign particular figures to named or pseudonymous assistants remain conjectural.

The case of the choir is different and clearer. The six scenes of the last days of the Virgin on the side walls of the chancel, together with much of the decoration of the apse, are generally agreed not to be by Giotto. They are attributed to an anonymous painter known as the Master of the Scrovegni Choir. Their date is uncertain. Most scholars now place them around 1315 to 1325, in connection with the alteration of the east end and the preparation of the choir as Enrico's burial place. The only firm terminus is the testament of Enrico's widow, Iacobina, in 1365, which is too late to be useful. A minority has argued for a date closer to the nave campaign. The thematic link is clear: the choir completes the Marian story with her Dormition and Assumption, consistent with the programme of the nave. The style shows a painter trained in Giotto's manner but working with a softer and more decorative sensibility. The question is debated, but the weight of opinion clearly removes the choir from Giotto's own campaign.

The altar furnishings the patron commissioned confirm the level of his ambition. Three marble statues by [Giovanni Pisano](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Giovanni-Pisano.html), the Virgin and Child flanked by two deacons, stood on the altar. The two leading artists of the day in painting and sculpture were thus brought together in one small building, a combination unmatched elsewhere at the time. A large painted crucifix associated with Giotto, which once hung in the chapel, is now in the Musei Civici. Its autograph status is generally accepted, though workshop participation has been suggested.

## Early Fame: Riccobaldo, Francesco da Barberino, Dante and the Commentators

The chapel's fame began almost at once, and the earliest testimony is unusually good. Riccobaldo of Ferrara<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-riccobaldo-ferrarese" class="footnote"><sup>13</sup></a> lived in Padua around 1306–1308, and his *Compilatio chronologica*, a universal chronicle completed around 1312–1313, contains the first datable reference to Giotto's work in the chapel. In a phrase without precedent in medieval historical writing for a painter, he wrote that "Zotus pictor eximius florentinus agnoscitur qualis in arte fuerit", Giotto, an outstanding Florentine painter, whose quality in his art is recognized. He went on: "testantur opera facta per eum in ecclesiis Minorum Assisii, Arimini, Padue, et per ea que pinxit in palatio Comunis Padue et in ecclesia Arene Padue". His works for the Franciscans at Assisi, Rimini and Padua bear witness, and so do those he painted in the communal palace of Padua and in the church of the Arena of Padua. Because the passage is set among memories of Riccobaldo's own stay in Padua, those years may serve as a terminus ante quem of about 1308 for the works he lists, although the chronicle itself was completed only around 1312–1313. It confirms Giotto's authorship of the Arena paintings within a few years of their execution. The inclusion of a painter in a world chronicle is itself a sign of the new status Giotto had won.

Francesco da Barberino is the second early witness. A Florentine notary and poet, he lived in Padua from about 1304 to 1308. In his allegorical treatise *Documenti d'Amore*, whose Latin gloss was written mainly during his exile in Provence between 1309 and 1313, he refers to the figure of Envy in the chapel's dado. In the modern edition the passage reads "Hanc Padue in Arena optime pinsit Giottus". Other wordings, such as "quam Giottus egregie pinxit in capella de l'Arena apud Paduam", circulate in the secondary literature; the critical edition's text is the one to quote. Whatever the exact phrasing, the testimony is precious. It shows a contemporary visitor singling out one specific allegorical figure, and it places the dado among the parts of the chapel that struck viewers first. Recent scholarship has gone further and argued that Barberino's own illustrations and use of images were shaped by what he saw at the Arena.

Dante's testimony is better known and harder to pin down. In *Purgatorio* XI, among the proud, the illuminator Oderisi da Gubbio speaks on the vanity of fame: "Credette Cimabue ne la pittura / tener lo campo, e ora ha Giotto il grido, / sì che la fama di colui è scura". [Cimabue](/Content/Artists/XIII-c/Cimabue.html) thought he held the field in painting, and now Giotto has the cry, so that the other's fame is dimmed. The lines were written in the second decade of the fourteenth century. They are the most famous early assessment of Giotto, but they do not mention Padua and cannot be read as proof that Dante saw the chapel. The link between Dante and the Arena is otherwise twofold. One part is the *Inferno*'s usurer with the azure sow on his purse, discussed above, which shows that Dante knew of the Scrovegni and their reputation. The other is an anecdote recorded by Benvenuto da Imola in his commentary on the *Commedia*, compiled in the 1370s and 1380s. Commenting on the lines of *Purgatorio* XI, Benvenuto tells that while Giotto, "satis adhuc iuvenis", still quite young, was painting a chapel in Padua in the place where the theatre or arena had been, Dante came there. Giotto received him with honour and took him into his house. Seeing the painter's children, who were very ugly and resembled their father, Dante asked how one who painted such beautiful figures could make such ugly children. Giotto replied, "quia pingo de die, sed fingo de nocte", because I paint by day but make by night.

The anecdote is charming and has been retold for six centuries. It even gave the theme of a Paduan celebration of "Dante a Padova" in 1865. Its documentary value is low. Benvenuto wrote seventy years after the event. The joke is borrowed almost word for word from Macrobius's *Saturnalia*, where it is told of a Roman painter, so it belongs to a rhetorical stock of witticisms about artists. Dante's presence in Padua during his exile is plausible and widely accepted for some date in the first decade of the century, but it is not securely documented for the years of the chapel's painting. The most that can be said is that a personal acquaintance between poet and painter is possible and was believed in by the later fourteenth century. Claims of mutual influence between the *Commedia* and the Last Judgment should therefore be treated with caution. Similarities in the punishments of the damned reflect a shared visual and homiletic culture as much as any direct exchange. A Paduan visit by Dante during the painting campaign remains a pleasing hypothesis.

Later writers carried the chapel's reputation into the Renaissance. In the mid-fifteenth century Lorenzo Ghiberti<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-lorenzo-ghiberti" class="footnote"><sup>14</sup></a>'s *Commentarii* included Giotto's Paduan work in the first artist-centred history of Italian art. Giorgio Vasari<a href="/endnotes.html#fn-giorgio-vasari" class="footnote"><sup>15</sup></a>'s *Lives*, in the editions of 1550 and 1568, gave the attribution canonical form, though his account of the Paduan works is neither complete nor fully accurate. By then, however, the chapel had passed out of Scrovegni hands. Its fame survived because it was inseparable from Giotto's.

## Afterlife: Neglect, Rescue, War, Restoration and World Heritage

From the confiscation of 1439 to the early nineteenth century the chapel belonged to a series of private owners. It continued to be used for worship and for the Annunciation feast, which has been celebrated there in some form ever since. A confraternity of the Annunciation, the Scuola dell'Annunziata, grew up in the vicinity. The chapel’s survival was not assured. The loss of the portico and the palace exposed the building to structural movement and damp, and the commune’s acquisition in 1880–1881, though it secured the chapel legally, opened a period of interventions that were well meant and sometimes harmful, among them the removal of the façade’s painted plaster in 1885. Another was a series of fresco restorations in the late nineteenth century and again in the early 1960s, whose differing materials left the surfaces uneven in colour and introduced consolidants and adhesives that later analysis had to map. Atmospheric pollution in the twentieth century added a new form of decay. Sulphur compounds converted the carbonate of the plaster into gypsum, causing the paint layer to powder and flake.

The greatest threat of the twentieth century came from the war. On 11 March 1944 an Allied air raid struck the church of the Eremitani a few dozen metres from the chapel. It destroyed the façade and roof of the church and wiped out the Ovetari chapel with its frescoes by the young Andrea Mantegna, a loss often described as the gravest artistic loss Italy suffered in the war. Only a few scenes, removed earlier, survived intact, and the rest has been partially reconstructed from fragments and photographs. The Arena Chapel came through. Its survival a stone's throw from the ruin of the Eremitani, the very convent whose friars had protested against it in 1305, is one of the ironies of its history. It is also a reminder of how fragile the whole ensemble of Paduan Trecento painting is.

The modern conservation campaign was planned on an unusual scale and over an unusual length of time. The Istituto Centrale per il Restauro conducted more than twenty years of preparatory investigation, beginning in the 1980s, to diagnose the causes of decay and to make the chapel's interior environment fit for the long-term survival of the paintings. Work on the building included screening the windows, consolidating the exterior and, most visibly, building a climate-controlled access structure next to the entrance. This is the *Corpo Tecnologico Attrezzato*, later also called the *Corpo tecnico di accesso*, which came into operation on 31 May 2000. Visitors, as the Paduan press describes the procedure, "sono tenuti a sostare per una quindicina di minuti all'interno del corpo tecnologico attrezzato, per sottoporsi a un processo di deumidificazione e depurazione dalle polveri", after which the visit to the chapel itself is likewise limited, usually to about fifteen minutes. Only after a year of monitoring had confirmed that the system worked did the restoration of the paintings begin, in the summer of 2001. It was carried out by the Institute's own conservators and by several private firms under the technical direction of the Institute, with an international committee of experts and an interdisciplinary commission. The chapel was reopened on 18 March 2002, at a ceremony in the presence of the President of the Republic, and to the public a week later.

The restoration had two kinds of result. The first was conservative: securing the plaster and paint, removing deposits and earlier restoration materials, and harmonizing the colour disturbed by earlier campaigns, with the entrance wall, the most degraded zone, receiving particular attention. The second was cognitive. The campaign produced an exceptionally full documentation of Giotto's technique. It was published in a special volume of the *Bollettino d'Arte* in 2005 and in a large report on the investigations, project and results in 2003. These publications established the mixed technique of the cycle, the use of azurite rather than lapis, the revival of polished stucco for the faux marbles, the mirrors in the halo of Christ the Judge, the sinopia under the panel of God the Father, and many small details of expression, like the tears of the mothers in the *Massacre of the Innocents*, that had been lost under grime. The restoration was conceived as the start of a permanent maintenance programme, not as an end. An addendum to the agreement between the city and the ministry, signed on the day of the reopening, recognized the need for "uninterrupted activity of monitoring and maintenance".

International recognition came in 2021. At its forty-fourth session, held at Fuzhou and online, the World Heritage Committee inscribed on the World Heritage List a serial property first nominated as "'Padova Urbs picta', Giotto's Scrovegni Chapel and Padua's fourteenth-century fresco cycles" and inscribed as "Padua's fourteenth-century fresco cycles". The decision was proclaimed on 24 July 2021. The property consists of eight religious and secular complexes within the walled city, grouped in four clusters, with fresco cycles painted between 1302 and 1397 by Giotto, [Guariento](/Content/Artists/XIV-c/GuarientodiArpo.html), Giusto de' Menabuoi, Altichiero da Zevio, Jacopo Avanzi and Jacopo da Verona. The Scrovegni Chapel and the church of the Eremitani form one component together. The property was inscribed on a single criterion, criterion (ii), as illustrating the interchange of ideas among science, literature and the visual arts in pre-humanist Padua. The Committee's statement notes that the cycles show "an entirely new way of depicting allegorical narratives in spatial perspectives", influenced by advances in optics, and a new ability to capture human figures with individual features and emotions. The choice to inscribe a serial property rather than the Arena Chapel alone is historically apt. It places Giotto's chapel at the head of a century of Paduan painting that absorbed and transformed it, rather than as an isolated miracle.

## Conclusion: Reading the Chapel with Epistemic Discipline

What, then, can be said with confidence about the Arena Chapel? The land was bought on 6 February 1300 from Manfredo Dalesmanini. A licence to build was granted by Bishop Ottobono dei Razzi before his translation in 1302. The church was in use and bore the title of Santa Maria della Carità by 1 March 1304, when Benedict XI granted its indulgence. The Eremitani protested against its bells and splendour on 9 January 1305. The Venetian Great Council lent cloths of San Marco for its consecration on 16 March 1305, and the consecration took place on 25 March 1305. Giotto's authorship of the nave decoration is attested within a decade by Riccobaldo and Francesco da Barberino. Enrico Scrovegni died in Venice on 20 August 1336, having made his will on 12 March of that year. These points are documented.

A dedication in 1303 is probable but rests on a lost inscription. A fresco campaign running from about 1303 to 1305, with the Last Judgment completed around 1306, is probable and consistent with all the evidence, but it is not documented as such. Probable too are a learned theological adviser behind the programme and a significant enlargement of the patron's ambitions between 1300 and 1305, reflected in the difference between the model and the building. So is a date for the choir frescoes around 1315–1325, by a painter other than Giotto. Giotto's role as architect is possible, argued powerfully by Jacobus, but undocumented. Identifying the adviser as Alberto da Padova is conjectural. The meaning of the God the Father panel and the shape of the chancel-arch summit before it remain open. So do the original form of the façade and east end, and any personal meeting of Giotto and Dante at the Arena.

Several errors should be retired. The chapel was not built simply to expiate Rinaldo Scrovegni's usury. That reading depends on a later chronology and a simplified view of a patron whose own words speak of civic honour and suffrage, and whose painted programme presents him as a man of charity. Dante does not name Rinaldo, who is identified only by his heraldic sow. The consecration was on 25 March 1305, not 1303 or 1304. Enrico's donor portrait is not the first of its kind, though it is an extraordinarily bold appropriation, by a lay creditor, of a type once reserved for popes and princes.

Interpretation of the chapel will move forward, as it has since Longhi looked at two empty painted rooms and saw in them the birth of modern pictorial space, by holding together the documents, the building and the paint. Frugoni showed how much the iconography could reveal about a patron's self-presentation once the expiatory reflex had been set aside. Jacobus showed how much the paintings depend on the building and on the rituals once performed in and around it. The restorers showed how much remained to be discovered in the material surface. The Arena Chapel is still a working archive, and its most recent reading, the restorers' map of plaster, pigment and light, will not be its last.
